How to edit
-----------
Change the sentences in place. Send the file back and the wording will be put back on the same screen, or into the same kind of question.

Each paragraph is a single line, except where a passage is several lines on purpose. A blank line starts a new paragraph.

*these words* are italic on the page.
**these words** are bold.

The line Answer: is what the app counts as right.
Why: is the explanation shown after the answer.


Ars Logica
The second art of the trivium

**Logic is the art of reasoning well, and the science of second intentions.** It studies the relations things have only because they are known — genus, species, subject, predicate, statement, and conclusion — and trains the mind to move from what is known to what follows logically.

@path
Title: Opening the path

Logic as an Art

Logic is the art of reasoning well. This study sets out what logic is and what its parts are. A study of each of the three acts of the mind (simple apprehension, judgment, and reasoning) opens the section of exercises that perfects it.

The first act of the mind, which comes before all judging or reasoning, is simply to grasp what a thing is. Here we learn the five **predicables** (the ways a universal is said of a subject) and the ten **categories** into which all that can be said of a thing falls; then we learn to **divide** a whole into its kinds and to **define** what a thing essentially is. These are the arts by which a concept is made clear and distinct, and everything that follows depends on them.

The second act of the mind joins or separates concepts in order to affirm or deny, and what it forms is the **proposition**. We picture propositions on Venn diagrams, relate one proposition to another by **opposition**, **equipollence**, and **conversion**, and qualify them by the **modes** of necessity and possibility.

The third act of the mind reasons from things already known to things unknown, and its form is the **syllogism**. An argument is **valid** when its conclusion must be true if its premises are, whatever they are about; it is **invalid** when the premises could be true and the conclusion still false. That single test is applied here to arguments of every kind (categorical, conjunctive and disjunctive, conditional, modal, and stated in ordinary speech), and in categorical and modal arguments the conclusion follows the weaker part in quantity and quality.

When valid form is joined to true matter, the argument is sound; when its premises are also necessary, first, and causal, it becomes **science**, that is, sure knowledge of why a thing must be so. This exercise weighs an argument in form and in matter at once. It does not yet present complete demonstrations, but it prepares the mind for those later and higher proofs.

Where we cannot demonstrate, we still need not guess, because **dialectic** reasons from what is *probable* (what seems so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise) and reaches an opinion held for good reasons. Here we learn the **topics** an argument may be drawn from, what standing a premise has before we offer it, and the four ways the schools answered an objection.

Here the reasoning of the third act is carried into the art of persuasion. The **enthymeme** is the orator’s syllogism, in which a premise is left unsaid and silently supplied by the hearer. Here we learn to state the hidden assumption and examine it.

Reasoning goes wrong, and it goes wrong in ways that can be named. A **fallacy** is an argument that looks sound and is not, and *De fallaciis*, a treatise attributed to St. Thomas, insists that each has *two* causes: what makes it look good, and what makes it fail. Six come from the words and seven from outside them. We study them not to use them but to recognize them when others use them.

Logic as a Science

Each act of the mind opens with its study and is trained by its exercises; these studies and exercises carry the same arts further. They take up what the earlier studies only name: the tree of Porphyry and the kinds of property, supposition, the modal and compound propositions, the figures and their reduction, induction, demonstration, and the modes of the fallacies.

**Opening the acts.** The path unfolds by the **acts of the mind**. All four exercises of the **First Act** (the Predicables, the Categories, Division, and Definition) are open from the start; work them in any order. Only **Division and Definition** must be passed to open the **Second Act**; when those two are passed, the Second Act appears. (The Predicables and the Categories are not required to open the path, so they can be studied at leisure and deepened along the way.) When all of the Second Act is passed, the **Third Act** (the syllogism) appears; and when all of its exercises are passed, the final sections, *Ad Demonstrationem*, *Dialectica*, *Rhetorica*, and *Sophistica*, open together. In **Logic as a Science**, the studies of each act open when that act’s exercises are passed: the First Act’s with all four of its exercises, the Second Act’s with all three, and the Third Act’s with its reasoning (VIII–XVII); the Demonstration exercise and study open with *Ad Demonstrationem*, and the study of the fallacies with *Sophistica*. To *pass* a set is to show real competence: **9 of your last 10 answers at Level III or above** correct, across **two different days**. The **Progress & Review** page names the next exercise to learn and any reviews due, with a button that opens them.

**An art and a science.** Logic is both an art and a science: an art, because it gives rules for reasoning; a speculative science, because its end is to know. Medicine offers a comparison, since it is an art that aims at producing health in the patient and also a body of certain knowledge about the body.

The parts
---------

1. The Predicables
    The five ways a general term can be said of a subject, after Porphyry: genus and species, the difference that makes a species, the property that follows from a thing’s nature, and the accident that merely happens to be true of it. We name the predicable in each statement, and we learn why “genus” and “species” are relative terms, and how a property differs from an accident.
2. The Categories
    The ten highest kinds under which anything that can be said of a thing falls, after Aristotle and St. Thomas: substance, and the nine accidents (quantity, quality, relation, action, passion, when, where, posture, and having). We place each predicate in its category and learn St. Thomas’s account of how the nine follow from their different relations to substance.
3. Division
    Division leads to definition: a general kind is divided by differences until a single species is left. Each question shows one rule (the members must cover the whole, must not overlap, must each actually belong to the whole, and must be sorted on a single basis), and the four kinds (logical, integral, by powers, accidental) are taught alongside.
4. Definition
    We learn the rules of definition by using them. Each question shows one rule (exact fit, clarity, no circle, saying what a thing is rather than what it is not, and brevity) and asks which everyday definition breaks or keeps it. The four kinds (nominal, essential, descriptive, causal) are taught the same way.
5. Diagramming Propositions
    We picture single propositions on a Venn diagram, shading what they declare empty and placing an × where they assert existence. From 50 points onward, two premises are diagrammed together on three circles.
6. Opposition, Equipollence, and Conversion
    John of St. Thomas treats opposition, equipollence, and conversion as properties of the proposition, and not as a separate kind of inference. We judge what truth or falsity the square carries from one proposition to another, and we give the equipollent, the converse, or the contrapositive in standard form. Obversion is practiced with them. Later logicians call these properties “immediate inferences.” The truth values assigned here are those of the classical square; on the modern reading only the contradictory would be determined.
7. The Modal Propositions
    John of St. Thomas counts four modes: necessary, impossible, possible, and contingent, where the contingent is what can be, and can also not be. We judge equipollences (whether two expressions have the same force and the same corner of the square, as when we ask whether “not possible that not” comes to “necessary”), the modal square, and the composite and divided senses (whether the seated man may walk).
8. Diagramming Syllogisms
    Diagram a whole valid syllogism on three circles: shade the universal premises, place the ×s, and, where the conclusion needs the existence of a term, the import mark ⊗.
9. Syllogistic Form in the Abstract
    Judge whether a syllogism in schematic letters is valid, considering its form alone. Capital letters are general terms; lower-case letters are singular.
10. Syllogistic Form in the Concrete
    Judge validity for syllogisms stated in ordinary English terms, general or singular. Attend to the form, not the truth of the premises.
11. Drawing the Conclusion
    Two premises are given in standard form. Type the conclusion that follows logically, or “none” if nothing follows.
12. The Conjunctive and Disjunctive Syllogism
    There are two forms, the conjunctive (“not both”) and the disjunctive (“either … or”). The conjunctive concludes only in the mood that denies by affirming (modus ponendo tollens). The disjunctive concludes in the mood that affirms by denying (modus tollendo ponens), and in the mood that denies by affirming only when it is strict (“but not both”). So we must note which marker the premise uses.
13. The Hypothetical Syllogism
    The conditional and its two lawful moods, the mood that affirms by affirming (modus ponendo ponens) and the mood that denies by denying (modus tollendo tollens), set beside the twin fallacies of affirming the consequent and denying the antecedent.
14. The Mixed Consequence
    Conditional, conjunctive, and disjunctive premises together in one drill. Type the conclusion that follows about the subject, or “none” if the mood is fallacious.
15. Arguments in Ordinary Speech
    Ordinary English arguments of every kind (categorical, conditional, conjunctive, disjunctive, and modal), not in standard form. Discern the syllogism within and judge its validity.
16. The Modal Syllogism
    Syllogisms with premises necessary, assertoric, or contingent. In quality and quantity the conclusion follows the weaker premise; in mode the course follows Aristotle, so that in the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion.
17. The Chain of Syllogisms
    The purely formal art at its fullest extent: a chain of syllogisms (a polysyllogism), where each conclusion becomes a premise of the next. Follow the reasoning link by link and judge whether the whole chain holds, for one invalid link makes the whole chain invalid. The terms are schematic letters, and there are no diagrams.
18. The Matter and the Form
    Arguments drawn from four matters: the everyday world of living things and things made, judged by ordinary knowledge; classical mathematics after Euclid; the doctrine of virtue after Aristotle and St. Thomas; and the soul and living things after De Anima. Judge the form (is it valid?) and the matter (are the premises in fact true?), and so whether the argument proves its conclusion. Any term you may not know is explained above the argument.
19. The Enthymeme
    The syllogism with a premise left unsaid, where arguments hide their weakness. Supply the tacit premise in standard form (or “none”, when nothing can complete it), and weigh examples ancient and modern, from Aristotle, Plato, Descartes, and St. Thomas to the arguments of the street, the field, and the market, asking what is being taken for granted.
20. Dialectic
    Reasoning from what is granted rather than from what is proved. The probable, says the tradition, is what seems so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise, and dialectic reaches a reasoned opinion, not a proof. The Topics is the art of FINDING an argument, not merely of judging one: given something we must show, we learn which of the twelve places will supply it, and what maxim the argument will depend on. Then we judge what standing a premise has before we offer it, and we answer an objection as the schools did, by distinguishing the term, denying the premise, or granting the premises and denying the consequence; and when the objection is simply sound, we concede it and state the thesis again more narrowly.
21. The Fallacies
    Arguments that look sound and are not, after De fallaciis, a treatise attributed to St. Thomas, and Aristotle’s Sophistical Refutations. Six fallacies come from the words and seven from outside them; every one has two causes: what makes it look sound, and what makes it fail. We name the fallacy, its two causes, and the answer to it, and we find the false rule the argument depends on.
22. Supposition
    What a term stands for in a proposition: the word itself, the nature as known, or the individuals, and among the individuals one, one or another, each, some without any in particular, or all together. We name the supposition of a marked term, judge whether an affirmative or a negative is true when its subject stands for nothing, and find the argument that fails because a term changes what it stands for.
23. Compound and Exponible Propositions
    Propositions made of propositions, joined by “and,” “or,” “not both,” “if,” and “because,” and the propositions that look simple but contain “only,” “except,” or “as.” We name the kind, say when it is true, give its contradictory, and resolve the exponibles into the propositions they contain.
24. Figures, Moods, and Reduction
    Syllogisms in schematic letters. We name the figure and the mood (the letters first, the Latin name after), the mood of the first figure to which an imperfect mood is reduced, and the operations its name prescribes: simple conversion, conversion by limitation, transposition, or reduction to the impossible.
25. Compound Arguments
    The epicheirema, the polysyllogism, the Aristotelian and the Goclenian sorites, the dilemma, and the disjunctive syllogism. We name the kind of argument, judge whether a dilemma holds and how it may be answered, and test whether a sorites keeps its rules.
26. Demonstration
    Arguments judged as science. We tell a demonstration of the reasoned fact, through the proper cause, from a demonstration of the fact, through an effect or a remote cause, and from an argument that is only probable; we say in which way a predicate belongs to its subject; and we find why a proof falls short of demonstration.

============================================================

########################################################################
# What Logic Is
########################################################################

@deck logic
Title: What Logic Is
Small heading: INTRODUCTION

What logic is and what it covers; logic as an art and a science; logic and thought; Aristotle’s *Organon*; the parts of logic (demonstration, dialectic, rhetoric, poetics, and the study of fallacies); form and matter; a short history of logic; and the order of this course.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Timothy Kearns, “Outline and Synopsis of Accounts of Logic in the Western Tradition”; St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, proem, and *Summa theologiae* II-II, q. 2, a. 1, and I-II, q. 90, a. 1, ad 2; John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, the *Organon*; Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**What logic is.** Man is the rational animal. Reason is his defining faculty, and every human act, so far as it is well directed, is directed by reason. Reason can be exercised with more or less skill, and the art that governs its exercise is logic; so the tradition defines logic simply as *the art of reasoning well*. St. Thomas says the same thing more fully in the proem to his commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*: logic is the art that directs the very act of reason, so that a man may proceed in that act in order, with ease, and without error.

Logic is therefore not merely the study of precise arguments, or of statements and inferences written in symbols, as is sometimes thought. It directs all the ways we use reason to arrive at truth: proof and demonstration, probable argument, rhetoric, the proposing of plausible explanations, and the guarding against errors in reasoning. It also includes how we make definitions, how we classify things, and how we come to know things from experience and by comparison with other things; and it governs the decisions we make about how to act in accord with the truth.

@question 1.1
Question: What is logic?
Choices:
- The art of reasoning well
- The study of language and its correct use
- The science of number and quantity
- The rules of debate in a law court
Answer: The art of reasoning well
Why: Logic is the art of reasoning well. Because reason is man’s defining faculty, the art that directs reason bears on every human act that is well directed.
Why not the others: The correct use of language belongs to grammar, number and quantity to mathematics, and debate to dialectic and rhetoric, which are only parts of logic.

@question 1.2
Question: True or false: logic is only the study of formal arguments written in symbols.
Choices:
- True
- False
Answer: False
Why: Logic directs every use of reason to reach truth: demonstration, probable argument, rhetoric, the proposing of explanations, and the guarding against error. It also governs how we define and classify.
Why not the others: Symbolic and formal argument is one part of logic, but the art also directs probable argument, persuasion, explanation, definition, and classification.

@question 1.3
Question: According to St. Thomas, what does logic direct?
Choices:
- The very act of reason, so that we proceed in order, with ease, and without error
- The movements of the body, so that we act with grace, ease, and skill in every craft
- The feelings, so that we are moved rightly
- The use of numbers, so that we calculate correctly
Answer: The very act of reason, so that we proceed in order, with ease, and without error
Why: In the proem to his commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, St. Thomas calls logic the art that directs the act of reason itself.
Why not the others: The body, the feelings, and calculation have their own arts or virtues; logic is the art whose proper matter is the act of reason.

@card 2

**An art and a science.** Logic is both an art and a science: an art, because it gives rules for reasoning; a speculative science, because its end is to know. Medicine offers a comparison, since it is an art that aims at producing health in the patient and also a body of certain knowledge about the body.

As a science, logic has its own object, which is second intentions, that is, the relations that things have only as they are known. When we first come to know what white is, we form a first intention; when the mind turns back to what it knows and sees that white is a species of color, it forms a second intention. Genus, species, subject, predicate, proposition, and conclusion are relations of this second kind, which the tradition calls beings of reason (*entia rationis*). Because such relations are found wherever anything is known, the art of logic can be used in every inquiry.

@question 2.1
Question: Is logic an art or a science?
Choices:
- Both: an art, because it gives rules for reasoning, and a speculative science, because its end is to know
- An art only, since it is a skill for reasoning well
- A science only, since it is a body of knowledge
- Neither, since logic is a language
Answer: Both: an art, because it gives rules for reasoning, and a speculative science, because its end is to know
Why: Logic gives rules for reasoning, so it is an art; and its end is also to know, so it is a speculative science.
Why not the others: The second and third answers each leave out half of what logic is. Logic is carried out in language, but it is not itself a language.

@question 2.2
Question: What is the object of logic as a science?
Choices:
- Second intentions, the relations things have only as they are known
- Material bodies and their motions, as they are known through the senses alone
- Quantity, both continuous and discrete
- Being as being
Answer: Second intentions, the relations things have only as they are known
Why: Logic studies relations such as genus, species, subject, and predicate, which belong to things only as they are known. These are second intentions.
Why not the others: Bodies in motion belong to natural philosophy, quantity to mathematics, and being as being to metaphysics.

@question 2.3
Question: Which of these is a second intention?
Choices:
- “Species,” as when we say that man is a species
- “White,” as when we say that snow is white
- “Heavy,” as when we say that lead is heavy
- “Running,” as when we say that a horse is running
Answer: “Species,” as when we say that man is a species
Why: We first know what man is, which is a first intention. When the mind considers man as known and relates it to animal, it sees that man is a species. Being a species belongs to man only as man is known.
Why not the others: White, heavy, and running belong to things as they exist, and so they are known by first intentions.

@card 3

**Logic and thought.** Rationality is a distinctively human capacity, and logic is the art that governs its use. What, then, is thought? The goal of rationality is the discovery of truth and the guidance of human action by the truths we have discovered. While we are still seeking, and have not yet arrived at knowledge, the acts of the mind as it deliberates are thoughts. Since thought exists for the sake of discovering truth, it is not the fundamental human capacity, and logic is not properly called the art of thinking.

St. Thomas puts the point this way: “Thinking, properly speaking, is the movement of the mind as it deliberates, while it has not yet arrived at the full vision of the truth in question” (*Summa theologiae* II-II, q. 2, a. 1). Logic therefore governs the whole process of human reasoning, from principles to conclusions, for the sake of knowing.

@question 3.1
Question: What is the goal of rationality?
Choices:
- The discovery of truth, and the guidance of action by the truths discovered
- Thinking for its own sake, which is the highest activity of the human mind
- Winning arguments
- Expressing our feelings
Answer: The discovery of truth, and the guidance of action by the truths discovered
Why: Reason aims at knowing the truth and at acting according to it, and thought serves that end.
Why not the others: Thinking is a means and not the end; an argument can be won with a falsehood; and expressing feelings is not the end of reason.

@question 3.2
Question: According to St. Thomas, what is thinking, properly speaking?
Choices:
- The movement of the mind as it deliberates, before it has arrived at the full vision of the truth
- The possession of a truth once it has been seen
- The same thing as sensation
- The act of the will in choosing a good
Answer: The movement of the mind as it deliberates, before it has arrived at the full vision of the truth
Why: St. Thomas defines thinking in this way in *Summa theologiae* II-II, q. 2, a. 1.
Why not the others: Possessing the truth is knowledge, which thinking aims at; sensation is not thought; and choosing is an act of the will.

@question 3.3
Question: Why does the tradition not call logic “the art of thinking”?
Choices:
- Because thought exists for the sake of discovering truth, so the art of reasoning is ordered to knowing
- Because logic has nothing to do with thought
- Because thinking needs no art at all
- Because only the senses can reach truth
Answer: Because thought exists for the sake of discovering truth, so the art of reasoning is ordered to knowing
Why: Thought is subordinate to the discovery of truth. Logic does govern thought, but it governs it as the art of reasoning toward knowledge.
Why not the others: Logic does govern thought; thought does need an art; and it is the intellect, not the senses alone, that knows truth.

@card 4

**The instrument.** By tradition, Aristotle’s logical writings are called the *Organon*, which means “instrument,” because logic is studied chiefly as a tool for coming to know. The books are these: the *Categories*, on terms and what can be said of things; the *De interpretatione*, on propositions and their relations to one another; the *Prior Analytics*, on the form of reasoning; the *Posterior Analytics*, on demonstration and the matter of reasoning; the *Topics*, on probable argument; and the *Sophistical Refutations*, on errors and tricks in reasoning.

Avicenna, a Persian philosopher of the early eleventh century, and St. Thomas also count the *Rhetoric* (on persuasion) and the *Poetics* (on storytelling and literature) among Aristotle’s logical works, since each directs an act of reason that falls short of certainty.

@question 4.1
Question: What does the name *Organon* mean?
Choices:
- Instrument
- Law
- Book of wisdom
- Science of being
Answer: Instrument
Why: The Greek *organon* means “instrument.” The name says that logic is a tool for coming to know.
Why not the others: The name does not mean a law, a book of wisdom, or the science of being; it names a tool.

@question 4.2
Question: Which book of the *Organon* treats probable argument?
Choices:
- The *Topics*
- The *Categories*
- The *Prior Analytics*
- The *De interpretatione*
Answer: The *Topics*
Why: The *Topics* treats dialectic, the art of arguing from what is probable.
Why not the others: The *Categories* treats terms, the *Prior Analytics* the form of reasoning, and the *De interpretatione* propositions.

@question 4.3
Question: Which two works did Avicenna and St. Thomas count among Aristotle’s logical works, beside the *Organon*?
Choices:
- The *Rhetoric* and the *Poetics*
- The *Physics* and the *Metaphysics*
- The *Ethics* and the *Politics*
- The *De anima* and the *History of Animals*
Answer: The *Rhetoric* and the *Poetics*
Why: Both count the *Rhetoric* and the *Poetics* as parts of logic, since each directs an act of reason that falls short of certainty.
Why not the others: The other works belong to natural philosophy, first philosophy, and moral philosophy.

@card 5

**The parts of logic: demonstration.** Logic has parts, and they are distinguished by the evidence available to us. The highest part is **demonstration**, which produces science (Latin *scientia*), that is, certain knowledge of what things are and of why they are as they are. The Greek word for demonstration means “showing,” and the proofs in Euclid’s *Elements* are the standard examples of it.

A demonstration of the fact (*quia*) shows *that* something is so, often by reasoning from an effect or a sign; a demonstration of the cause (*propter quid*) shows *why* it is so, through its proper cause. Because we think we know a thing best when we know both what it is and why it is as it is, demonstration of the cause gives the highest kind of knowledge. Aristotle treats demonstration in the *Prior* and *Posterior Analytics*.

@question 5.1
Question: What does demonstration produce?
Choices:
- Science, that is, certain knowledge of what a thing is and why it is so
- Probable opinion
- The persuasion of a hearer
- A plausible story
Answer: Science, that is, certain knowledge of what a thing is and why it is so
Why: Demonstration is the highest part of logic, because it produces certain knowledge through causes.
Why not the others: Probable opinion belongs to dialectic, persuasion to rhetoric, and the plausible story to poetics.

@question 5.2
Question: What does a demonstration of the fact (*quia*) show?
Choices:
- *That* something is so, often by reasoning from an effect or a sign
- *Why* something is so, through its proper and immediate cause and its definition
- What is probably so
- What would persuade an audience
Answer: *That* something is so, often by reasoning from an effect or a sign
Why: Demonstration of the fact gives certain knowledge that something is the case, even before we know the cause that explains it.
Why not the others: Knowing why belongs to demonstration of the cause; the probable belongs to dialectic; and persuasion belongs to rhetoric.

@question 5.3
Question: Why does demonstration of the cause (*propter quid*) give the highest kind of knowledge?
Choices:
- Because we know a thing best when we know both what it is and why it is as it is
- Because it is the easiest kind of knowledge to obtain, even for those just beginning
- Because it persuades the largest audience
- Because it needs no premises
Answer: Because we know a thing best when we know both what it is and why it is as it is
Why: Knowing the cause is knowing the reason why, and that is the most complete knowledge we can have of a thing.
Why not the others: It is the hardest kind of knowledge to obtain; it does not aim at persuasion; and like every demonstration it proceeds from premises.

@card 6

**Dialectic and rhetoric.** We cannot always find a demonstration. When the evidence makes one view probable but does not prove it, we consider as carefully as we can what we already know and the opinions of those who know the matter, and we discuss both sides of the question to see which is more probably true. This part of logic is **dialectic**, which Aristotle treats in the *Topics*. For example, a doctor who cannot know for certain which disease a patient has may still judge that one disease is the most likely cause of the sickness.

Sometimes the evidence favors one view about as much as the other. Then we look for whatever gives us a reason to prefer one view, and we persuade others by examples of similar things, by the character of the speaker, by the circumstances, and, where it is fitting, by appeal to the feelings of the hearers. This part of logic is **rhetoric**. Aristotle’s *Rhetoric* concerns chiefly speeches in law courts and assemblies, but the art is used far more widely: in teaching, among friends and family, and in business.

@question 6.1
Question: What does dialectic concern?
Choices:
- What is probable, that is, supported but not yet proved
- What is certain and demonstrated from necessary first principles
- What is known to be false
- What is merely imagined
Answer: What is probable, that is, supported but not yet proved
Why: Dialectic is the use of reason to discover what is probably true where we have not yet found demonstrative proof.
Why not the others: Certainty belongs to demonstration; what is known to be false has already been refuted; and dialectic works from evidence, not from imagination.

@question 6.2
Question: When does rhetoric come into use?
Choices:
- When the evidence favors one view about as much as the other
- When we have already found a demonstration of the proper cause
- Only in law courts and assemblies
- Only when we wish to deceive
Answer: When the evidence favors one view about as much as the other
Why: Rhetoric seeks a reason to prefer one view when direct evidence does not settle the question. Aristotle began with public speaking, but the art is used in teaching, among friends and family, and in business.
Why not the others: Where there is a demonstration, persuasion is not needed; rhetoric is not confined to public speaking; and its proper use is to persuade others of what is true.

@question 6.3
Question: A doctor cannot tell for certain which disease a patient has, but judges that one disease is the most likely cause. Which part of logic is the doctor using?
Choices:
- Dialectic
- Demonstration
- Poetics
- The study of fallacies
Answer: Dialectic
Why: The doctor weighs what is known and the opinions of experts and reaches what is most likely, though it is not proved. That is dialectic.
Why not the others: Demonstration would require certainty through the cause; poetics proposes possible accounts where there is little evidence; and the study of fallacies concerns errors.

@card 7

**Poetics and the fallacies.** Below even probable evidence, the mind can still be inclined by a likeness: a story or an image makes a thing seem attractive or repugnant, as food is made repugnant when it is pictured as something disgusting. This part of logic is **poetics**, whose end, St. Thomas says, is to lead to virtue by a fitting representation. We also use stories to propose possible accounts of something we barely understand and to see which account fits best, as a doctor who finds a patient unconscious considers how he might have come to be so.

Finally, reasoning can fail. Besides accepting something false, we may err in the form of our reasoning, as in “if it rained, the ground is wet; the ground is wet; so it rained.” The study of the typical errors in reasoning, the **fallacies**, is the subject of Aristotle’s *Sophistical Refutations*. In the proem to his commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, St. Thomas orders all these parts by the certainty they reach: demonstration gives science; dialectic gives belief or opinion; rhetoric gives only a suspicion; poetics inclines the mind by a representation; and the study of fallacies concerns reasoning that falls short of the truth.

@question 7.1
Question: On St. Thomas’s account, how does poetics move the mind?
Choices:
- By a likeness, a story or image that makes a thing seem attractive or repugnant
- By proof from necessary causes
- By weighing the opinions of the wise
- By counting the votes on each side
Answer: By a likeness, a story or image that makes a thing seem attractive or repugnant
Why: Below even probable evidence, a fitting representation can still incline the mind, as a picture of something disgusting makes food repugnant. St. Thomas says that the end of poetics is to lead to virtue by such a representation.
Why not the others: Proof belongs to demonstration and weighing opinions to dialectic; counting votes belongs to no part of logic.

@question 7.2
Question: Why do we study the fallacies?
Choices:
- To recognize and avoid them, and to answer those who use them
- To use them to win arguments
- Because they are a kind of demonstration that reaches certain conclusions
- Because poetry requires them
Answer: To recognize and avoid them, and to answer those who use them
Why: Aristotle studies typical errors so that we may avoid them, and so that we may refute the sophists, who used them to confuse and manipulate others.
Why not the others: The art studies errors in order to guard against them; a fallacy is the opposite of a demonstration; and poetry has no need of bad reasoning.

@question 7.3
Question: “If it rained, the ground is wet; the ground is wet; so it rained.” What kind of error is this?
Choices:
- An error in the form of the reasoning
- A false premise
- A word used in two senses within one argument
- No error at all
Answer: An error in the form of the reasoning
Why: Both premises may be true and the conclusion false, since the ground could be wet for another reason. The fault is in the form, because the argument affirms the consequent.
Why not the others: Neither premise need be false, and no word is used in two senses; the conclusion simply does not follow.

@card 8

**Form and matter.** Formal logic and material logic are the two parts of the logic that judges arguments, by their form (the *Prior Analytics*) and by their matter (the *Posterior Analytics*). **Formal logic** considers the **form** of an argument (how its terms are arranged), and **material logic** considers its **matter** (whether its premises are true, and of what kind). Formal logic studies the arrangement of the terms and asks whether, from premises taken as given, the conclusion can be deduced. Such reasoning is **valid** when the truth of the premises necessitates the truth of the conclusion, that is, when it is impossible for the premises to be true and the conclusion false. For example, “all B are A, and all C are B; therefore all C are A” is valid because of the way its terms are related, whatever B, A, and C stand for.

Material logic studies how demonstration works, how different kinds of premises (necessary, probable, or merely possible) relate to the truths we can reach through them, and how each discipline reasons according to its own subject matter. In family counseling, for example, no one reasons about a family with 1.9 children, but in demography such a family can be reasoned about as a statistical average. In the proem to his commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, St. Thomas assigns the judgment by form to the *Prior Analytics* and the judgment by matter, which demonstration requires, to the *Posterior Analytics*.

@question 8.1
Question: Which of these says the same thing as “the argument is valid”?
Choices:
- It is impossible for the premises to be true and the conclusion false
- The premises and the conclusion are all in fact true of the things themselves
- The conclusion is true, so the premises must be true
- The argument sounds convincing when spoken aloud
Answer: It is impossible for the premises to be true and the conclusion false
Why: To say that the conclusion must be true if the premises are is to say that it is impossible for the premises to be true and the conclusion false. Whether the premises are in fact true is a question about the matter of the argument.
Why not the others: True premises do not make an argument valid, a true conclusion says nothing about the premises, and sounding convincing is no test at all.

@question 8.2
Question: What does formal logic chiefly consider?
Choices:
- The arrangement of the terms, whatever they stand for
- Whether the premises are in fact true
- How a particular science reasons about its own subject matter
- How a speaker moves an audience
Answer: The arrangement of the terms, whatever they stand for
Why: Formal logic studies the arrangement of terms in reasoning, apart from what the terms stand for. That is why a valid form is valid in any matter.
Why not the others: The truth of premises and the reasoning proper to each science belong to material logic, and moving an audience belongs to rhetoric.

@question 8.3
Question: To which study does demonstration belong?
Choices:
- To the study of the matter of reasoning, since its premises must be true, necessary, and causal
- To the study of form alone
- To rhetoric
- To grammar
Answer: To the study of the matter of reasoning, since its premises must be true, necessary, and causal
Why: A demonstration needs more than a valid form. So St. Thomas assigns demonstration to the *Posterior Analytics*, which studies the matter of reasoning, while the *Prior Analytics* studies its form.
Why not the others: Formal logic judges only the arrangement of terms; rhetoric persuades; and grammar concerns correct speech.

@card 9

**The parts as stages of an inquiry.** Each part of logic is used in every branch of knowledge, and the parts also correspond broadly to the stages of an inquiry that begins without much knowledge of its subject. At first we propose different possible accounts to make sense of what little we know (poetics). As we learn more, we find reasons to prefer one account over the others (rhetoric). Through further study we may reach a probable explanation that makes sense of much of the evidence, though it does not prove what the truth is (dialectic). Finally, we may discover a demonstration of what the thing is or of what the answer must be. Not every inquiry proceeds in this way, but many do.

@question 9.1
Question: As an inquiry matures, in what order are the parts of logic often used, from least certain to most certain?
Choices:
- Poetics, rhetoric, dialectic, demonstration
- Demonstration, dialectic, rhetoric, poetics
- Rhetoric, poetics, demonstration, dialectic
- Dialectic, demonstration, poetics, rhetoric
Answer: Poetics, rhetoric, dialectic, demonstration
Why: We propose possible accounts (poetics), find reasons to prefer one (rhetoric), reach what is probable (dialectic), and at last demonstrate.
Why not the others: Certainty comes last, not first; to reverse the order would be to demonstrate before there is anything to demonstrate.

@question 9.2
Question: Which part of logic yields the most certainty?
Choices:
- Demonstration
- Dialectic
- Rhetoric
- Poetics
Answer: Demonstration
Why: Demonstration proves through the cause, and its conclusion cannot be otherwise.
Why not the others: Dialectic reaches the probable, rhetoric only inclines, and poetics proposes what is possible; each falls short of proof.

@question 9.3
Question: Do the parts of logic compete with one another?
Choices:
- No, since they often mark the stages by which one inquiry matures
- Yes, since only demonstration is worth having
- Yes, since each belongs to a different subject
- No, since they are four different names for one and the same thing
Answer: No, since they often mark the stages by which one inquiry matures
Why: The parts are used in every branch of knowledge, and within one inquiry they often follow one another, from possible accounts to demonstration.
Why not the others: Most inquiries never reach demonstration and are not therefore worthless; the parts apply within one and the same subject; and they are plainly distinct.

@card 10

**The history of logic.** Aristotle and St. Thomas usually begin the study of a subject with an account of how it has been studied, because the history of a discipline brings its terms, distinctions, and principles to light and shows how they were discovered. Before philosophy, people commonly thought of the things around them as persons, and they told the order of the world as a story. The Greek philosophers were not the first to inquire into causes, but they were the first to propose explanations based on the natures or essences of things, and that search made the discovery of demonstration possible.

Aristotle drew on the sophists for the construction of arguments, for methods of disputation, and for the discovery of fallacies, and on Plato for the theory of classification and definition, for the root notion of the syllogism, and for a conception of proof. The Stoics then developed a logic of propositions, which studies what follows from given propositions, as in “if A, then B; A; therefore B.” Aristotle’s logic was recovered in the West in the eleventh and twelfth centuries, and the medieval universities, from about 1150 to 1500, brought it to great refinement. It remained the logic of the schools until the early twentieth century, when modern mathematical logic largely replaced it. From the classical point of view, mathematical logic is the formal and material logic of mathematics, not logic in general; it applies widely because every discipline has quantitative aspects.

@question 10.1
Question: Why do Aristotle and St. Thomas usually begin an inquiry with its history?
Choices:
- To bring its terms and principles to light, and to show how they were discovered
- Out of courtesy to earlier writers
- Because the past is always right and must be followed
- To make the book longer
Answer: To bring its terms and principles to light, and to show how they were discovered
Why: Seeing how a distinction was arrived at is a large part of understanding it, so the history of a discipline belongs to the discipline itself.
Why not the others: Courtesy and length are no reasons, and both writers often disagree with their predecessors.

@question 10.2
Question: What did the Stoics develop?
Choices:
- A logic of propositions, which studies what follows from given propositions
- The first full account of scientific demonstration through causes and definitions
- The ten categories
- Modern mathematical logic
Answer: A logic of propositions, which studies what follows from given propositions
Why: The Stoics studied argument and inference in general, for example “if A, then B; A; therefore B.” Aristotle’s logic, by contrast, is a logic of terms aimed chiefly at demonstration.
Why not the others: Demonstration and the categories are Aristotle’s, and mathematical logic is modern.

@question 10.3
Question: From the classical point of view, what is modern mathematical logic?
Choices:
- The formal and material logic of mathematics
- The whole of logic, which shows Aristotle’s logic to be mistaken
- A part of rhetoric
- The same thing as grammar
Answer: The formal and material logic of mathematics
Why: Modern mathematical logic was designed for modern pure and applied mathematics. It applies widely because every discipline has quantitative aspects, but it is the logic of mathematics, not logic in general.
Why not the others: On the classical view it is one logic among the logics of the disciplines; it is not persuasion; and it is not grammar.

@card 11

**How this course is ordered.** This course follows the order that the teaching of logic kept through the Middle Ages: it begins with terms and concepts, then treats propositions, then reasoning, and ends with the fallacies. The three acts of the mind are *simple apprehension*, by which we grasp what a thing is; *judgment*, by which we affirm or deny one thing of another; and *reasoning*, by which we move from what we know to what we do not yet know. St. Thomas names the product of each: the definition, the proposition, and the syllogism or argument (*Summa theologiae* I-II, q. 90, a. 1, ad 2).

The First, Second, and Third Acts of the Mind are the first three parts of the exercises, and a study deck introduces each. Then *Ad Demonstrationem* weighs an argument in both its form and its matter, as demonstration requires; *Dialectica* treats reasoning from the probable; *Rhetorica* treats the enthymeme, the orator’s argument; and *Sophistica* treats the fallacies.

@question 11.1
Question: Grasping what a thing is belongs to which act of the mind?
Choices:
- The first (simple apprehension)
- The second (judgment), which composes and divides
- The third (reasoning)
- None of these
Answer: The first (simple apprehension)
Why: Simple apprehension is the grasp of what a thing is, before anything is affirmed or denied of it.
Why not the others: Judgment affirms or denies, and reasoning moves from one proposition to another.

@question 11.2
Question: “No stone is alive.” Which act of the mind forms this?
Choices:
- The second (judgment)
- The first (simple apprehension)
- The third (reasoning)
- None of these
Answer: The second (judgment)
Why: Two concepts have been separated and the separation is asserted. That is judgment, and it can be true or false.
Why not the others: The first act only grasps “stone” and “alive”; the third would move from this to something further.

@question 11.3
Question: According to St. Thomas, what does the third act of the mind produce?
Choices:
- The syllogism, or argument
- The definition
- The proposition
- The category
Answer: The syllogism, or argument
Why: St. Thomas names the definition, the proposition, and the syllogism or argument as the products of the three acts in order.
Why not the others: The definition belongs to the first act and the proposition to the second; the categories are kinds of things that can be said, not a product of reasoning.


########################################################################
# The First Act: Simple Apprehension
########################################################################

@deck act1
Title: The First Act: Simple Apprehension
Small heading: THE FIRST ACT

The grasp of what a thing is: signs, natural, conventional, and formal; how words signify concepts and concepts represent things; the concept and the term; abstraction; comprehension and extension; and what the exercises of the First Act take up.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Timothy Kearns, “Outline and Synopsis of Accounts of Logic in the Western Tradition”; St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, proem, and commentary on the *De interpretatione*, bk. I, lect. 2; John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, *Categories*; Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**What the first act is.** The first act of the mind is *simple apprehension*: the grasp of what a thing is, without affirming or denying anything about it. When we think “man” or “snow,” we have only the beginning of a truth, because nothing is yet asserted, and so nothing is yet true or false. St. Thomas assigns the study of this act to Aristotle’s *Categories*, because by it the mind conceives what a thing is.

The tradition takes the word “thing” (Latin *res*) very broadly. Informally, a thing is anything we can speak about, anything that could answer the question “What are you talking about?”: a color, an attitude, a thought, an animal, a species of animal, a rock, a cloud, or an association of lawyers.

@question 1.1
Question: What does simple apprehension do?
Choices:
- It grasps what a thing is, without affirming or denying anything about it
- It affirms one thing of another
- It draws a conclusion from premises
- It persuades a hearer
Answer: It grasps what a thing is, without affirming or denying anything about it
Why: Simple apprehension is the first act of the mind, the bare grasp of what a thing is.
Why not the others: Affirming belongs to judgment, concluding to reasoning, and persuading to rhetoric.

@question 1.2
Question: True or false: the act of thinking “snow,” by itself, is either true or false.
Choices:
- True
- False
Answer: False
Why: Simple apprehension asserts nothing, so it is not yet true or false. Truth and falsity first appear when we affirm or deny, as in “snow is white.”
Why not the others: A single concept asserts nothing, so there is nothing yet to be true or false.

@question 1.3
Question: Which of these can be a “thing” in the logician’s sense?
Choices:
- A color, a thought, and an association of lawyers alike
- Only a material body
- Only a living being
- Only what we can touch, see, or otherwise perceive by the senses
Answer: A color, a thought, and an association of lawyers alike
Why: Informally, a thing is anything we can speak about, anything that could answer the question “What are you talking about?”
Why not the others: The word is not limited to bodies, to living things, or to what can be touched.

@card 2

**Signs.** Words are signs of things, but what is a sign? John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, gives the definition: “What represents to a cognoscitive faculty something other than itself” (*Summulae*, bk. I, ch. 2).

Some signs are **natural**: smoke is a sign of fire, and tracks in the sand are a sign that an animal has passed, because the sign is caused by what it signifies. Other signs are **conventional**, having their meaning by the agreement of a community: the sounds of our words, or an arrow painted on a post to tell drivers that the road bends. Both kinds are **instrumental** signs, because the sign itself must be perceived first, and only then does it lead us to what it signifies.

@question 2.1
Question: What is a sign?
Choices:
- That which represents something other than itself to a cognitive power
- Any material thing
- A word written on paper
- A picture of something
Answer: That which represents something other than itself to a cognitive power
Why: This is John of St. Thomas’s definition, and it covers every kind of sign.
Why not the others: Not every material thing represents another; written words and pictures are only some kinds of sign.

@question 2.2
Question: Smoke is a sign of fire. What kind of sign is it?
Choices:
- A natural sign, since it is caused by what it signifies
- A conventional sign, since people agreed on it
- A formal sign, since it represents without first being known
- No sign at all
Answer: A natural sign, since it is caused by what it signifies
Why: Smoke signifies fire because fire causes it, and no agreement is needed. It is also an instrumental sign, since we must see the smoke before it leads us to the fire.
Why not the others: No one agreed that smoke should mean fire; and smoke must itself be seen first, so it is not a formal sign.

@question 2.3
Question: An arrow painted on a post tells drivers that the road bends. What kind of sign is it?
Choices:
- A conventional sign
- A natural sign, like smoke
- A formal sign
- No sign at all
Answer: A conventional sign
Why: The arrow has its meaning by the agreement of a community, so it is conventional; and it must be seen first, so it is instrumental.
Why not the others: The arrow is not caused by the bend in the road, so it is not natural; and it is known before it leads us further, so it is not formal.

@card 3

**The concept, a formal sign.** There is a third kind of sign. When something is brought to our attention, we form a concept of it, and a concept is a representation. But a concept does not represent by means of something else, as a spoken word uses sound or a photograph uses ink on paper; it represents through itself, without a medium. So the concept is a **formal sign**, while smoke and spoken words are instrumental signs, which must themselves be known first.

This is why the classical tradition holds that concepts of the same thing are the same for everyone. Words differ from language to language (“cat” in English, “billee” in Hindi), but two concepts of the same thing, so far as they represent it, are the same, because what distinguishes one concept from another is the thing that each represents.

@question 3.1
Question: Why is a concept called a formal sign?
Choices:
- Because it represents through itself, without a medium
- Because it is always expressed in formal and technical language
- Because it was agreed on by convention
- Because it is caused by a word
Answer: Because it represents through itself, without a medium
Why: A concept does not first have to be known as a thing in its own right; it simply makes its object present to the mind.
Why not the others: A concept is not a piece of language, it is not conventional, and words express concepts, not the reverse.

@question 3.2
Question: “Cat” in English and “billee” in Hindi both refer to cats. What do they share?
Choices:
- The same concept, though the sounds differ
- Nothing, since each language has its own concepts
- The same sound, written differently
- The same grammar
Answer: The same concept, though the sounds differ
Why: Words differ from language to language, but concepts of the same thing are the same for everyone.
Why not the others: The sounds and the grammars differ, but the concept they express is one.

@question 3.3
Question: Which of these is an instrumental sign?
Choices:
- A spoken word
- A concept
- Both a spoken word and a concept
- Neither a spoken word nor a concept
Answer: A spoken word
Why: A spoken word must first be heard before it leads the mind to what it signifies. A concept represents through itself, so it is a formal sign.
Why not the others: The concept is the formal sign; only the word is instrumental.

@card 4

**Words, concepts, and things.** What is the order among words, concepts, and things? According to St. Thomas, in his commentary on Aristotle’s *De interpretatione* (bk. I, lect. 2), we form concepts to represent things; words signify concepts immediately, and they signify things only by means of the concepts. Words do not signify things immediately, because what exists outside the mind is singular, while a word such as “man” signifies the nature that the mind has grasped.

So logic is concerned with words, but not with their correct use, which is the business of grammar. It is concerned with words in so far as reasoning is carried out by means of words that express the acts of the mind.

@question 4.1
Question: According to St. Thomas, what does a word signify immediately?
Choices:
- The concept
- The thing outside the mind
- Another word
- The sound itself
Answer: The concept
Why: Words signify concepts immediately, and things only by means of the concepts.
Why not the others: The word reaches the thing only through the concept; and it signifies something other than itself.

@question 4.2
Question: Why do words not signify things immediately?
Choices:
- Because what exists outside the mind is singular, while a word such as “man” signifies a nature grasped by the mind
- Because words have no meaning
- Because things cannot be known
- Because concepts are made of words
Answer: Because what exists outside the mind is singular, while a word such as “man” signifies a nature grasped by the mind
Why: A common word signifies a nature that exists as universal only in the mind’s concept, so the word reaches the thing through the concept.
Why not the others: Words do have meaning, things can be known, and concepts are not made of words.

@question 4.3
Question: Is logic concerned with the correct use of words?
Choices:
- No; that belongs to grammar, and logic considers words as they express the acts of the mind in reasoning
- Yes; logic is the art of correct speech
- No; logic has nothing to do with words
- Yes; logic and grammar are the same art
Answer: No; that belongs to grammar, and logic considers words as they express the acts of the mind in reasoning
Why: Reasoning is carried out in words, so logic considers words, but only in so far as they express the acts of the mind.
Why not the others: Correct speech is grammar’s work; logic does concern words; and logic and grammar are different arts.

@card 5

**The concept and the term.** The concept is the end of the act of understanding a thing. A concept is called a **mental term** (from the Latin *terminus*, an end or boundary) because it can be one of the two ends of a simple proposition: in “A is B,” A and B are the terms, and the copula “is” joins them. The spoken term is the word that expresses the mental term, that is, a sound that signifies a concept by convention.

The tradition therefore distinguishes each act of the mind from its expression in words. The mental term is expressed by the spoken term, the judgment by the proposition, and the inference by the syllogism.

@question 5.1
Question: Why is a concept called a “term”?
Choices:
- Because it can be one of the ends of a proposition, its subject or its predicate
- Because it lasts for a fixed term of time
- Because it is a technical word
- Because it ends a syllogism
Answer: Because it can be one of the ends of a proposition, its subject or its predicate
Why: The Latin *terminus* means an end or boundary. In “A is B,” the terms A and B are the two ends that the copula joins.
Why not the others: The name has nothing to do with time or with technical vocabulary, and the syllogism ends in a proposition, not a term.

@question 5.2
Question: In “Snow is white,” which words express the terms?
Choices:
- “Snow” and “white”
- “Is” alone
- “Snow is,” the subject with its copula
- “White” alone
Answer: “Snow” and “white”
Why: The subject “snow” and the predicate “white” are the terms. The copula “is” joins them; it is not a term itself.
Why not the others: The copula is not a term, and a proposition always has two terms, not one.

@question 5.3
Question: Which pair matches an act of the mind with its expression in words?
Choices:
- The mental term and the spoken term
- The judgment of the second act and the syllogism
- The inference and the spoken term
- The mental term and the proposition
Answer: The mental term and the spoken term
Why: The mental term is expressed by the spoken term, the judgment by a proposition, and the inference by a syllogism.
Why not the others: The other pairs mix the acts: the syllogism expresses an inference, and the proposition expresses a judgment.

@card 6

**Abstraction.** How do we come to have concepts? We begin from the experience of the senses. From our experience of men, for example, the mind draws out what it is to be a man, leaving aside what belongs only to this or that man, such as his weight or the place where he stands. This drawing out is **abstraction**, and its result is a universal concept, one that can be said of many: “man” can be said of Socrates, of Plato, and of every other man.

An image of a particular man in the imagination, by contrast, represents only that man, with his color and size. The senses and the imagination supply the materials, but the concept is the work of the intellect.

@question 6.1
Question: What is abstraction?
Choices:
- Drawing out what a thing is from experience, leaving aside what belongs only to this or that individual
- Forgetting what we once knew
- Imagining something that does not exist
- Counting the individuals of a kind
Answer: Drawing out what a thing is from experience, leaving aside what belongs only to this or that individual
Why: By abstraction the intellect draws out the nature from our experience of individuals and forms a universal concept.
Why not the others: Abstraction is not forgetting, inventing, or counting; it is grasping what is common to many.

@question 6.2
Question: Which of these is a universal concept?
Choices:
- “Man”
- “Socrates”
- “This man here”
- “The tallest man in Athens”
Answer: “Man”
Why: “Man” can be said of many, of Socrates, of Plato, and of every other man.
Why not the others: The other three each pick out one individual.

@question 6.3
Question: What do the senses supply when we come to know?
Choices:
- The materials from which the intellect abstracts the concept
- The concept itself, already complete and universal before any abstraction
- Nothing at all
- Only errors
Answer: The materials from which the intellect abstracts the concept
Why: Knowledge begins in the senses, but the concept is the work of the intellect, which abstracts it from what the senses supply.
Why not the others: The senses do not form concepts, but they do supply the materials, and they are not a source of error alone.

@card 7

**Comprehension and extension.** Every universal concept can be measured in two ways. Its **comprehension** is the set of notes (that is, the characteristics) that make it up: the comprehension of “man” includes substance, living body, sentient, and rational. Its **extension** is the range of individuals, or of kinds, of which it can be said: the extension of “man” is all men, and that of “animal” is all sentient beings.

The two vary inversely. “Animal” has less comprehension than “man,” since it lacks the note “rational,” and for that very reason it has greater extension, since it can be said of beasts as well as of men. In the same way “polygon” has greater extension but less comprehension than “quadrilateral,” and “quadrilateral” than “square.”

@question 7.1
Question: What is the comprehension of a concept?
Choices:
- The notes, or characteristics, that make it up
- The individuals or kinds of which it can be said
- The number of letters in its word
- How well we understand it
Answer: The notes, or characteristics, that make it up
Why: The comprehension of “man” includes substance, living body, sentient, and rational.
Why not the others: The individuals make up its extension; the word and our degree of understanding are another matter.

@question 7.2
Question: What is the extension of a concept?
Choices:
- The individuals or kinds of which it can be said
- Its notes or characteristics, taken all together
- How long it lasts in the mind
- Its place in a sentence
Answer: The individuals or kinds of which it can be said
Why: The extension of “animal” is all sentient beings, of whatever kind.
Why not the others: The notes make up its comprehension; time and place in a sentence have nothing to do with extension.

@question 7.3
Question: Compare “animal” with “man.” Which is true?
Choices:
- “Animal” has greater extension and less comprehension
- “Animal” has greater extension and greater comprehension
- “Animal” has less extension and less comprehension
- The two have the same extension
Answer: “Animal” has greater extension and less comprehension
Why: “Animal” lacks the note “rational,” so it has less comprehension, and for that reason it can be said of more things, so it has greater extension.
Why not the others: Comprehension and extension vary inversely, so they cannot both be greater or both be less.

@card 8

**What follows in the First Act.** The exercises of the First Act take up four ways of making a concept clear and distinct. **The predicables** (Exercise I) are the five ways a universal can be said of a subject (genus, species, difference, property, and accident), which Porphyry, a third-century Neoplatonist, set out in his introduction to the *Categories*. **The categories** (Exercise II) are Aristotle’s ten highest kinds: substance and the nine kinds of accident.

**Division** (Exercise III) distributes a whole into its parts. Dividing a genus into its species is *logical division*, as when animal is divided into rational and irrational animals; dividing an essence into its parts is *essential division*, as when man is divided into body and soul, or into animal and rational. **Definition** (Exercise IV) sets forth what a thing is, or what a word means. John of St. Thomas explains the use of each: a simple thing is made clear by a definition when its essence is obscure, and by a division when its parts are confused.

@question 8.1
Question: Dividing “animal” into rational and irrational animals is…
Choices:
- logical division, a genus into its species
- essential division, an essence into its parts
- a definition
- a category
Answer: logical division, a genus into its species
Why: To divide a genus into its species is logical division.
Why not the others: Essential division divides an essence into its parts, as man into body and soul; a definition says what a thing is; and a category is a highest kind.

@question 8.2
Question: Dividing man into body and soul is…
Choices:
- essential division, an essence into its parts
- logical division, a genus into the species beneath it
- a definition
- one of the predicables
Answer: essential division, an essence into its parts
Why: Body and soul are the parts of man’s essence, so this division is essential.
Why not the others: Body and soul are not species of man, so the division is not logical.

@question 8.3
Question: How many categories did Aristotle name, and which comes first?
Choices:
- Ten, and substance comes first
- Five, and genus comes first
- Ten, and quality comes first
- Three, and judgment comes first
Answer: Ten, and substance comes first
Why: The ten categories are substance and the nine kinds of accident. Substance comes first because the others exist in substance.
Why not the others: Five is the number of the predicables, and three the number of the acts of the mind.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Predicables
########################################################################

@deck leadin-1
Title: Before the Exercise · The Predicables
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963).

**The five predicables.** Porphyry, a third-century Neoplatonist, set out in his introduction to Aristotle’s *Categories* the five ways in which a universal term can be said of a subject: genus, species, difference, property, and accident. Each question asks which of these a predicate is in relation to its subject. The same predicate can be a different predicable of a different subject, so the question always concerns this predicate said of this subject.

The **genus** is said of many things that differ in species, and it answers the question “What is it?” with the wider nature, as “animal” is said of man and of the horse. The **species** is said of many things that differ only in number, that is, of individuals, and it answers the same question completely: Socrates is a man, and Fido is a dog. The **difference** (differentia) divides the genus and makes the species; it answers the question “What sort of thing is it, in its very nature?” (*quale quid*), as “rational” divides animal and makes man.

The **property** (proprium) is no part of what the thing is, but it follows from what the thing is, and it belongs to every member of the species, to that species alone, and always; so the power to laugh is a property of man. The **accident** is what a subject may have or lack while it remains the same thing, as Socrates may be pale or tanned. An accident is either separable, as sitting is, or inseparable, as the blackness of a raven is; but even an inseparable accident is no part of what the subject is, since being a raven does not consist in being black.

@question 1.1
Question: In “Socrates is a man,” which predicable is “man”?
Choices:
- Species
- Genus
- Difference
- Accident
Answer: Species
Why: “Man” is said of Socrates as his species, the kind said of individuals that differ only in number.
Why not the others: The genus would be a wider kind, such as “animal”; the difference would be “rational”; and an accident can come and go while Socrates remains.

@question 1.2
Question: Which question does the difference answer?
Choices:
- “What sort of thing is it, in its very nature?”
- “What is it?” as to the wider kind
- “How much of it is there?”
- “What merely happens to be true of it, and could be otherwise?”
Answer: “What sort of thing is it, in its very nature?”
Why: The genus answers “What is it?” with the wider nature; the difference tells what sort of thing it is in its nature, as “rational” tells what sort of animal a man is.
Why not the others: The wider kind is the genus, quantity is a category and not a predicable, and what merely happens to be true is an accident.

@question 1.3
Question: Every raven is black. Which predicable is “black” of the raven?
Choices:
- An inseparable accident
- A property
- The difference
- The genus
Answer: An inseparable accident
Why: Blackness never leaves the raven, but being a raven does not consist in being black, and much that is black is not a raven. So it is an accident, though an inseparable one.
Why not the others: A property belongs to its species alone, and the difference and the genus are part of what the thing is; blackness is neither.

@card 2

**Two questions, and a test.** Two questions sort four of the predicables: is the predicate part of what the subject is, and is it said of that subject alone? If it is part of what the subject is and is said of more than the subject, it is the genus, as “figure” is said of the triangle. If it is part of what the subject is and is said of it alone, it is the difference (“three-sided”). If it is not part of what the subject is, yet is said of it alone, of all of it, and always, it is the property (“having its angles equal to two right angles”). If it is not part of what the subject is and is not said of it alone, it is an accident (“drawn in chalk”).

A property converts with its species; that is, subject and predicate can exchange places and both statements remain true. Every man is able to laugh, and whatever is able to laugh is a man. A genus does not convert: every man is an animal, but not every animal is a man. Nor does an accident, even one that never leaves its subject: every raven is black, but much that is black is not a raven. Porphyry gives several senses of “property,” and the exercise uses the strict one, in which a property belongs to the whole species, to it alone, and always.

A property is often a power, so a small change of wording changes the predicable. A man is always able to laugh, though he is not always laughing; so “Man is able to laugh” gives the property, and “This man is laughing” gives an accident. In the same way, being able to learn grammar is a property of man, but knowing grammar is an accident of this boy, since knowledge is gained and can be lost.

@question 2.1
Question: “A triangle is a figure.” How do the two questions sort the predicate?
Choices:
- It is part of what a triangle is, and is said of more than triangles, so it is the genus
- It is part of what a triangle is, and is said of triangles alone, so it is the difference
- It is not part of what a triangle is, yet is said of triangles alone, so it is the property
- It is not part of what a triangle is, and is not said of triangles alone, so it is an accident
Answer: It is part of what a triangle is, and is said of more than triangles, so it is the genus
Why: To be a triangle is to be a figure, but squares and circles are figures too; so “figure” is the genus.
Why not the others: “Three-sided” would be the difference, and “having its angles equal to two right angles” the property.

@question 2.2
Question: Which of these gives the property of man?
Choices:
- “Man is able to laugh”
- “This man is laughing”
- “Man is an animal”
- “Man is rational”
Answer: “Man is able to laugh”
Why: The power to laugh belongs to every man, to man alone, and always, and it follows from what man is without being part of it.
Why not the others: The act of laughing comes and goes, so it is an accident; “animal” is the genus, and “rational” the difference.

@question 2.3
Question: Every man is an animal. Is every animal a man?
Choices:
- No, so “animal” does not convert with “man,” and it is the genus
- Yes, so “animal” is a property of man
- No, so “animal” is an accident of man
- Yes, so “animal” is the difference that marks off man from the brutes
Answer: No, so “animal” does not convert with “man,” and it is the genus
Why: A genus is said of more than any one species under it, and so it does not convert with the species.
Why not the others: Not every animal is a man, so it does not convert; and “animal” is part of what man is, so it is not an accident.

@card 3

**Genus and species are relative.** In the tree of Porphyry, which arranges the kinds from the highest genus down to the individual, a kind in the middle is a genus in relation to what is below it and a species in relation to what is above it: “animal” is a genus in relation to man, and a species in relation to living body. A remote genus is still a genus, so “An oak is a living thing” gives a genus of the oak (an oak is a tree, a tree a plant, and a plant a living thing). The highest genus in that tree is substance, which is never a species; each of the ten categories is a highest genus in its own line, and being itself is not a genus. The lowest kind, such as man, is always a species and never a genus. An individual such as Socrates is neither, because he is said of nothing else; things are only said of him.

The predicables are not the same as the categories. The categories sort what things are, as we first know them (first intentions); the predicables are relations that natures have only because they are known (second intentions), though these relations are grounded in what the things are. So in “Man is a species,” the term “man” does not stand for any man, since no man is a species; it stands for the nature as known. What a term stands for in a proposition is called its supposition, and in this sentence the supposition of “man” is simple.

@question 3.1
Question: Is “animal” a genus or a species?
Choices:
- Both, since it is a genus in relation to man and a species in relation to living body
- A genus only, never a species
- A species only, never a genus
- Neither, since it is an individual
Answer: Both, since it is a genus in relation to man and a species in relation to living body
Why: In the tree of Porphyry a kind in the middle is a genus to what lies below it and a species to what lies above it.
Why not the others: Only the highest genus is never a species, only the lowest kind is never a genus, and an individual is neither.

@question 3.2
Question: In “Man is a species,” what does “man” stand for?
Choices:
- The nature as known, not any man
- Each particular man
- The word “man” itself, as spoken or written
- Nothing at all
Answer: The nature as known, not any man
Why: No man is a species, so the term stands for the nature as known; its supposition here is simple.
Why not the others: If it stood for particular men the sentence would be false; and it is not about the word, as “‘Man’ has three letters” would be.

@question 3.3
Question: How do the predicables differ from the categories?
Choices:
- The predicables are relations natures have only as known (second intentions); the categories sort what things are (first intentions)
- The categories are relations in the mind; the predicables are things outside it
- They are two names for the same ten kinds
- The predicables are mere names, with no basis in things
Answer: The predicables are relations natures have only as known (second intentions); the categories sort what things are (first intentions)
Why: Genus, species, and the rest are relations a nature has because it is known, though grounded in what things are; the categories sort the things themselves as we first know them.
Why not the others: The order is the reverse of the second option; there are five predicables and ten categories; and the predicables are grounded in things, not mere names.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Categories
########################################################################

@deck leadin-2
Title: Before the Exercise · The Categories
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963); St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Metaphysics*, bk. V, lect. 9; Aristotle, *Categories* 5.

**Substance and the nine accidents.** Aristotle sets out ten categories, the highest kinds under which anything said of a thing falls: substance, quantity, quality, relation, action, passion, when, where, posture, and having. Each question asks under which category a predicate falls. **Substance** is what exists in its own right and not in another. The other nine are accidents, which exist only in a substance or affect it, and so every other category depends on substance.

Aristotle distinguishes two senses of substance (*Categories* 5). Primary substance is the individual, such as this man or this horse, and it is neither said of a subject nor present in one. Secondary substance is the species or genus to which the individual belongs, such as man or animal, and it is said of a subject but is present in none, since “man” is said of Socrates. The mark most proper to substance is that one and the same substance can receive contraries through its own change: the same man is pale at one time and dark at another, while one and the same color cannot be both white and black. Substance itself has no contrary, and it does not admit of more and less.

@question 1.1
Question: On which category do the other nine depend?
Choices:
- Substance
- Quality
- Quantity
- Relation
Answer: Substance
Why: Substance alone exists in its own right; the other nine are accidents, which exist only in a substance or affect it.
Why not the others: Quality, quantity, and relation are themselves accidents of a substance.
