@question 1.2
Question: How does secondary substance (man, animal) differ from primary substance (this man)?
Choices:
- It is said of a subject, though it is present in none
- It is present in a subject, as an accident is
- It is neither said of a subject nor present in one
- It is an accident of the individual
Answer: It is said of a subject, though it is present in none
Why: “Man” is said of Socrates, but it is not present in him as whiteness is (*Categories* 5).
Why not the others: Being neither said of a subject nor present in one is the mark of primary substance; and secondary substance is not an accident.

@question 1.3
Question: What is the mark most proper to substance?
Choices:
- One and the same substance can receive contraries
- It has a contrary, as hot has cold
- It admits of more and less, as white and hot both do
- It is always present in a subject
Answer: One and the same substance can receive contraries
Why: The same man is pale at one time and dark at another, but one and the same color cannot be both white and black.
Why not the others: Substance has no contrary and admits of no degrees, and it is never present in a subject.

@card 2

**How the nine follow from substance.** St. Thomas explains how the nine accidents follow from the different ways they relate to substance (commentary on the *Metaphysics*, bk. V, lect. 9). Some accidents inhere in the substance. What inheres in it absolutely follows either from its matter, and this is **quantity** (“two feet long,” “the number ten”), or from its form, and this is **quality** (“white,” “hot,” “just”). Quantity is either discrete, as a number is, or continuous, as a line or a surface is. What inheres in the substance relatively, as a bearing toward something else, is **relation** (“double,” “half,” “a master”).

Other accidents affect the substance. What affects it by being in it is **action**, what the substance does to another (“cutting,” “burning”), and **passion**, what is done to it (“being cut,” “being burnt”). What affects it from outside either measures it or does not. It measures it in time, and this is **when** (“yesterday,” “now”), or in position; and position has two aspects, **where**, the place the substance is in (“in the marketplace”), and **posture**, the arrangement of its parts in that place (“he sits,” “he lies”). What affects it from outside without measuring it, as something it has on it, is **having**, also called habit (“he is shod,” “he is armed”).

@question 2.1
Question: Quantity belongs to a substance by reason of its ___, quality by reason of its ___.
Choices:
- matter; form
- form; matter
- end; agent
- place; time
Answer: matter; form
Why: St. Thomas derives quantity from the matter and quality from the form (commentary on the *Metaphysics*, bk. V, lect. 9).
Why not the others: The order is not reversed, and end, agent, place, and time do not ground these two categories.

@question 2.2
Question: Under which category does “being burnt” fall?
Choices:
- Passion
- Action
- Quality
- When
Answer: Passion
Why: It is what is done to the substance, the receiving end of an action.
Why not the others: “Burning” would be action; “hot” would be quality; and “being burnt” does not measure the thing in time.

@question 2.3
Question: Under which category does “he is armed” fall?
Choices:
- Having
- Posture
- Where
- Action
Answer: Having
Why: It is something the man has on him, which affects him from outside without measuring him.
Why not the others: Posture is the arrangement of his parts, where is his place, and action is something he does to another.

@card 3

**Distinctions that are easily missed.** Knowledge and virtue, taken as lasting states of the soul, are habits in the sense of a lasting quality, the first species of quality. This is not the category of having, which concerns what a thing has on it, such as shoes or armor. (Knowledge spoken of as knowledge of the knowable is also relative.) Posture differs from where, because where is the place itself, while posture is the arrangement of the parts in that place; and the posture taken up (“he sits”) is not an action going on. Relation is the category whose whole being is a bearing toward another, so that double cannot be understood without half, nor a master without a servant.

Finally, the categories differ from the predicables. The ten categories sort what things are, as we first know them (first intentions). The five predicables are relations that natures have only because they are known (second intentions), though these relations are grounded in what the things are.

@question 3.1
Question: To which category does knowledge, taken as a lasting state of the soul, belong?
Choices:
- Quality
- Having
- Substance
- Action
Answer: Quality
Why: Knowledge and virtue are habits in the sense of a lasting quality, the first species of quality.
Why not the others: The category of having concerns what a thing has on it, such as shoes or armor; knowledge is neither a substance nor something done to another.

@question 3.2
Question: How does posture differ from where?
Choices:
- Where is the place; posture is the arrangement of the parts in that place
- They are the same category under two names
- Posture places the thing in time
- Posture is what a thing has on it
Answer: Where is the place; posture is the arrangement of the parts in that place
Why: A man may stay in the same place and sit, stand, or lie; so posture and where differ.
Why not the others: Placing in time is when, and what a thing has on it is having.

@question 3.3
Question: Which category has for its whole being a bearing toward another?
Choices:
- Relation
- Quality
- Substance
- Where
Answer: Relation
Why: A relative such as double cannot be understood without half, nor a master without a servant.
Why not the others: Quality and substance are understood in themselves, and where places a thing without referring it to another.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Division
########################################################################

@deck leadin-3
Title: Before the Exercise · Division
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963).

**What division is, and its kinds.** John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, defines division as “a statement that distributes a thing into its members or a term into its meanings” (*Summulae*, bk. II, ch. 4). Each question gives divisions of a whole into its members and asks which keeps or breaks a rule, or which kind of division one of them is. Division leads to definition, since a genus is divided by differences until a single species is reached.

Because there are different kinds of whole, there are different kinds of division, and the exercise names four. A **logical division** divides a genus into its species, which are marked off from one another by opposed differences, as animals are divided into the rational and the irrational, or musical instruments into strings, winds, and percussion. An **integral division** divides a whole into the parts that make it up, and none of these parts is called by the name of the whole; so a book is divided into its chapters, and no chapter is a book. A **division by powers** divides a soul, or one of its powers, according to what it is able to do, as the soul is divided into its power to know and its power to desire, or prudence into taking counsel, judging, and commanding. An **accidental division** sorts a subject by features it can gain or lose without becoming a different thing, as apples are divided into the red and the green.

Logical division is not the same as **essential division**, which divides an essence into its parts, as man is divided into body and soul, or into animal and rational. A division by powers is not an integral division either: a paw is a bodily part, not a power of the soul.

@question 1.1
Question: What kind of division is “animals into the rational and the irrational”?
Choices:
- A logical division
- An integral division
- A division by powers
- An accidental division
Answer: A logical division
Why: It divides a genus into its species by opposed differences.
Why not the others: Rational and irrational animals are not parts that make up a whole, nor powers of a soul, nor features gained and lost.

@question 1.2
Question: What kind of division is “man into body and soul”?
Choices:
- An essential division, since it divides an essence into its parts
- A logical division, since it divides a genus into the species under it
- An accidental division
- A division by powers
Answer: An essential division, since it divides an essence into its parts
Why: Body and soul are the parts that make up man’s essence; neither is a species of man.
Why not the others: A logical division would give kinds of which the whole is said, and body is not a kind of man.

@question 1.3
Question: Why is “a house into foundation, walls, and roof” an integral division?
Choices:
- It divides a whole into the parts that make it up, and no part is called a house
- It divides a genus into its species
- It sorts houses by features they can gain or lose
- It divides the house according to what it can do
Answer: It divides a whole into the parts that make it up, and no part is called a house
Why: The parts together make up the house, and none of them is itself a house.
Why not the others: A wall is not a kind of house, a feature gained or lost, or a power.

@card 2

**The rules of division.** Whatever its kind, a sound division keeps four rules. First, the members taken together must cover the whole, leaving nothing out; otherwise the division is incomplete, as when living things are divided into plants and animals and the fungi are left out. Second, the members must not overlap, so that nothing falls under two of them at once. Third, every member must belong to the whole being divided, since a member that lies outside the whole does not divide it; so a tree is not divided into root, trunk, and the soil, because the soil is no part of the tree. Fourth, the division must be made on a single basis: to divide shoes into leather, canvas, and left-footed is to sort by material and by foot at once, and so the members cut across one another.

A division can break more than one rule at once, and then each true diagnosis is correct. So in judging a division we ask four questions in turn: does anything belonging to the whole fall under none of the members; does anything fall under two of them; is any member outside the whole; and are the members sorted on one basis?

@question 2.1
Question: “Living things into plants and animals.” Which rule does this division break?
Choices:
- The members must cover the whole
- The members must not overlap
- Every member must belong to the whole
- The division must be made on a single basis
Answer: The members must cover the whole
Why: The fungi are living things that fall under neither member, so the division is incomplete.
Why not the others: Nothing is both plant and animal, both members are living things, and both are sorted on one basis.

@question 2.2
Question: “Shoes into leather, canvas, and left-footed.” Which rule does this division break?
Choices:
- The division must be made on a single basis
- The members must cover the whole
- Every member must belong to the whole being divided
- It breaks no rule
Answer: The division must be made on a single basis
Why: Leather and canvas sort by material, and left-footed by foot, so the members cut across one another.
Why not the others: The fault is the mixed basis, which makes a left-footed leather shoe fall under two members.

@question 2.3
Question: “Numbers into the even, the odd, and the circles.” Which rule does this division break?
Choices:
- Every member must belong to the whole
- The members must not overlap
- The members must cover the whole
- It breaks no rule
Answer: Every member must belong to the whole
Why: A circle is not a number, so that member lies outside the whole being divided.
Why not the others: The even and the odd neither overlap nor leave any number out; the fault is the member that does not belong.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Definition
########################################################################

@deck leadin-4
Title: Before the Exercise · Definition
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**The four kinds of definition.** A definition, as John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, puts it, is “a statement that sets forth the essence of a thing or the meaning of a term” (*Summulae*, bk. II, ch. 3). Each question gives definitions and asks which keeps or breaks a rule, or which kind of definition one of them is. The tradition distinguishes four kinds.

The **nominal** definition gives what the name means, as “philosopher” means a lover of wisdom. Aristotle puts it first, because we cannot ask what a thing is until we know what its name refers to. The **essential** definition says what the thing is, by its nearest genus and the difference that marks it off from everything else in that genus: man is a rational animal. The **descriptive** definition picks the thing out by a property or by a telltale feature, without saying what it is: man is the animal that can laugh, and the bee is the insect that makes honey. The **causal** definition identifies the thing through its outside causes, that is, by what made it or by what it is for: a clock is an instrument made to measure time, and a scar is the mark left by a healed wound.

A definition by matter and form is not a causal definition, although matter and form are causes, because they are inside the thing rather than outside it. John of St. Thomas counts it as a kind of essential definition, which he calls the physical definition.

@question 1.1
Question: “‘Philosopher’ means a lover of wisdom.” What kind of definition is this?
Choices:
- Nominal
- Essential
- Descriptive
- Causal
Answer: Nominal
Why: It explains what the word means, not what the thing is.
Why not the others: It gives no genus and difference, no property or telltale feature, and no outside cause.

@question 1.2
Question: “A saw is a toothed blade made for the cutting of wood.” What kind of definition is this?
Choices:
- Causal
- Nominal
- Essential
- Descriptive
Answer: Causal
Why: It defines through the end for which the saw is made, which is an outside cause.
Why not the others: It does not explain a word, give the nearest genus and specific difference, or point the thing out by a telltale feature.

@question 1.3
Question: “The magnet is the stone that draws iron.” What kind of definition is this?
Choices:
- Descriptive
- Essential
- Causal
- Nominal
Answer: Descriptive
Why: An effect peculiar to the magnet points it out without saying what it is.
Why not the others: It does not say what the magnet is, name what made it or what it is for, or explain the word.

@card 2

**The rules of definition.** Whatever its kind, a good definition keeps these rules. It must fit exactly what it defines, being neither too broad nor too narrow; that is, it must be convertible with the thing defined, true of exactly the things the term is true of. So “A triangle is a figure” is too broad, and “A triangle is a figure with three equal sides” is too narrow. It must be clearer than the thing defined, and so it may not use a metaphor: “A triangle is geometry’s arrowhead” pleases but explains nothing. It must not contain the term being defined, since nothing is made clearer by itself, and so “A triangle is a triangular figure” is circular.

A definition should also say what a thing is rather than what it is not, wherever a positive account is available; “A line is length without breadth” is allowed, because no positive account is at hand. And it should be brief, giving the nearest genus and the difference and nothing more, so that “A triangle is a three-sided figure drawn with a ruler” and “Man is a rational animal able to laugh” both carry wasted words. “A triangle is a three-sided figure” keeps every rule.

@question 2.1
Question: Which rule does “A triangle is a figure” break?
Choices:
- It is too broad
- It is too narrow
- It is circular
- It is a metaphor
Answer: It is too broad
Why: It gives only the genus, and so it covers squares and circles as well as triangles.
Why not the others: It leaves out no triangle, does not contain the word “triangle,” and is literal.

@question 2.2
Question: Why may the defined term not appear in its own definition?
Choices:
- Because nothing is made clearer by itself
- Because a definition must be a single word
- Because only a division may repeat a term
- Because it would make the definition too brief
Answer: Because nothing is made clearer by itself
Why: A definition must be clearer than the thing defined, and a thing is not clarified by itself.
Why not the others: A definition is a phrase, not a single word, and the fault is circularity, not brevity.

@question 2.3
Question: Which rule does “Man is a rational animal able to laugh” break?
Choices:
- It should be brief, giving only the nearest genus and the difference
- It is too narrow
- It is circular
- It is negative
Answer: It should be brief, giving only the nearest genus and the difference
Why: “Rational animal” already says what man is, so the property adds wasted words.
Why not the others: It still fits every man and only men, it does not contain “man,” and it is positive.


########################################################################
# The Second Act: Judgment (Composition and Division)
########################################################################

@deck act2
Title: The Second Act: Judgment (Composition and Division)
Small heading: THE SECOND ACT

Affirming or denying one thing of another: the proposition and its parts; quality and quantity; the four forms A, E, I, and O; truth in the proposition; existential import; and the square of opposition.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Timothy Kearns, “Outline and Synopsis of Accounts of Logic in the Western Tradition”; St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, proem; John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, *De interpretatione* and *Metaphysics* IV.7; Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**Composing and dividing.** The second act of the mind affirms or denies one thing of another. The tradition calls this act *composing and dividing*: to affirm is to compose, joining a predicate to a subject (“snow is white”), and to deny is to divide, holding them apart (“no stone is alive”). St. Thomas says that in this act of the intellect the true and the false are present for the first time, and he assigns its study to Aristotle’s *De interpretatione*.

The act of the mind is the **judgment**; its expression in words is the **proposition**.

@question 1.1
Question: True or false: composing and dividing, that is, affirming or denying one thing of another, is the second act of the mind.
Choices:
- True
- False
Answer: True
Why: To compose is to affirm and to divide is to deny; either way the second act makes a statement that is true or false.
Why not the others: Composing and dividing is judgment, the second act of the mind.

@question 1.2
Question: To deny one thing of another is to…
Choices:
- divide, holding predicate and subject apart
- compose, joining the predicate to the subject
- define, saying what the subject is
- infer, drawing a conclusion
Answer: divide, holding predicate and subject apart
Why: “No stone is alive” divides “alive” from “stone.”
Why not the others: Composing is affirming; defining belongs to the first act; inferring to the third.

@question 1.3
Question: According to St. Thomas, where are the true and the false present for the first time?
Choices:
- In the second act, composing and dividing
- In the first act, simple apprehension, before any judgment
- In the senses
- Only in a completed syllogism
Answer: In the second act, composing and dividing
Why: The true and the false first appear when the mind affirms or denies, which is the second act.
Why not the others: Simple apprehension asserts nothing, the senses do not judge, and a syllogism is made of propositions that are already true or false.

@card 2

**The proposition.** A proposition is a sentence that is true or false; more fully, it is a complete discourse signifying the true or the false. Not every sentence is a proposition. A command (“Shut the door”), a question (“Is it raining?”), or a wish (“May you be well”) is neither true nor false, so none of these is a proposition.

Every simple proposition has three parts: the **subject**, of which something is said; the **predicate**, which is said of it; and the **copula** “is” (or “is not”), which joins or separates them. In “Every man is mortal,” the subject is “man,” the predicate is “mortal,” and the copula is “is.”

@question 2.1
Question: Which of these is a proposition?
Choices:
- Every man is mortal.
- Shut the door.
- Is it raining?
- May you be well.
Answer: Every man is mortal.
Why: It says something that is either true or false.
Why not the others: A command, a question, and a wish are neither true nor false.

@question 2.2
Question: In “No stone is alive,” what is the predicate?
Choices:
- “Alive”
- “Stone”
- “Is”
- “No”
Answer: “Alive”
Why: “Alive” is what is denied of the subject, “stone.”
Why not the others: “Stone” is the subject, “is” the copula, and “no” the sign of quantity.

@question 2.3
Question: What does the copula do?
Choices:
- It joins or separates subject and predicate
- It names the subject
- It states the quantity
- It marks the conclusion of an argument as drawn
Answer: It joins or separates subject and predicate
Why: The copula “is” (or “is not”) unites or divides the two terms.
Why not the others: The subject names what is spoken of; “all,” “no,” and “some” state the quantity; and “therefore” marks a conclusion.

@card 3

**Quality and quantity.** Each simple categorical proposition has a quality and a quantity. Its **quality** is affirmative or negative, according as it affirms or denies the predicate of the subject. Its **quantity** is universal or particular, according as it speaks of every member of the subject (“all,” “no”) or only of some (“some”).

A singular proposition, such as “Socrates is wise,” has a single individual as its subject. Because it speaks of the whole of its subject, it is treated in the syllogism as universal.

@question 3.1
Question: What is the quality of “Some men are not just”?
Choices:
- Negative
- Affirmative
- Universal
- Particular
Answer: Negative
Why: It denies “just” of some men, so it is negative. (Its quantity is particular.)
Why not the others: Quality is affirmative or negative; universal and particular are quantities.

@question 3.2
Question: What is the quantity of “No stone is alive”?
Choices:
- Universal
- Particular
- Affirmative
- Negative
Answer: Universal
Why: It speaks of every stone, so it is universal. (Its quality is negative.)
Why not the others: Quantity is universal or particular; affirmative and negative are qualities.

@question 3.3
Question: How is “Socrates is wise” treated in the syllogism?
Choices:
- As universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject
- As particular, since Socrates is only one man among very many
- As neither true nor false
- As a question
Answer: As universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject
Why: A singular proposition speaks of the whole of its subject, so it is treated as universal.
Why not the others: Being about one man does not make it particular in quantity; it is a proposition, true or false.

@card 4

**The four forms: A, E, I, O.** Quality and quantity together give four forms, which the tradition names with the vowels of the Latin *affirmo* (“I affirm”) and *nego* (“I deny”):

**A**, the universal affirmative: All S are P.
**E**, the universal negative: No S are P.
**I**, the particular affirmative: Some S are P.
**O**, the particular negative: Some S are not P.

These four are not an arbitrary choice. They relate two general terms in the simplest ways possible, and so the study of them shows the basic structure of the assertions and denials we make in demonstration.

@question 4.1
Question: Which form is “No S are P”?
Choices:
- E
- A
- I
- O
Answer: E
Why: E is the universal negative.
Why not the others: A is the universal affirmative, I the particular affirmative, and O the particular negative.

@question 4.2
Question: Which form is “Some S are not P”?
Choices:
- O
- E
- I
- A
Answer: O
Why: O is the particular negative.
Why not the others: E denies of every S, I affirms of some, and A affirms of every.

@question 4.3
Question: Where do the names A, E, I, O come from?
Choices:
- From the vowels of *affirmo* (A, I) and *nego* (E, O)
- From the first letters of four philosophers
- From the Greek alphabet
- From the order of the categories
Answer: From the vowels of *affirmo* (A, I) and *nego* (E, O)
Why: The affirmatives take the first two vowels of *affirmo*, “I affirm,” and the negatives the vowels of *nego*, “I deny.”
Why not the others: The letters are the vowels of two Latin verbs.

@card 5

**Truth is in the proposition.** Truth is properly in the judgment of the intellect, and the proposition, which signifies that judgment, is true in a secondary sense. Aristotle states it this way: “To say of what is that it is not, or of what is not that it is, is false, while to say of what is that it is, and of what is not that it is not, is true” (*Metaphysics* IV.7).

The judgment is the act that asserts, and so truth does not belong properly to the first act of the mind, which asserts nothing. As St. Thomas says, truth has its foundation in reality, but it is completed in the intellect, when a thing is understood to be as it is.

@question 5.1
Question: According to Aristotle, which of these is true?
Choices:
- To say of what is that it is
- To say of what is that it is not
- To say of what is not that it is
- To say nothing at all
Answer: To say of what is that it is
Why: To say of what is that it is, and of what is not that it is not, is true (*Metaphysics* IV.7).
Why not the others: The second and third are Aristotle’s examples of falsehood; and saying nothing asserts nothing, so it is neither true nor false.

@question 5.2
Question: Where is truth properly found?
Choices:
- In the judgment of the intellect
- In the proposition alone, apart from the judgment
- In a single concept, before anything is asserted
- In the sound of the voice that pronounces the words
Answer: In the judgment of the intellect
Why: Truth is properly in the judgment of the intellect. The proposition signifies that judgment, and so the proposition is true only in a secondary sense.
Why not the others: The proposition is true because it signifies a true judgment, so its truth is secondary. A concept asserts nothing, and the sound of the voice is only a sign.

@question 5.3
Question: True or false: a single concept, such as “snow,” is properly true or false.
Choices:
- True
- False
Answer: False
Why: A single concept asserts nothing, so truth does not belong to it properly. Truth is properly in the judgment, and the proposition that signifies the judgment is true only in a secondary sense.
Why not the others: Truth belongs properly to the judgment of the intellect.

@card 6

**Existential import.** Does a proposition say that its subject exists? In the older logic an affirmative proposition is true only if its subject stands for something that exists, so an affirmative whose subject stands for nothing is false. “All unicorns are white” is false, because there are no unicorns. That is why “All S are P” yields “Some S are P” (the term of art is *existential import*).

A negative proposition carries no such existence: “No unicorns are white” is true, and so is “Some unicorn is not white.” Modern set theory reads “All S are P” as true when there are no S, and so it denies the step from “All S are P” to “Some S are P.” This course follows the classical way: affirmatives carry existential import, and negatives do not. We do speak truly of fictional and merely thought things, as in “Sherlock Holmes lives on Baker Street,” but there the copula signifies being in a story or in the mind. In the exercises the copula signifies actual existence, unless the matter or the wording extends it, as in a proposition in necessary matter or one with “can,” “will,” or “was.” (We restrict signification to actual existence here because our goal in logic is guiding reasoning toward certain knowledge. This restriction keeps that goal clear and it also allows us to choose obvious example propositions that are false, like about the Phoenix or Sherlock Holmes; and if we did not restrict consideration to actual existence some of these propositions would be true or at least their truth might be in doubt.) *The Square of Opposition* (workshop) sets the classical reading beside the modern one, on which only the contradictories survive.

@question 6.1
Question: On the classical account that this course follows, “All unicorns are white” is…
Choices:
- false, since an affirmative asserts that its subject exists
- true, since nothing contradicts it
- neither true nor false, since it is about nothing
- true, since a universal only declares a region of the diagram empty
Answer: false, since an affirmative asserts that its subject exists
Why: An affirmative is true only if its subject stands for something that exists. There are no unicorns, so the proposition is false.
Why not the others: The last answer is the modern reading, which the course does not follow; and an affirmative about nothing is false, not without a truth value.

@question 6.2
Question: On the classical view, what follows from “All S are P”?
Choices:
- “Some S are P”
- Nothing further
- “No S are P”
- “All P are S”
Answer: “Some S are P”
Why: Because an affirmative carries existential import, the particular follows from the universal.
Why not the others: Modern set theory denies the step; and neither the contrary nor the converse follows.

@question 6.3
Question: Which propositions carry existential import?
Choices:
- The affirmatives, A and I
- The negatives, E and O
- All four
- None of them
Answer: The affirmatives, A and I
Why: An affirmative asserts that its subject exists; a negative does not, so “No unicorns are white” is true.
Why not the others: The negatives carry no import, and the affirmatives do.

@card 7

**Opposition.** Propositions with the same subject and predicate that differ in quality, in quantity, or in both are *opposed*, and their relations make up the square of opposition. **Contradictories** (A and O; E and I) differ in both quality and quantity, so one must be true and the other false. **Contraries** (A and E) cannot both be true, though both may be false. **Subcontraries** (I and O) cannot both be false, though both may be true. **Subalterns** (A and I; E and O) share their quality: if the universal is true, the particular is true, and if the particular is false, the universal is false.

Because the affirmatives carry existential import and the negatives do not, these relations hold even when the subject stands for nothing: then A and I are false, and E and O are true. On the modern reading, which gives the universal no existential import, contrariety, subcontrariety, and subalternation fail; this is why the course states where import lies.

@question 7.1
Question: “All S are P” and “Some S are not P” are…
Choices:
- contradictories
- contraries
- subcontraries
- subalterns
Answer: contradictories
Why: They differ in both quality and quantity, so one must be true and the other false.
Why not the others: Contraries are A and E, subcontraries I and O, and subalterns share their quality.

@question 7.2
Question: “Some S are P” and “Some S are not P” are…
Choices:
- subcontraries, which cannot both be false
- contraries, which cannot both be true
- contradictories, one true and one false
- subalterns
Answer: subcontraries, which cannot both be false
Why: I and O are subcontraries: both may be true, but they cannot both be false.
Why not the others: Contraries are the two universals, contradictories differ in both quality and quantity, and subalterns share their quality.

@question 7.3
Question: If “All S are P” is true, what is “Some S are P”?
Choices:
- True
- False
- Undetermined
- Neither true nor false
Answer: True
Why: By subalternation, truth passes from the universal to the particular beneath it.
Why not the others: The particular cannot be false when its universal is true.

@card 8

**What follows in the Second Act.** Exercise V draws single propositions on Venn diagrams, in which shading a region declares it empty and an × declares that something is there. Exercise VI treats opposition, equipollence, and conversion as properties of the proposition. Later logicians call these properties “immediate inferences.” Exercise VII treats the *modal* propositions, which say how the predicate belongs to the subject: necessarily, impossibly, or possibly.

The diagrams are taught at the head of Exercise V, and *The Square of Opposition* shows each relation for any pair of terms. The circles picture the extension of the terms, that is, what they stand for, and not their meaning; Venn devised them in 1880 for Boole’s algebra of classes.

@question 8.1
Question: In a Venn diagram, what does shading a region mean?
Choices:
- That the region is empty
- That the region is full
- That we are unsure about the region
- That the region is the conclusion
Answer: That the region is empty
Why: Shading declares a region empty; an × declares that something is there.
Why not the others: The diagram never marks fullness or doubt, and no region is a conclusion.

@question 8.2
Question: What is the converse of “No S are P”?
Choices:
- “No P are S”
- “All P are S”
- “Some S are P”
- “No S are non-P”
Answer: “No P are S”
Why: Conversion exchanges subject and predicate, and an E proposition converts without change of quantity.
Why not the others: The other answers change the quality or add a negation to a term.

@question 8.3
Question: What does a modal proposition say?
Choices:
- How the predicate belongs to the subject: necessarily, impossibly, or possibly
- Only that the predicate belongs to the subject
- What a word means
- Whether an argument is valid
Answer: How the predicate belongs to the subject: necessarily, impossibly, or possibly
Why: A plain proposition says that something is; a modal proposition says how it is.
Why not the others: A plain proposition says only that it belongs; meaning belongs to definition; validity belongs to reasoning.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Diagramming Propositions
########################################################################

@deck leadin-5
Title: Before the Exercise · Diagramming Propositions
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**The diagram.** Venn diagrams are a modern aid, published by John Venn, an English logician, in 1880, and they are used to picture what the tradition teaches. They are not part of its doctrine. In Boole’s algebra, for which Venn drew them, a term names a class that may be empty; in this course a circle pictures what a term stands for. Each circle stands for a term, and where the circles overlap lie the things that fall under both. To **shade** a region is to declare it empty, so that nothing falls there; an **×** says that at least one thing is there; and a region left plain is left open, because the diagram asserts only what is marked. The diagrams could instead be drawn by shading the regions that are occupied and leaving the empty ones blank, but that is not the convention of this course.

SP**A** All S are PSP**E** No S are P×SP**I** Some S are P×SP**O** Some S are not PThe universals (A and E) shade a region empty, and the particulars (I and O) place an × to say that something is there. A shades the part of S that lies outside P; E shades the overlap; I places an × in the overlap; and O places an × in the part of S that lies outside P. The × for O marks something there for the diagram, while O itself carries no existential import, so “Some unicorn is not white” is true.

Useful as they are, these diagrams prove nothing by themselves. They display what a proposition already says, so that we can follow what is included, what is excluded, and whether a thing exists. The diagram assists the reasoning, but it does not replace it. The first instrument of reason is the word, and every exercise in this course is carried out in language.

@question 1.1
Question: In a Venn diagram, to shade a region is to say that…
Choices:
- The region is empty
- The region is full
- The region is left undecided
- The region is the conclusion
Answer: The region is empty
Why: Shading declares a region empty. An × declares that at least one thing is there, and a region left plain is left open.
Why not the others: Fullness would need an ×; a plain region is left open on purpose; and no region is a conclusion.

@question 1.2
Question: An × placed in the overlap of S and P asserts…
Choices:
- Some S are P
- All S are P
- No S are P
- Some S are not P
Answer: Some S are P
Why: An × in the overlap says that at least one thing is both S and P, which is the form I.
Why not the others: All S are P is drawn by shading S outside P; No S are P shades the overlap; and Some S are not P places the × in S outside P.

@question 1.3
Question: A region is left plain, neither shaded nor marked. What does the diagram assert about it?
Choices:
- Nothing is asserted there
- The region is empty
- The region is occupied
- The diagram is unfinished there
Answer: Nothing is asserted there
Why: A diagram asserts only what is marked. What is left plain is left open.
Why not the others: Empty would need shading, and occupied would need an ×. A finished diagram may still leave a region plain.

@question 1.4
Question: Which of the four forms place an ×?
Choices:
- The particulars, I and O
- The universals, A and E
- A and I, the affirmatives
- E and O, the negatives
Answer: The particulars, I and O
Why: A and E shade a region empty. I and O place an × to say that something is there.
Why not the others: The universals shade, and the division is by quantity, so it is not the affirmatives or the negatives as such.

@card 2

**Existence and the ⊗.** In the older logic an affirmative proposition is true only if its subject stands for something that exists, so an affirmative whose subject stands for nothing is false. “All unicorns are white” is false, because there are no unicorns. That is why “All S are P” yields “Some S are P,” and the term of art for this is *existential import*. A negative proposition carries no such existence, so “No unicorns are white” is true. Modern set theory reads “All S are P” as true when there are no S, and so it denies the step from “All S are P” to “Some S are P.” This course follows the classical way.

Because an affirmative carries existential import, “All S are P” also asserts that some S exists. The course draws that existence with a **⊗** in the overlap, which is the open part of S. The ⊗ may be added to a diagram of A, but it is not required, except where an argument needs the existence that the affirmative asserts. A negative asserts no existence, so E and O never take a ⊗. A particular shows its existence with an ×, and not with a ⊗. On the modern reading the diagram of A is shading alone, and no ⊗ could ever be added; the ⊗ is the classical import drawn on a modern figure.

@question 2.1
Question: On the classical account this course follows, “All unicorns are white” is…
Choices:
- False
- True, since nothing contradicts it
- Neither true nor false
- True, because the universal only empties a region
Answer: False
Why: An affirmative asserts that its subject exists, and there are no unicorns, so the proposition is false.
Why not the others: Modern set theory is what reads the universal as true when the subject is empty.

@question 2.2
Question: On the classical view, what else does “All S are P” give?
Choices:
- “Some S are P”
- Nothing further
- “No S are P”
- “All P are S”
Answer: “Some S are P”
Why: An affirmative asserts that its subject exists, so the particular follows.
Why not the others: The contrary and the simple converse do not follow, and the classical account does not stop at the universal.

@question 2.3
Question: Where does modern set theory differ from this course?
Choices:
- It counts “All S are P” true when there are no S
- It denies that classes exist
- It has no universal statements
- It uses diagrams other than these
Answer: It counts “All S are P” true when there are no S
Why: On the modern reading “All S are P” no longer guarantees that any S exists, and the step to “Some S are P” fails. The course also allows a term to stand for nothing (there are no unicorns); the difference is that it counts an affirmative with such a subject false.
Why not the others: Set theory is built on classes, has universal statements, and can use these same diagrams. The real difference is the truth value of an affirmative whose subject stands for nothing.

@question 2.4
Question: How is “No S are P” drawn?
Choices:
- The overlap shaded empty
- An × in the overlap
- The whole of S shaded
- An × outside both circles
Answer: The overlap shaded empty
Why: If nothing is both S and P, the overlap is declared empty, which is what shading does.
Why not the others: An × in the overlap asserts the contradictory. Shading the whole of S would say that there are no S.

@question 2.5
Question: May a ⊗ be added to the diagram of “No S are P”?
Choices:
- No
- Yes, in the overlap
- Yes, and it is required
- Yes, in place of the shading
Answer: No
Why: E shades the overlap and nothing more, because a negative proposition asserts no existence.
Why not the others: Only an A proposition may take a ⊗, and only in the overlap.

@card 3

**Finding the form.** Each item gives one proposition to draw on two circles (from 50 points onward, two premises on three circles). The first step is to find its form. The four standard forms are A, “All S are P”; E, “No S are P”; I, “Some S are P”; and O, “Some S are not P.”

Ordinary wording often hides the form. “Every S is P” and “Whatever is S is P” are A; “S are never P” and “There is no S that is P” are E; “There are S that are P” and “A few S are P” are I; “Not all S are P” and “Not every S is P” are O. “Only P are S” means “All S are P,” so its subject is S. The subject’s circle and the predicate’s circle must not be exchanged, because A and O do not convert: “All S are P” does not say “All P are S.”

@question 3.1
Question: Which form is “Not all S are P”?
Choices:
- O
- A
- E
- I
Answer: O
Why: “Not all S are P” says that some S are not P, which is the O form.
Why not the others: It does not say that every S is P (A), or that none is (E), or that some are (I).

@question 3.2
Question: Which proposition does “Only P are S” state?
Choices:
- All S are P
- All P are S
- Some P are S
- No P are S
Answer: All S are P
Why: If only P are S, then whatever is S is P; so the subject is S.
Why not the others: “Only P are S” does not say that every P is S; that would be the converse.

@card 4

**Two premises on three circles.** From 50 points onward, two premises are drawn together on three circles. The third circle cuts each region of the two-circle diagram in two, so each universal premise shades two cells. The universal premises are shaded first; then each particular premise’s × goes in the one cell of its region that the shading leaves open. Only what the two premises say is drawn, so no ⊗ is added here.

@question 4.1
Question: With two premises on three circles, which is drawn first?
Choices:
- The shading of the universal premises
- The × of the particular premise
- The conclusion
- A ⊗ for each term
Answer: The shading of the universal premises
Why: Shading first narrows the region of the particular premise to one open cell, where its × then goes.
Why not the others: An × placed first may land in a cell that the shading later empties; the conclusion and ⊗ are not drawn here.

@question 4.2
Question: How many cells does one universal premise shade on three circles?
Choices:
- Two
- One
- Three
- Four
Answer: Two
Why: The third circle cuts the region in two, so the premise empties two cells.
Why not the others: Shading only one cell leaves half the region open.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Opposition, Equipollence, and Conversion
########################################################################

@deck leadin-6
Title: Before the Exercise · Opposition, Equipollence, and Conversion
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**Properties of the proposition.** John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, treats these in the *Summulae* (the first part of the *Ars Logica*) as properties of the whole proposition, and not as a separate kind of inference. In book II, chapter 9, he says that the properties that follow the whole proposition are three: opposition, conversion, and equipollence. They hold between propositions that have the same terms. Later logicians call these properties “immediate inferences.”

**Opposition** is, as he defines it, “the affirmation and negation of the same thing concerning the same thing.” The species are contradictories, contraries, and subcontraries. Contradictories are never both true and never both false. Contraries are never both true, though both can be false when the matter is contingent. Subcontraries are never both false, though both can be true. Subalternation, between the universal and the particular of the same quality, is not properly opposition, except relatively, as he notes. The square of opposition nevertheless sets it out with the other three, and the exercise asks about it.

Some questions suppose that one proposition is true or false and ask whether a second, with the same terms, is true, false, or undetermined. **Contradictories** (A and O, E and I) always take opposite truth values: if “All S are P” is true, “Some S are not P” is false, and if it is false, “Some S are not P” is true. **Contraries** (A and E) cannot both be true, but they can both be false; so if one is true the other is false, but if one is false the other is undetermined. **Subcontraries** (I and O) cannot both be false, but they can both be true; so if one is false the other is true, but if one is true the other is undetermined. In **subalternation** (A and I, E and O), truth descends from the universal to the particular, and falsity ascends from the particular to the universal. If “All S are P” is true, “Some S are P” is true, because an affirmative carries existential import and so supplies the existence that “some” requires; if “Some S are P” is false, “All S are P” is false. A false universal leaves its particular undetermined, and a true particular leaves its universal undetermined. Wherever the square forbids nothing, the answer is “undetermined.” The Square of Opposition, in the Study section, sets out these relations for any pair of terms.

**Equipollence** is the equivalence of opposed propositions, that is, their having the same meaning, brought about by changing the place of a negative particle. A negative placed before one of two contradictories makes them equipollent, provided it negates and does not merely make a term indefinite. So “Not every man argues” has the same meaning as “Some man does not argue,” and “Not some man does not argue” has the same meaning as “Every man argues.” A negative placed after the subject and before the copula makes contraries equipollent, where the distribution is complete. So “Every man does not run” has the same meaning as “No man runs,” and “No man does not run” has the same meaning as “Every man runs.” A negative placed both before and after makes subalterns equipollent. So “Not every man does not argue” has the same meaning as “Some man argues.” The verse is *Prae Contradic, Post Contra, Prae postque Subalter.* Subcontraries have no equipollent by these placements. A negative before a particular makes a universal, which is the contradictory and not an equipollent of the other particular. A negative after a particular affirmative only makes that particular negative, and a second negative after the negative is a useless repetition.

**Conversion** is “the change of the extremes of a proposition from the subject into the predicate and the predicate into the subject, while the quality and the truth remain the same.” There are three kinds: simple, or mutual; accidental, or nonmutual; and by contraposition. Simple conversion keeps the quantity, and it belongs to E and to I. “No man is a stone” therefore gives “No stone is a man,” and “Some man is white” gives “Some white thing is a man.” Accidental conversion changes the quantity. “Every man is an animal” does not give “Every animal is a man,” but “Some animal is a man.” E, which converts simply, can also convert accidentally. Conversion by contraposition exchanges the terms and changes them from determinate to indeterminate, and it belongs to A and to O. From “Every man is an animal” it follows that every non-animal is a non-man. John of St. Thomas notes that this is a conversion according to meaning, since the extremes do not remain the same. The verse is *FECI simpliciter convertitur, EVA per acci, ASTO per contra, sic fit conversio tota.*

@question 1.1
Question: If “All S are P” is true, what of “Some S are not P”?
Choices:
- False
- True
- Undetermined
Answer: False
Why: They are contradictories, which always take opposite truth values.
Why not the others: Contradictories can be neither both true nor both false, so nothing is left undetermined.

@question 1.2
Question: If “All S are P” is false, what of “No S are P”?
Choices:
- Undetermined
- It must be true
- It must be false
Answer: Undetermined
Why: They are contraries, which cannot both be true but can both be false; so the falsity of one settles nothing about the other.
Why not the others: Treating contraries as contradictories is the usual error: “All men are just” and “No men are just” are both false.

@question 1.3
Question: If “Some S are P” is true, what of “All S are P”?
Choices:
- Undetermined
- It must be true
- It must be false
Answer: Undetermined
Why: In subalternation truth descends from the universal to the particular, but it does not ascend.
Why not the others: A true particular leaves its universal open: some men are wise, but that does not show that all are, or that not all are.

@question 1.4
Question: Where is the negative placed when contradictories are made equipollent?
Choices:
- Before one of them
- After the subject only
- Both before the proposition and after the subject
- It cannot be placed there
Answer: Before one of them
Why: A negative before one contradictory makes the two equipollent, as “Not every man argues” has the same meaning as “Some man does not argue.”
Why not the others: A negative after the subject serves the contraries, and a negative both before and after serves the subalterns.

@question 1.5
Question: Which pair has no equipollent by these placements?
Choices:
- The subcontraries
- The contradictories
- The contraries
- The subalterns
Answer: The subcontraries
Why: Subcontraries have no equipollent. A negative before a particular makes a universal, and a negative after a particular affirmative only makes that particular negative.
Why not the others: Contradictories, contraries, and subalterns are the three pairs named in the verse.

@card 2

**Converse, obverse, contrapositive, contradictory, equipollent.** Other questions give one proposition, or a proposition with the negative particle placed in it, and ask for the result, typed in standard form, or “none” when nothing follows. The **contradictory** changes both quantity and quality: the contradictory of A is O, of E is I, of I is E, and of O is A. **Conversion** exchanges subject and predicate. E and I convert simply, so “No S are P” gives “No P are S,” and “Some S are P” gives “Some P are S.” A converts only by limitation (per accidens), to I: “All S are P” gives only “Some P are S,” because the predicate of an affirmative is not distributed. O has no converse.

The **equipollent** is typed as the standard-form proposition that has the same meaning. A negative before the proposition gives the contradictory: “Not every S is P” gives “Some S are not P,” and “Not some S is not P” gives “All S are P.” A negative after the subject of a universal, where the distribution is complete, gives the contrary: “Every S is not P” gives “No S are P,” and “No S is not P” gives “All S are P.” A negative both before and after gives the subaltern: “Not every S is not P” gives “Some S are P.” When the placement makes no equipollent, the answer is “none.”

**Obversion** changes the quality and replaces the predicate by its complement, written with “non-”: “All S are P” gives “No S are non-P,” “No S are P” gives “All S are non-P,” “Some S are P” gives “Some S are not non-P,” and “Some S are not P” gives “Some S are non-P.” The complement must be the contradictory term (“non-just”), not the contrary (“unjust”), since a man may be neither just nor unjust. **Contraposition** obverts, then converts, then obverts again, so that both terms are replaced by their complements and exchange places. “All S are P” gives “All non-P are non-S,” and “Some S are not P” gives “Some non-P are not non-S.” E contraposes only by limitation, to “Some non-P are not non-S,” and I has no contrapositive, because its obverse is an O, and an O does not convert.

Because affirmatives carry existential import, two of these operations need a further condition, as John Buridan, the fourteenth-century master at Paris, observed. Obversion from the negative side to the affirmative (E to A, O to I), and contraposition, hold only when the terms stand for things that exist. “No unicorn is a horse” is true, but “Every unicorn is a non-horse” is false, since there are no unicorns and an affirmative whose subject stands for nothing is false. So when a question says that nothing falls under the subject, the answer to such an operation is “none.”

@question 2.1
Question: What is the converse of “All S are P”?
Choices:
- “Some P are S”
- “All P are S”
- “No P are S”
- It has no converse
Answer: “Some P are S”
Why: A converts only by limitation, since its predicate is not distributed.
Why not the others: “All P are S” is the illicit conversion; and only O lacks a converse.

@question 2.2
Question: What is the obverse of “No S are P”?
Choices:
- “All S are non-P”
- “No non-S are P”
- “All S are P”
- “Some S are non-P”
Answer: “All S are non-P”
Why: Obversion changes the quality (E becomes A) and replaces the predicate by its complement.
Why not the others: Obversion keeps the subject and the quantity, and it must both change the quality and negate the predicate.

@question 2.3
Question: There are no griffins. What follows by obversion from “No griffins are lions”?
Choices:
- Nothing follows
- “All griffins are non-lions”
- “No griffins are non-lions”
- “Some lions are griffins”
Answer: Nothing follows
Why: Obversion from E to A holds only when the subject stands for something; “All griffins are non-lions” would be an affirmative about nothing, and so false.
Why not the others: The obverse form is “All griffins are non-lions,” but with no griffins it does not follow, and the other answers are not obverses at all.

@question 2.4
Question: What is the contrapositive of “Some S are P”?
Choices:
- “Some non-P are non-S”
- “Some P are S”
- “No non-P are non-S”
- None, since an I proposition has no contrapositive
Answer: None, since an I proposition has no contrapositive
Why: Its obverse is “Some S are not non-P,” an O, and an O does not convert, so the contrapositive cannot be formed.
Why not the others: “Some P are S” is the converse; and no proposition with the terms exchanged and complemented follows from an I.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Modal Propositions
########################################################################

@deck leadin-7
Title: Before the Exercise · The Modal Propositions
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Mode and dictum.** A modal proposition says not only that the predicate belongs to the subject but how it belongs. John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, counts four modes: necessary, impossible, possible, and contingent (*Summulae*, bk. II, ch. 21). In this course the contingent is what can be, and can also not be; John of St. Thomas sometimes uses “contingent” to mean simply “possible.” A modal proposition has two parts, the **dictum**, which is what is said (“that Peter is running”), and the **mode**, which is said of the dictum (“it is possible”). Necessary and impossible are universal in force, like “every” and “no”; possible and possible not are particular, like “some” and “some not.” So every modal expression comes to one of four corners: *necessary* (it cannot be false), *impossible* (it cannot be true, which is the same as “necessary not”), *possible* (it can be true), and *possible not* (it can be false).

Two modal expressions are **equipollent** (equal in force) when they come to the same corner. A “not” placed before the mode moves the expression to the contradictory corner: “not possible” is “impossible,” and “not necessary” is only “possible not.” A “not” placed after the mode denies only the dictum: “possible that he is not” is “possible not,” and “necessary that he is not” is “impossible.” So “It is not possible that Peter is not running” comes to “It is necessary that Peter is running.” The commonest error is to read “not necessary” as “impossible,” but denying the stronger mode grants only the weaker denial. The scholastics set out these equipollences under the words Amabimus, Edentuli, Iliace, Purpurea.

@question 1.1
Question: To which corner does “It is not possible that Peter is running” come?
Choices:
- Impossible
- Possible not
- Necessary
- Possible
Answer: Impossible
Why: A “not” before the mode moves the expression to the contradictory corner, and the contradictory of possible is impossible.
Why not the others: “Possible not” would need the “not” after the mode; and denying possibility cannot make a thing necessary or possible.

@question 1.2
Question: Is “It is not necessary that Peter is running” equipollent to “It is impossible that Peter is running”?
Choices:
- No, since “not necessary” comes only to “possible not”
- Yes, since both deny the necessity
- Yes, since both are negative
- No, since “not necessary” comes to “necessary”
Answer: No, since “not necessary” comes only to “possible not”
Why: Denying the stronger mode grants only the weaker denial; what is not necessary may still be possible.
Why not the others: Reading “not necessary” as “impossible” is the commonest modal error, and a denial cannot come to “necessary.”

@question 1.3
Question: In “It is possible that Peter is running,” what is the dictum?
Choices:
- “that Peter is running”
- “possible”
- “Peter”
- “It is possible”
Answer: “that Peter is running”
Why: The dictum is what is said, and the mode is said of it.
Why not the others: “Possible” and “It is possible” give the mode; “Peter” is only the subject of the dictum.

@card 2

**The modal square.** The four corners stand on a square, as A, E, I, and O do. Necessary and impossible are **contraries**: they cannot both be true, but both are false of whatever is contingent. Possible and possible not are **subcontraries**: they cannot both be false, but both are true of whatever is contingent. Necessary and possible not are **contradictories**, and so are impossible and possible; they always take opposite truth values. From the necessary to the possible the inference holds (*ab necesse ad posse valet consequentia*), and likewise from the impossible to the possible not; so truth descends from the stronger mode to the weaker. From the possible to the necessary it does not hold (*a posse ad necesse non valet consequentia*), and only falsity ascends from the weaker mode to the stronger.

**The composite and the divided sense.** Other questions ask whether a modal sentence is true in the composite sense, in the divided sense, in both, or in neither. In the composite sense (*in sensu composito*) the mode governs the whole dictum, its parts taken together at once: “It is possible that the seated man walks” asks whether sitting and walking can be joined at once, and so it is false. In the divided sense (*in sensu diviso*) the mode is said of the subject alone: “The seated man can walk” says that the man who is seated has the power to walk, and this is true, since sitting does not take the power away. In the same way “The one who is seated is necessarily seated” is true in the composite sense and false in the divided sense. Some sentences are true in both senses, as “A man is necessarily an animal,” where the predicate belongs by essence. Some are true in neither, as “The blind man can see,” since blindness is a privation that removes the power itself, and “A man can be a stone,” since the essence of man excludes it.

@question 2.1
Question: If “It is necessary that s is P” is false, what of “It is impossible that s is P”?
Choices:
- Undetermined
- It must be true
- It must be false
Answer: Undetermined
Why: Necessary and impossible are contraries; both are false of whatever is contingent, so the falsity of one settles nothing.
Why not the others: The contradictory of “necessary” is “possible not,” not “impossible.”

@question 2.2
Question: If “It is necessary that s is P” is true, what of “It is possible that s is P”?
Choices:
- True
- False
- Undetermined
Answer: True
Why: From the necessary to the possible the inference holds, so truth descends.
Why not the others: What must be so can be so; only the step from possible to necessary fails.

@question 2.3
Question: “The man who is seated can walk.” In which sense is it true?
Choices:
- In the divided sense only
- In the composite sense only
- In both senses
- In neither sense
Answer: In the divided sense only
Why: Sitting and walking cannot be joined at once, so it is false in the composite sense; but the seated man keeps the power to walk, so it is true in the divided sense.
Why not the others: The composite reading joins sitting and walking at once, which is impossible; and the power to walk is not taken away by sitting.


########################################################################
# The Third Act: Reasoning
########################################################################

@deck act3
Title: The Third Act: Reasoning
Small heading: THE THIRD ACT

Moving from what is known to what is not yet known: the syllogism in the broad and narrow senses; its three terms and two premises; the middle term and the cause; valid, sound, and demonstrative arguments; induction; and what the exercises of the Third Act take up.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Timothy Kearns, “Outline and Synopsis of Accounts of Logic in the Western Tradition”; John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* and *Posterior Analytics*; Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**What reasoning is.** Reasoning is the third act of the mind, by which we move from what we already know to what we do not yet know. John of St. Thomas, a seventeenth-century Dominican commentator on St. Thomas, defines argumentation as a “statement in which knowledge follows from something already known” (*Summulae*, bk. II, ch. 5). Put another way, reasoning is a discourse in which, once certain things have been granted (the **premises**), something else must of necessity be granted (the **conclusion**).

For example, from “no C is A” and “some B is A,” we see, simply from what these propositions mean, that some B is not C. The movement of the mind from the premises to the conclusion is the **inference**; its expression in words is the argument.

@question 1.1
Question: What is reasoning?
Choices:
- Moving from what is already known to what is not yet known
- Grasping what a thing is
- Affirming one thing of another
- Repeating in order what has already been learned from a teacher
Answer: Moving from what is already known to what is not yet known
Why: Reasoning, the third act of the mind, draws knowledge from something already known.
Why not the others: Grasping what a thing is belongs to the first act, and affirming to the second; repetition draws nothing new.

@question 1.2
Question: In reasoning, once the premises are granted, the conclusion…
Choices:
- must of necessity be granted
- may be granted or not, as we please
- must be proved by a new experiment
- is always false
Answer: must of necessity be granted
Why: That is what it is for a conclusion to follow from premises.
Why not the others: A conclusion that need not be granted does not follow, and no new experiment is needed once the premises are granted.

@question 1.3
Question: From “No C is A” and “Some B is A,” what follows?
Choices:
- “Some B is not C”
- “All B is C”
- “No B is A”
- “Some C is B”
Answer: “Some B is not C”
Why: Some B is A, and nothing that is C is A; so those B cannot be C.
Why not the others: The premises give no affirmative link between B and C, and they say that some B is A, not that none is.

@card 2

**The syllogism, broad and narrow.** In Greek, and in the languages that took the word from Greek, reasoning of this kind is called a *syllogism*; originally the word meant what “reasoning” means in English. In ordinary use it has come to mean a set form of argument, the syllogism in the narrow sense: two premises and a conclusion, relating three terms.

Why three terms? In the second act we join two terms in one proposition, and two terms alone give a single proposition, not a chain of reasoning. The simplest reasoning takes three terms related across two propositions, and from these alone it draws a third proposition that must be true if the first two are. “Every man is mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal” is the standard example.

@question 2.1
Question: In its original, broad sense, what did the word “syllogism” mean?
Choices:
- Reasoning in general
- Only the first figure
- A speech in a law court
- A definition
Answer: Reasoning in general
Why: The Greek word meant what “reasoning” means in English; only later did it come to mean a set form of argument.
Why not the others: The narrow sense is a set form with three terms, and a speech or a definition is not reasoning as such.

@question 2.2
Question: How many terms does a categorical syllogism have?
Choices:
- Three
- Two
- Four
- Six
Answer: Three
Why: The major, the minor, and the middle term.
Why not the others: Two terms give only one proposition, and a fourth term would break the link between the premises.

@question 2.3
Question: Why can two terms alone give no reasoning?
Choices:
- Because two terms make only one proposition, and reasoning relates two terms through a third
- Because two is an even number
- Because the copula needs three words
- Because every proposition has three terms
Answer: Because two terms make only one proposition, and reasoning relates two terms through a third
Why: With two terms we can form a single judgment; to infer, we need a third term related to both.
Why not the others: The number is not a matter of arithmetic, and every simple proposition has two terms, not three.

@card 3

**Three terms and two premises.** The conclusion has a subject and a predicate. The predicate of the conclusion is the **major term**, and its subject is the **minor term**. The third term appears in both premises but never in the conclusion; it is the **middle term**, so called because it mediates between the other two. The premise that relates the middle term to the major term is the **major premise**, and the premise that relates it to the minor term is the **minor premise**.

In “Every man is mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal,” the major term is “mortal,” the minor term is “Socrates,” and the middle term is “man.” The different positions of the middle term in the two premises are called the *figures* of the syllogism.

@question 3.1
Question: In “Every man is mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal,” which is the major term?
Choices:
- “Mortal”
- “Socrates”
- “Man”
- “Every”
Answer: “Mortal”
Why: The major term is the predicate of the conclusion.
Why not the others: “Socrates” is the minor term (the subject of the conclusion), “man” the middle term, and “every” the sign of quantity.

@question 3.2
Question: In the same syllogism, which is the middle term?
Choices:
- “Man”
- “Mortal”
- “Socrates”
- “Is”
Answer: “Man”
Why: “Man” appears in both premises and never in the conclusion.
Why not the others: “Mortal” and “Socrates” are the extremes, and “is” is the copula.

@question 3.3
Question: Which premise is the major premise?
Choices:
- The one that relates the middle term to the predicate of the conclusion
- Whichever premise is longer
- Whichever premise is stated second
- The one that relates the middle term to the subject of the conclusion itself
Answer: The one that relates the middle term to the predicate of the conclusion
Why: The major premise contains the major term, the predicate of the conclusion.
Why not the others: The last answer describes the minor premise; length and order of statement do not decide it.

@card 4

**The middle term and the cause.** The middle term is the term that allows us to state the relation between the other two. In a demonstration it is essential, because it can reveal the cause of the relation between the two terms of the conclusion. For example, in “Whatever subsists without matter is incorruptible; the human soul subsists without matter; therefore the human soul is incorruptible,” the middle term, “subsisting without matter,” gives the reason why the soul is incorruptible.

Since demonstration chiefly concerns the cause, negative conclusions are of limited use in it, because a negative conclusion can at best show what is not the cause.

@question 4.1
Question: Why is the middle term so important in a demonstration?
Choices:
- Because it can reveal the cause of the relation between the terms of the conclusion
- Because it is always the longest term
- Because it appears in the conclusion
- Because it must be a proper name
Answer: Because it can reveal the cause of the relation between the terms of the conclusion
Why: The middle term gives the reason why the predicate belongs to the subject, and that reason is the cause.
Why not the others: The middle term never appears in the conclusion, and its length and kind do not matter.

@question 4.2
Question: “Whatever subsists without matter is incorruptible; the human soul subsists without matter; therefore the human soul is incorruptible.” What is the middle term?
Choices:
- “Subsisting without matter”
- “Incorruptible”
- “The human soul”
- “Whatever”
Answer: “Subsisting without matter”
Why: It appears in both premises and gives the reason why the soul is incorruptible.
Why not the others: “Incorruptible” is the major term, “the human soul” the minor term, and “whatever” the sign of quantity.

@question 4.3
Question: Does the middle term appear in the conclusion?
Choices:
- No; its office is to join the other two terms in the premises
- Yes; it is the subject of the conclusion
- Yes; it is the predicate of the conclusion, as the major term is
- Only in the fourth figure
Answer: No; its office is to join the other two terms in the premises
Why: The conclusion joins the major and minor terms; the middle term has done its work in the premises.
Why not the others: The subject and predicate of the conclusion are the minor and major terms, in every figure.

@card 5

**Valid, sound, and demonstrative.** An argument is **valid** when it is impossible for its premises to be true and its conclusion false; validity depends on form alone. An argument is **sound** when it is valid and its premises are also true. A **demonstration** requires still more: its premises must be true, necessary, and better known than the conclusion, and in the strictest sense they must express the cause of the conclusion.

So every demonstration is sound, and every sound argument is valid, but the reverse does not hold. For example, “No man can do evil; this criminal is a man; therefore this criminal can do no evil” is valid, but it is not sound, because its first premise is false.

@question 5.1
Question: An argument has a valid form and a false premise. It is…
Choices:
- valid but not sound
- sound but not valid
- both valid and sound
- a demonstration
Answer: valid but not sound
Why: Validity depends on form alone; soundness requires true premises as well.
Why not the others: A false premise rules out soundness, and so it rules out demonstration too.

@question 5.2
Question: What does a demonstration require beyond a sound argument?
Choices:
- Premises that are necessary, better known than the conclusion, and causal
- A longer chain of premises, each of them probable, leading up to the conclusion
- An audience that agrees
- Nothing further
Answer: Premises that are necessary, better known than the conclusion, and causal
Why: Demonstration proceeds from true and necessary premises that give the cause of the conclusion.
Why not the others: Length and agreement are not conditions of demonstration, and soundness alone is not enough.

@question 5.3
Question: True or false: every valid argument is sound.
Choices:
- True
- False
Answer: False
Why: A valid argument may have false premises; then it is valid but not sound.
Why not the others: Validity concerns form only, so a valid argument can still have a false premise.

@card 6

**Induction and demonstration.** John of St. Thomas, following St. Thomas, names two ways of acquiring science: demonstration, which is a syllogism and proceeds from universals, and induction, which proceeds from singulars (*Summulae*, bk. III, ch. 2). There are only two ways of making a truth manifest: from principles known by the intellect, or from the singular things given in experience.

**Induction** is the movement from sufficiently enumerated singulars to a universal: this fire warms, and this one, and this one; therefore every fire warms. The *example*, which argues from one case to another like it, is an incomplete induction. A proof by induction shows *that* something is so (a demonstration of the fact), not *why* it is so; a syllogism whose middle term gives the cause can show why.

@question 6.1
Question: What is induction?
Choices:
- The movement from sufficiently enumerated singulars to a universal
- The movement from a universal to the particular cause that explains it
- An argument with a premise left unsaid
- A definition by genus and difference
Answer: The movement from sufficiently enumerated singulars to a universal
Why: Induction rises from singular cases to the universal they share.
Why not the others: The enthymeme leaves a premise unsaid, and definition is a work of the first act.

@question 6.2
Question: “This fire warms, and this one, and this one; therefore every fire warms.” What kind of argument is it?
Choices:
- Induction
- A syllogism in the first figure
- A conditional syllogism
- A fallacy of equivocation
Answer: Induction
Why: It proceeds from singular cases to a universal conclusion.
Why not the others: It has no middle term linking two premises, no “if,” and no word used in two senses.

@question 6.3
Question: What does a proof by induction show?
Choices:
- *That* something is so, though not yet *why*
- *Why* something is so, through its proper cause
- Only what is probable
- Nothing at all
Answer: *That* something is so, though not yet *why*
Why: An induction is a demonstration of the fact; a syllogism whose middle term gives the cause can show why.
Why not the others: Knowing why requires the cause as middle term; a sufficient induction gives more than probability.

@card 7

**What follows in the Third Act.** The exercises of the Third Act begin with the categorical syllogism: drawing it on three circles (Exercise VIII), judging its validity in letters and in English (IX and X), and drawing the conclusion from two premises (XI). They then take up syllogisms built on compound propositions: the conjunctive (“not both”) and the disjunctive (“either … or”) in XII, the conditional (“if … then”) in XIII, The Hypothetical Syllogism, and all of them together (The Mixed Consequence) in XIV. Exercise XV finds the syllogism in ordinary speech, XVI treats the modal syllogism, and XVII the chain of syllogisms, in which the conclusion of one syllogism becomes a premise of the next.

The later sections apply the third act to its uses. *Ad Demonstrationem* (XVIII) weighs an argument in its form and its matter together; *Dialectica* (XX) treats reasoning from what is probable; *Rhetorica* (XIX) treats the enthymeme, an argument with one premise left unsaid; and *Sophistica* (XXI) treats the fallacies, arguments that seem sound and are not.

@question 7.1
Question: What is a chain of syllogisms?
Choices:
- A series in which the conclusion of one syllogism becomes a premise of the next
- A syllogism with four terms
- An argument with a premise left unsaid
- A list of fallacies
Answer: A series in which the conclusion of one syllogism becomes a premise of the next
Why: Each link concludes something that the next link uses as a premise.
