Why not the others: A syllogism with four terms is faulty, and an argument with a premise left unsaid is an enthymeme.

@question 7.2
Question: What is an enthymeme?
Choices:
- An argument with one premise left unsaid
- An argument from two contingent premises
- A chain of syllogisms
- A definition by causes
Answer: An argument with one premise left unsaid
Why: The orator often leaves a premise for the hearer to supply.
Why not the others: The other answers describe other things, or nothing the course treats under that name.

@question 7.3
Question: What is a fallacy?
Choices:
- An argument that seems sound and is not
- Any argument with a false conclusion
- An argument from probable premises
- An argument with one premise left unsaid
Answer: An argument that seems sound and is not
Why: A fallacy has an appearance of soundness that hides its failure.
Why not the others: A valid argument with a false premise can have a false conclusion without being a fallacy; probable premises belong to dialectic; and the unsaid premise to the enthymeme.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Diagramming Syllogisms
########################################################################

@deck leadin-8
Title: Before the Exercise · Diagramming Syllogisms
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Three circles for three terms.** Each item gives a valid syllogism, its two premises and its conclusion, to be drawn on three circles. The circles stand for the three terms: the subject of the conclusion (the minor term), the predicate of the conclusion (the major term), and the middle term, which appears in both premises. Each premise relates the middle term to one of the others, so each premise is drawn on the two circles it names.

The universal premises are shaded first, and each empties two cells, since the third circle cuts its region in two. Then each particular premise’s × goes in the one cell of its region that the shading leaves open. The conclusion is not drawn separately; once the premises are drawn, the conclusion can be read from the diagram.

@question 1.1
Question: Where does the × of a particular premise go?
Choices:
- In the one cell of its region that the shading leaves open
- In the middle of the diagram
- On the line between two cells
- In every cell of its region
Answer: In the one cell of its region that the shading leaves open
Why: The universals are shaded first, and then the particular’s region has only one open cell.
Why not the others: A mark on a line or in several cells says less than the premises say, and the center is not always open.

@question 1.2
Question: Is the conclusion drawn as a separate mark?
Choices:
- No; once the premises are drawn, it can be read from the diagram
- Yes, with its own shading and its own ×, drawn on the same circles
- Yes, with its own ×
- Yes, with a ⊗
Answer: No; once the premises are drawn, it can be read from the diagram
Why: In a valid syllogism the conclusion is already contained in the premises, and so in their diagram.
Why not the others: Shading or marking the conclusion separately would add what the premises have not said.

@card 2

**The ⊗.** Sometimes the conclusion is particular although neither premise is, as in Darapti: “All M are P; all M are S; therefore some S are P.” Then the shading alone does not show that anything exists. The existence comes from an affirmative premise, since an affirmative asserts that its subject exists, and it is drawn with a ⊗ in the only unshaded cell of the term whose existence the conclusion needs (in Darapti, the term M).

If a particular premise already supplies the ×, no ⊗ is drawn, and a ⊗ never takes the place of a particular premise’s ×. In two moods, Camestros and Camenos, the term concerned appears only in a negative premise. The ⊗ is still drawn there, because the diagram writes the particular negative conclusion with an ×, and so it must suppose that something falls under that term.

@question 2.1
Question: In Darapti (“All M are P; all M are S; so some S are P”), why is a ⊗ needed?
Choices:
- The conclusion is particular, no premise is, and the existence comes from an affirmative premise
- Because the premises are negative
- Because the middle term is undistributed
- It is not needed
Answer: The conclusion is particular, no premise is, and the existence comes from an affirmative premise
Why: “All M are …” asserts that some M exists, and the ⊗ goes in the only unshaded cell of M.
Why not the others: Darapti’s premises are affirmative and valid; without the ⊗ the diagram would not show “Some S are P.”

@question 2.2
Question: A syllogism has a particular premise, and its × already shows the conclusion. Is a ⊗ drawn?
Choices:
- No
- Yes, beside the ×
- Yes, in place of the ×
- Yes, in every open cell
Answer: No
Why: The × of the particular premise already supplies the existence, so nothing more is drawn.
Why not the others: A ⊗ never replaces or doubles a particular premise’s ×.

@card 3

**Which moods take a ⊗.** Nine valid moods need the ⊗: the five weakened moods, which conclude in particular what could be concluded universally (Barbari, Celaront, Cesaro, Camestros, and Camenos), and Darapti, Felapton, Bramantip, and Fesapo. On the modern reading, which gives no universal proposition existential import, all nine are invalid. This course follows the medieval reading, on which all nine are valid. So the ⊗ appears in these nine moods, in the one open cell of the term concerned, and in no others. Venn’s own diagrams have no mark for import, which is why these nine moods fail in Boolean logic.

@question 3.1
Question: On the modern reading, is Darapti valid?
Choices:
- No, because it gives no universal proposition existential import
- Yes, on every reading, since each such mood is valid in its own right
- Only in the fourth figure
- Only with a negative premise
Answer: No, because it gives no universal proposition existential import
Why: Without existential import the premises do not guarantee that anything is S and P. The course follows the medieval reading, on which Darapti is valid.
Why not the others: The modern reading rejects all nine moods that need the ⊗.

@question 3.2
Question: How many valid moods need a ⊗?
Choices:
- Nine
- Five
- Nineteen
- None
Answer: Nine
Why: The five weakened moods and Darapti, Felapton, Bramantip, and Fesapo.
Why not the others: Five counts only the weakened moods.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Syllogistic Form in the Abstract
########################################################################

@deck leadin-9
Title: Before the Exercise · Syllogistic Form in the Abstract
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Terms and figure.** Each item gives a syllogism in letters, and the question is whether its form is valid. The major premise is written first, then the minor premise, then the conclusion. The predicate of the conclusion is the **major term**, its subject is the **minor term**, and the **middle term** appears in both premises and never in the conclusion.

Capital letters stand for general terms and lower-case letters for singular terms, that is, single individuals. A singular proposition such as “s is P” speaks of the whole of its subject, so it counts as universal and its subject is distributed. The figure is fixed by the position of the middle term: in the first figure, M–P and S–M; in the second, P–M and S–M; in the third, M–P and M–S; in the fourth, P–M and M–S. The feedback names the mood and figure (for example, Barbara, AAA-1), but validity can be judged from the rules alone, without memorizing the moods.

@question 1.1
Question: In “All M are P; all S are M; so all S are P,” which is the major term?
Choices:
- P
- S
- M
- None of them
Answer: P
Why: The major term is the predicate of the conclusion.
Why not the others: S is the minor term and M the middle term.

@question 1.2
Question: What does a lower-case letter stand for?
Choices:
- A singular term, one individual
- A general term
- The middle term
- A negative term
Answer: A singular term, one individual
Why: Capitals are general terms, and lower-case letters are singular.
Why not the others: Case marks singular and general, not the role of the term or its quality.

@card 2

**Distribution.** A term is **distributed** when the proposition speaks of everything the term stands for. A distributes its subject only (“All S are P” speaks of every S, but not of every P). E distributes both terms, since it excludes every S from every P. I distributes neither term. O distributes its predicate only, since “Some S are not P” excludes those S from the whole of P. So the predicate of an affirmative is never distributed, and the predicate of a negative always is.

@question 2.1
Question: Which terms does “No S are P” distribute?
Choices:
- Both S and P
- S only
- P only
- Neither
Answer: Both S and P
Why: E excludes every S from every P, so it speaks of the whole of both terms.
Why not the others: A distributes only its subject, O only its predicate, and I neither.

@question 2.2
Question: Which terms does “Some S are not P” distribute?
Choices:
- P only
- S only
- Both
- Neither
Answer: P only
Why: O speaks of only some S, but it excludes them from the whole of P.
Why not the others: Its subject is taken only in part.

@card 3

**The general rules.** A syllogism is valid only if it keeps these rules. (1) The middle term must be distributed at least once; otherwise the fallacy is the *undistributed middle*. (2) A term distributed in the conclusion must be distributed in its premise; otherwise it is an *illicit major* or an *illicit minor*. (3) From two negative premises nothing follows. (4) A negative premise requires a negative conclusion. (5) A negative conclusion requires a negative premise. (6) From two particular premises nothing follows.

A form that keeps all six rules is valid, and a form that breaks any one is invalid. From these it also follows that a particular premise allows only a particular conclusion. On the traditional reading, a weakened conclusion, such as “Some S are P” where “All S are P” could be drawn, is also valid, because affirmatives carry existential import; and a valid syllogism in the fourth figure breaks no rule, though it reverses the natural order of predication. The weakened moods stand or fall with subalternation on the square.

@question 3.1
Question: “All P are M; all S are M; so all S are P.” Which rule is broken?
Choices:
- The middle term is not distributed
- Two negative premises
- Two particular premises
- A negative premise with an affirmative conclusion
Answer: The middle term is not distributed
Why: M is the predicate of two affirmatives, so it is never distributed.
Why not the others: Both premises are affirmative and universal.

@question 3.2
Question: “No M are P; no S are M; so no S are P.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- No; from two negative premises nothing follows
- Yes; it is Celarent
- Yes; both premises are universal, so a universal conclusion follows
- No; the middle is undistributed
Answer: No; from two negative premises nothing follows
Why: Two denials leave the extremes unconnected.
Why not the others: Celarent has an affirmative minor premise, and the middle here is distributed.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Syllogistic Form in the Concrete
########################################################################

@deck leadin-10
Title: Before the Exercise · Syllogistic Form in the Concrete
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Judging the form, not the facts.** Here the syllogisms use English terms, and the question is still whether the form is valid. True premises and a true conclusion do not make an argument valid, and false or absurd premises do not make it invalid. The surest method is to replace each term by a letter and judge the form as in Exercise IX.

A proper name such as “Socrates” is a singular term, and a singular proposition counts as universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject. Wording in the plural or the singular (“dogs,” “a dog,” “every dog”) does not change the term.

@question 1.1
Question: “All fish are mammals; all whales are fish; so all whales are mammals.” Is the form valid?
Choices:
- Valid, though a premise is false
- Invalid, because a premise is false
- Invalid, because the conclusion is true
- Neither valid nor invalid
Answer: Valid, though a premise is false
Why: The form is Barbara. Validity concerns form alone, so a false premise does not make it invalid.
Why not the others: Truth and falsity of premises and conclusion do not decide validity.

@question 1.2
Question: How is “Socrates is a philosopher” treated in a syllogism?
Choices:
- As universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject
- As particular
- As negative
- As a question
Answer: As universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject
Why: A singular proposition counts as universal.
Why not the others: It speaks of one individual, but of the whole of that individual.

@card 2

**The rules, in brief.** A distributes its subject, E both terms, I neither, and O its predicate. The middle term must be distributed at least once; no term may be distributed in the conclusion unless it is distributed in its premise; nothing follows from two negative premises or from two particular premises; a negative premise requires a negative conclusion, and a negative conclusion requires a negative premise.

@question 2.1
Question: “Some philosophers are Greeks; Socrates is a philosopher; so Socrates is a Greek.” Is the form valid?
Choices:
- Invalid; the middle term is not distributed
- Valid; it is Darii
- Valid; the conclusion is true
- Invalid; it has two negative premises and so concludes nothing
Answer: Invalid; the middle term is not distributed
Why: “Philosopher” is taken in part in both premises, so it may refer to different philosophers.
Why not the others: A true conclusion does not make a form valid, and there is no negative premise here.

@question 2.2
Question: Which term does an A proposition distribute?
Choices:
- Its subject only
- Its predicate only
- Both
- Neither
Answer: Its subject only
Why: “All S are P” speaks of every S, but not of every P.
Why not the others: E distributes both, O its predicate, I neither.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Drawing the Conclusion
########################################################################

@deck leadin-11
Title: Before the Exercise · Drawing the Conclusion
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Finding the conclusion.** Two premises are given, and the task is to type the conclusion that follows, or “none.” First find the middle term, the term in both premises: the conclusion joins the other two terms and never contains the middle. Then fix the quality: if either premise is negative, the conclusion is negative; if both are affirmative, it is affirmative. Then fix the quantity: if either premise is particular, the conclusion is particular. Otherwise it may be universal, but a term may be distributed in the conclusion only if it is distributed in its premise, so sometimes only a particular conclusion follows.

Either remaining term may be the subject of the conclusion, provided the conclusion keeps the rules; whichever term is its predicate is then the major term. Where a universal conclusion follows, the particular under it also follows and is accepted. The answer is typed in standard form: “All S are P,” “No S are P,” “Some S are P,” or “Some S are not P.”

@question 1.1
Question: “All M are P; some S are M.” What follows?
Choices:
- “Some S are P”
- “All S are P”
- “Some M are S”
- Nothing
Answer: “Some S are P”
Why: The middle term drops out, the particular minor premise makes the conclusion particular, and both premises are affirmative.
Why not the others: “All S are P” overstates the quantity, and the middle term never appears in the conclusion.

@question 1.2
Question: Can the middle term appear in the conclusion?
Choices:
- No
- Yes, as subject
- Yes, as predicate
- Only in a particular conclusion
Answer: No
Why: Its whole office is to join the other two terms in the premises.
Why not the others: The conclusion joins the minor and major terms only.

@card 2

**When nothing follows.** Some pairs of premises yield no conclusion in any arrangement. Nothing follows from two negative premises, from two particular premises, or when the middle term is distributed in neither premise. Nothing follows either when every possible conclusion would distribute a term that its premise leaves undistributed. In these cases the answer is “none.”

@question 2.1
Question: “No M are P; no S are M.” What follows?
Choices:
- Nothing follows
- “No S are P”
- “Some S are not P”
- “All S are P”
Answer: Nothing follows
Why: From two negative premises nothing follows.
Why not the others: Two denials give no link between S and P.

@question 2.2
Question: “Some M are P; some S are M.” What follows?
Choices:
- Nothing, since both premises are particular
- “Some S are P”
- “All S are P”
- “Some S are not P”
Answer: Nothing, since both premises are particular
Why: Each “some” may pick out a different part of M.
Why not the others: Two particular premises yield nothing.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Conjunctive and Disjunctive Syllogism
########################################################################

@deck leadin-12
Title: Before the Exercise · The Conjunctive and Disjunctive Syllogism
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**Arguments from a compound premise.** The conjunctive and the disjunctive syllogism are built not on terms but on whole propositions joined by a connective. The major premise is compound; the minor premise posits one member (asserts it) or removes it (denies it); and the conclusion posits or removes the other member. Each question asks whether the conclusion follows.

The **conjunctive** premise says “not both”: it denies that two propositions are true together, as in “No one can serve both God and Mammon.” Positing one member removes the other: “Judas serves Mammon; therefore he does not serve God.” This is the mood that denies by affirming (*modus ponendo tollens*), and it is the only lawful mood of the conjunctive. Removing one member posits nothing, because both members may be false together; so from “He does not serve Mammon” it does not follow that he serves God. To argue so is the fallacy of the mood that affirms by denying (*modus tollendo ponens*).

The **disjunctive** premise says “either … or,” and it asserts that at least one member is true. So removing one member posits the other: “The number is either odd or even; it is not odd; therefore it is even.” The mood that affirms by denying is therefore the proper mood of the disjunctive, and it holds for every disjunction.

@question 1.1
Question: “Socrates is not both a B and a C. Socrates is a B. So Socrates is not a C.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Valid, since positing one member of a conjunctive removes the other
- Invalid, since nothing follows from a conjunctive
- Invalid, since in a conjunctive the minor premise must always remove a member
- Valid, by the removing-positing mood
Answer: Valid, since positing one member of a conjunctive removes the other
Why: This is the positing-removing mood, the one lawful mood of the conjunctive.
Why not the others: The conjunctive does have a lawful mood, and here the minor posits rather than removes.

@question 1.2
Question: “Socrates is not both a B and a C. Socrates is not a B. So Socrates is a C.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, since both members may be false together
- Valid, since one of the two must be true
- Valid, by the positing-removing mood
- Invalid, since the conjunctive has no lawful mood
Answer: Invalid, since both members may be false together
Why: “Not both” asserts neither member, so removing one posits nothing.
Why not the others: Reading “not both” as “one or the other” is the usual error; the conjunctive does have a lawful mood, but it posits in order to remove.

@question 1.3
Question: “The number is either odd or even. It is not odd. So it is even.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Valid, since removing one member of a disjunction posits the other
- Invalid, since the disjunction may be broad
- Invalid, since in a disjunctive the minor premise must always posit a member
- Valid only if the disjunction is strict
Answer: Valid, since removing one member of a disjunction posits the other
Why: Every disjunction asserts that at least one member is true, so if one is removed the other stands.
Why not the others: The strict or broad kind matters only when a member is posited; removing-positing holds for both.

@card 2

**Strict and broad disjunction.** Whether positing one member of a disjunction removes the other depends on the kind of disjunction, and the premise marks the kind. A **strict** disjunction, marked “but not both,” says that exactly one member is true; so positing one removes the other, and both moods are lawful. A **broad** disjunction, marked “or perhaps both,” says only that at least one member is true; so from “He is either clever or lucky, or perhaps both” and “He is clever,” nothing follows about his luck. The marker must therefore be read before the conclusion is judged.

The members may concern one subject, as in “Socrates is not both a B and a C,” and then positing and removing are said of that subject. Or each member may be a whole categorical proposition, such as “All B are C.” To remove such a member is to assert its contradictory: the contradictory of A is O, of O is A, of E is I, and of I is E. So “Some B are not C” removes “All B are C,” and “No D are F” removes “Some D are F.” Whatever the members are, the mood alone decides whether the conclusion follows.

@question 2.1
Question: “Socrates is either a B or a C, or perhaps both. Socrates is a B. So Socrates is not a C.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, since a broad disjunction allows both members together
- Valid, since positing one member removes the other
- Valid, since the members are opposed
- Invalid, since nothing ever follows from a disjunction of two members
Answer: Invalid, since a broad disjunction allows both members together
Why: “Or perhaps both” marks a broad disjunction, so positing one member removes nothing.
Why not the others: Only a strict disjunction (“but not both”) lets the positing of one member remove the other; and removing one member of any disjunction does posit the other.

@question 2.2
Question: “Socrates is either a B or a C, but not both. Socrates is a B. So Socrates is not a C.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Valid, since a strict disjunction allows only one member to be true
- Invalid, since positing one member never removes the other
- Invalid, since the minor premise must remove a member
- Valid, since every disjunction is strict
Answer: Valid, since a strict disjunction allows only one member to be true
Why: “But not both” marks a strict disjunction, so both moods are lawful.
Why not the others: A broad disjunction would not allow this conclusion, so not every disjunction is strict.

@question 2.3
Question: To remove the member “All B are C” is to assert which proposition?
Choices:
- “Some B are not C”
- “No B are C”
- “Some B are C”
- “All C are B”
Answer: “Some B are not C”
Why: To remove a proposition is to assert its contradictory, and the contradictory of A is O.
Why not the others: “No B are C” is the contrary, which says more than the denial; “Some B are C” is the subaltern, and “All C are B” the converse.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Hypothetical Syllogism
########################################################################

@deck leadin-13
Title: Before the Exercise · The Hypothetical Syllogism
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**The conditional and its two lawful moods.** A conditional proposition asserts a connection, not a fact: “If it has rained, the ground is wet.” The clause after “if” is the **antecedent**, and the clause it supports is the **consequent**. A conditional is true when the consequent really follows from the antecedent, even if neither is true in fact. Each question gives a conditional premise, a second premise, and a conclusion, and asks whether the conclusion follows.

Two moods are lawful. In the mood that affirms by affirming (*modus ponendo ponens*), the second premise posits the antecedent, and the consequent follows: “It has rained; therefore the ground is wet.” In the mood that denies by denying (*modus tollendo tollens*), the second premise removes the consequent, and the antecedent is removed with it: “The ground is not wet; therefore it has not rained.” The second mood holds because the antecedent cannot be true without the consequent.

@question 1.1
Question: “If Socrates is a B, then Socrates is a C. Socrates is a B. So Socrates is a C.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Valid, by the mood that affirms by affirming
- Invalid, by affirming the consequent
- Invalid, by denying the antecedent
- Valid, by the mood that denies by denying
Answer: Valid, by the mood that affirms by affirming
Why: The second premise posits the antecedent, so the consequent follows.
Why not the others: Nothing is removed here, and it is the antecedent, not the consequent, that is posited.

@question 1.2
Question: “If Socrates is a B, then Socrates is a C. Socrates is not a C. So Socrates is not a B.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Valid, by the mood that denies by denying
- Invalid, by denying the antecedent
- Invalid, by affirming the consequent
- Valid, by the mood that affirms by affirming
Answer: Valid, by the mood that denies by denying
Why: The antecedent cannot be true without the consequent, so removing the consequent removes the antecedent.
Why not the others: The second premise removes the consequent, not the antecedent, so this is no fallacy.

@question 1.3
Question: In “If it has rained, the ground is wet,” which clause is the antecedent?
Choices:
- “it has rained”
- “the ground is wet”
- The whole conditional
- Neither, since a conditional asserts no fact
Answer: “it has rained”
Why: The antecedent is the clause after “if”; the consequent is the clause it supports.
Why not the others: “The ground is wet” is the consequent; a conditional asserts a connection, but its parts still have names.

@card 2

**The two fallacies.** Two arguments imitate these moods and fail. **Affirming the consequent** reasons, “If it rained, the ground is wet; the ground is wet; so it rained.” It fails because the consequent may be true on other grounds, as a burst pipe would also wet the ground. **Denying the antecedent** reasons, “If it rained, the ground is wet; it did not rain; so the ground is not wet,” and it fails for the same reason, since the consequent was never made to depend on the antecedent alone. A conditional asserts the connection in one direction only, from antecedent to consequent; it does not assert the reverse, even when the reverse happens to be true.

The antecedent or the consequent may itself be negative, as in “If Socrates is not a B, then Socrates is a C.” To posit a negative antecedent is to assert it as it stands (“Socrates is not a B”), and to remove it is to assert the affirmative (“Socrates is a B”). The components may also be whole categorical propositions, and then to remove one is to assert its contradictory, as “Some B are not C” removes “All B are C.” In every case the mood decides whether the conclusion follows, whatever the components are.

@question 2.1
Question: “If it rained, the ground is wet. The ground is wet. So it rained.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, by affirming the consequent
- Valid, by the positing mood
- Invalid, by denying the antecedent
- Valid, by the removing mood
Answer: Invalid, by affirming the consequent
Why: The ground may be wet on other grounds, so positing the consequent does not posit the antecedent.
Why not the others: The positing mood posits the antecedent, not the consequent; and nothing is denied here.

@question 2.2
Question: “If it rained, the ground is wet. It did not rain. So the ground is not wet.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, by denying the antecedent
- Valid, by the removing mood
- Invalid, by affirming the consequent
- Valid, by the positing mood
Answer: Invalid, by denying the antecedent
Why: The consequent was never made to depend on the antecedent alone, so removing the antecedent does not remove it.
Why not the others: The removing mood removes the consequent, not the antecedent.

@question 2.3
Question: “If Socrates is not a B, then Socrates is a C. Socrates is not a C.” What follows?
Choices:
- Socrates is a B
- Socrates is not a B
- Socrates is a C
- Nothing follows
Answer: Socrates is a B
Why: Removing the consequent removes the antecedent, and to remove “Socrates is not a B” is to assert “Socrates is a B.”
Why not the others: The antecedent is negative, so removing it gives the affirmative; and this is the removing mood, so something does follow.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Mixed Consequence
########################################################################

@deck leadin-14
Title: Before the Exercise · The Mixed Consequence
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955).

**The lawful moods.** Each item joins a compound premise to a simple one, and the task is to type what follows, or “none.” For the conditional (“if A, then B”), positing the antecedent posits the consequent, in the mood that affirms by affirming (*modus ponendo ponens*), and removing the consequent removes the antecedent, in the mood that denies by denying (*modus tollendo tollens*); affirming the consequent and denying the antecedent conclude nothing. For the conjunctive (“not both A and B”), positing one member removes the other, in the mood that denies by affirming (*modus ponendo tollens*), but removing one posits nothing.

For the disjunctive (“either A or B”), removing one member posits the other, in the mood that affirms by denying (*modus tollendo ponens*). Positing one member removes the other only when the disjunction is strict (“but not both”); when it is broad (“or perhaps both”), positing one member removes nothing.

@question 1.1
Question: “Either Socrates is a B, or he is a C, or perhaps both; Socrates is a B.” What follows?
Choices:
- Nothing
- Socrates is not a C
- Socrates is a C
- Socrates is not a B
Answer: Nothing
Why: A broad disjunction allows both members together, so positing one removes nothing.
Why not the others: Only a strict disjunction (“but not both”) lets us remove the other member.

@question 1.2
Question: “If Socrates is a B, then Socrates is a C; Socrates is not a C.” What follows?
Choices:
- Socrates is not a B
- Socrates is a B
- Nothing
- Socrates is a C
Answer: Socrates is not a B
Why: Removing the consequent removes the antecedent, in the mood that denies by denying.
Why not the others: The premises deny that Socrates is a C, so nothing can make him one; and something does follow.

@card 2

**Writing the answer.** When the premises concern one subject, as in “If Socrates is a B, then Socrates is a C,” the answer is written about that subject: “Socrates is a C” or “Socrates is not a C.” Removing a negative component asserts the positive one: if the premise begins “If Socrates is not a B,” then removing that antecedent gives “Socrates is a B.”

From Level III, the components may be whole categorical propositions, such as “All B are C.” To deny such a component is to assert its contradictory: the contradictory of A is O, of O is A, of E is I, and of I is E. So if the conclusion removes “All B are C,” the answer is “Some B are not C.” Where the mood is fallacious, the answer is “none.”

@question 2.1
Question: “Either all B are C, or some D are F, or perhaps both; no D are F.” What follows?
Choices:
- “All B are C”
- “Some B are not C”
- “No B are C”
- Nothing
Answer: “All B are C”
Why: “No D are F” is the contradictory of “Some D are F,” so it removes the second member, and the first is posited.
Why not the others: Removing one member of a disjunction posits the other; it does not deny it.

@question 2.2
Question: What is the contradictory of “Some B are C”?
Choices:
- “No B are C”
- “Some B are not C”
- “All B are C”
- “All C are B”
Answer: “No B are C”
Why: The contradictory of I is E.
Why not the others: “Some B are not C” is the subcontrary; “All B are C” is the subaltern.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Arguments in Ordinary Speech
########################################################################

@deck leadin-15
Title: Before the Exercise · Arguments in Ordinary Speech
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**Finding the argument in the prose.** Each question states an argument in ordinary English, which may be categorical, conditional, conjunctive, disjunctive, or modal, and asks whether it is a valid syllogism. The first step is to find the conclusion. Words such as “therefore,” “so,” “hence,” and “it follows that” mark the conclusion; words such as “since,” “because,” “for,” and “after all” mark a premise. The conclusion often comes first, as in “No oaks are shrubs, since every oak is a tree and no tree is a shrub.”

The next step is to restate each premise in standard form. “Every S is a P,” “Whatever is an S is a P,” “If something is an S, then it is a P,” and “S are always P” are A. “S are never P,” “Not a single S is a P,” and “There is no S that is a P” are E. “There are S that are P,” “Certain S are P,” and “A few S are P” are I, but “Few S are P” chiefly denies and is read as “Some S are not P.” “Not all S are P” and “Not every S is a P” are O, since they deny the universal without asserting “none.” The English “All S are not P” may mean “No S are P” or only “Some S are not P” (“All that glitters is not gold” means the second), so it should be read twice. “Only P are S” means “All S are P,” so the term after “only” becomes the predicate, and “none but” and “no one except” work in the same way.

When no sign of quantity appears, the sense decides: “Dogs are mammals” means all dogs, but “Dogs were barking in the alley” means some. A verb other than the copula is restated with it, so “All dogs bark” becomes “All dogs are things that bark.” A singular subject, as in “Socrates is a philosopher,” is taken as universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject. A term stated in the singular or in the plural, or in other words with the same meaning, is still one term.

@question 1.1
Question: Which form is “Not every sailor is a pirate”?
Choices:
- O
- A
- E
- I
Answer: O
Why: It denies the universal without saying that no sailor is a pirate, so it says that some sailors are not pirates.
Why not the others: It does not say that every sailor is a pirate (A), that none is (E), or that some are (I).

@question 1.2
Question: What does “Only citizens are voters” state?
Choices:
- “All voters are citizens”
- “All citizens are voters”
- “Some citizens are voters”
- “No voters are citizens”
Answer: “All voters are citizens”
Why: The term after “only” becomes the predicate, so whatever votes is a citizen.
Why not the others: It does not say that every citizen votes; that would be the converse.

@question 1.3
Question: “No oaks are shrubs, since every oak is a tree and no tree is a shrub.” Which is the conclusion?
Choices:
- “No oaks are shrubs”
- “Every oak is a tree”
- “No tree is a shrub”
- The argument has no conclusion
Answer: “No oaks are shrubs”
Why: “Since” marks what follows it as premises, so the conclusion is the clause that comes first.
Why not the others: The two clauses after “since” are the premises.

@card 2

**Judging what has been found.** Once the argument is in standard form, it is judged by the rules already learned. For a categorical syllogism, the middle term must be distributed at least once; no term may be distributed in the conclusion unless it is distributed in its premise; nothing follows from two negative premises or from two particular premises; and a negative premise requires a negative conclusion, as a negative conclusion requires a negative premise. For a compound premise, the connective decides which moods are lawful. “If … then” concludes by positing the antecedent or by removing the consequent, and affirming the consequent is the common error. “Not both” concludes only by positing one member in order to remove the other. “Either … or” concludes by removing one member in order to posit the other, and by positing one in order to remove the other only when it is strict (“but not both”).

Some arguments carry a mode, such as “necessarily” or “contingently,” and then the conclusion may not claim a stronger mode than follows from the premises. When both premises have the same mode, the conclusion has that mode. In the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion. When a premise is contingent (in the first figure, the major premise; in the third figure, either premise), the conclusion is only contingent. In the second figure two contingent premises give no conclusion at all. An argument whose conclusion claims a stronger mode than these rules allow is invalid as it is stated.

@question 2.1
Question: “If Plato is a philosopher, he loves wisdom. Plato loves wisdom. So he is a philosopher.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, since it affirms the consequent
- Valid, since it posits the antecedent
- Valid, since it removes the consequent
- Invalid, since a conditional never yields a conclusion
Answer: Invalid, since it affirms the consequent
Why: The second premise posits the consequent, and the consequent may be true on other grounds.
Why not the others: The antecedent is not posited and the consequent is not removed; and a conditional does yield conclusions by its two lawful moods.

@question 2.2
Question: “Necessarily, every man is an animal. Every runner is a man. So, necessarily, every runner is an animal.” Is it valid as stated?
Choices:
- Valid, since in the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion
- Invalid, since the conclusion must follow the weaker premise in mode
- Invalid, since the minor premise is plain
- Valid, since both premises are necessary
Answer: Valid, since in the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion
Why: Aristotle holds this for the first figure (*Prior Analytics* I.9), and the course follows him.
Why not the others: The rule of the weaker premise in mode is Theophrastus’s, which the course does not follow here; and the minor premise is plain, not necessary.

@question 2.3
Question: “Contingently, no traveler is a merchant. Contingently, every pilgrim is a merchant. So, contingently, no pilgrim is a traveler.” Is it valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, since in the second figure two contingent premises give no conclusion
- Valid, since its plain form (Cesare) is valid
- Valid, since the conclusion claims only contingency
- Invalid, since the middle term is undistributed
Answer: Invalid, since in the second figure two contingent premises give no conclusion
Why: “Merchant” is the predicate of both premises, so the syllogism is in the second figure, where two contingent premises give nothing (*Prior Analytics* I.17).
Why not the others: The plain form is valid and the middle term is distributed in the first premise, but the modes decide that nothing follows.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Modal Syllogism
########################################################################

@deck leadin-16
Title: Before the Exercise · The Modal Syllogism
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.9, 14–17, 20–21.

**The question of mode.** A modal syllogism is one whose premises say how the predicate belongs to the subject. Three modes are considered here: the **necessary** (it cannot be otherwise), the **assertoric** or plain (it simply is so), and the **contingent** (it can be, and can also not be). Each question gives a syllogism with its premises in these modes and asks in what mode the conclusion follows: necessarily, assertorically, contingently, or not at all.

The first test is the form. If the syllogism is invalid by the general rules, nothing follows in any mode. In quality and quantity the conclusion follows the weaker premise, as in every categorical syllogism, so a negative premise requires a negative conclusion, and a particular premise a particular one.

In mode the course follows Aristotle. When both premises are necessary, the conclusion is necessary, and when both are plain, it is plain. In the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion, because what belongs necessarily to everything under the middle term belongs necessarily to whatever falls under it: “Every man is necessarily an animal; every runner is a man; therefore every runner is necessarily an animal” (*Prior Analytics* I.9). But a plain major premise with a necessary minor premise gives only a plain conclusion, because the major premise says only that the predicate belongs, not that it must.

@question 1.1
Question: “Necessarily, all M are P. All S are M. So all S are P.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Necessarily
- Assertorically
- Contingently
- Nothing follows
Answer: Necessarily
Why: In the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion.
Why not the others: “Assertorically” is the answer by Theophrastus’s rule of the weaker premise, which the course does not follow here; and the form is Barbara, so something follows.

@question 1.2
Question: “All M are P. Necessarily, all S are M. So all S are P.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Assertorically
- Necessarily
- Contingently
- Nothing follows
Answer: Assertorically
Why: In the first figure a plain major premise with a necessary minor premise gives only a plain conclusion, because the major premise says only that the predicate belongs.
Why not the others: The necessity of the minor premise does not carry over to the conclusion, and the form is valid.

@question 1.3
Question: “Necessarily, all P are M. Necessarily, all S are M. So all S are P.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Nothing follows
- Necessarily
- Assertorically
- Contingently
Answer: Nothing follows
Why: The middle term is the predicate of two affirmatives and is never distributed, so the form is invalid, and no mode can rescue it.
Why not the others: Necessary premises cannot make an invalid form valid.

@card 2

**Contingent premises.** When a premise is contingent, Aristotle’s rules differ from figure to figure, and the exercise uses only the cases in which his rule is simple. In the first figure, a contingent major premise with a contingent or plain minor premise gives a contingent conclusion (*Prior Analytics* I.14–15). In the third figure, two contingent premises, or one contingent and one plain, give a contingent conclusion (I.20–21). In the second figure, two contingent premises give no conclusion at all, whatever their quantity or quality (I.17). Aristotle’s other mixtures differ from mood to mood, and the exercise does not ask about them.

**A note on another view.** Theophrastus, Aristotle’s successor at the Lyceum, held that the conclusion always follows the weaker premise, in mode as in quality and quantity; by his rule the syllogism of the runner concludes only that every runner is an animal, as a plain assertion. The medieval commentators, the logic handed down under St. Thomas’s name, and St. Thomas himself when he cites the rule all follow Aristotle on this point, and so does this course.

@question 2.1
Question: “Contingently, no P are M. Contingently, all S are M. So no S are P.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Nothing follows
- Contingently
- Assertorically
- Necessarily
Answer: Nothing follows
Why: The syllogism is in the second figure, where two contingent premises give no conclusion (*Prior Analytics* I.17).
Why not the others: Its plain form (Cesare) is valid, but in this figure the modes leave nothing to conclude.

@question 2.2
Question: “Contingently, all M are P. All S are M. So all S are P.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Contingently
- Assertorically
- Necessarily
- Nothing follows
Answer: Contingently
Why: In the first figure a contingent major premise with a plain minor premise gives a contingent conclusion (*Prior Analytics* I.15).
Why not the others: The plain minor premise does not raise the conclusion above contingency, and the form is valid.

@question 2.3
Question: Whose rule is it that the conclusion always follows the weaker premise in mode?
Choices:
- Theophrastus, Aristotle’s successor at the Lyceum
- Aristotle
- St. Thomas
- John of St. Thomas, the seventeenth-century commentator
Answer: Theophrastus, Aristotle’s successor at the Lyceum
Why: Theophrastus held the rule; Aristotle held otherwise for the first figure, and the course follows Aristotle.
Why not the others: Aristotle and St. Thomas, when he cites the rule, grant a necessary conclusion from a necessary major premise and a plain minor premise in the first figure.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Chain of Syllogisms
########################################################################

@deck leadin-17
Title: Before the Exercise · The Chain of Syllogisms
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**A chain of syllogisms.** Reasoning rarely stops at one syllogism. A chain of syllogisms, which the tradition calls a polysyllogism, links several syllogisms so that the conclusion of one serves as a premise of the next: “All B are C; all A are B; therefore all A are C. All C are D; therefore all A are D.” In each link after the first, one new premise is stated, and the conclusion just drawn serves as the other premise. (When the intermediate conclusions are left unstated, the chain is called a sorites; the chains here state every conclusion.) Each question gives a chain in schematic letters and asks whether the whole chain is valid or whether some link fails.

The chain is judged link by link, and each link by the general rules of the syllogism. In each link the middle term is the term its two premises share and its conclusion omits. That middle term must be distributed at least once; no term may be distributed in the conclusion unless it is distributed in its premise; nothing follows from two negative premises or from two particular premises; and a negative premise requires a negative conclusion, as a negative conclusion requires a negative premise. The whole chain is valid only if every link is valid, so one invalid link makes the whole chain invalid, even when the last conclusion looks plausible.

@question 1.1
Question: “All B are C; all A are B; therefore all A are C. All C are D; therefore all A are D.” Is the whole chain valid?
Choices:
- Valid, since each link keeps every rule
- Invalid, since the second link has no middle term
- Invalid, since a chain may have only one link
- Valid only if the premises are true
Answer: Valid, since each link keeps every rule
Why: Both links are in Barbara: C is the middle term of the second link, and it is distributed in “All C are D.”
Why not the others: The second link’s middle term is C, shared by “All C are D” and “all A are C”; and validity does not depend on the truth of the premises.

@question 1.2
Question: “All B are C; all A are B; therefore all A are C. Some C are D; therefore some A are D.” Is the whole chain valid?
Choices:
- Invalid, since the middle term C is not distributed in the second link
- Valid, since the first link is valid
- Valid, since every link states a conclusion
- Invalid, since the second link has two negative premises
Answer: Invalid, since the middle term C is not distributed in the second link
Why: C is the subject of a particular and the predicate of an affirmative, so it is distributed in neither premise of the second link.
Why not the others: A valid first link does not save an invalid second one, and no premise here is negative.

@question 1.3
Question: If one link of a chain is invalid, what of the whole chain?
Choices:
- The whole chain is invalid
- The chain is valid if its last link is valid
- The chain is valid if most of its links are valid
- The chain is neither valid nor invalid
Answer: The whole chain is invalid
Why: The chain is valid only if every link is valid.
Why not the others: A conclusion that does not follow at one step cannot be made to follow by later steps.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Matter and the Form
########################################################################

@deck leadin-18
Title: Before the Exercise · The Matter and the Form
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963).

**Form and matter.** Every argument can be judged in two ways. Its **form** is the arrangement of its terms, and the argument is **valid** when it is impossible for the premises to be true and the conclusion false, whatever the terms stand for. Its **matter** is what the premises say, and the question there is whether they are in fact true. So the tradition distinguishes, within demonstrative science, **formal logic**, which studies the arrangement of the terms and whether the conclusion can be deduced from premises taken as given, and **material logic**, which studies how demonstration works, how different kinds of premises relate to the truths we can reach through them, and how each discipline reasons according to its own subject matter.

An argument that is valid and has true premises is **sound**, and it proves its conclusion. A valid argument with a false premise proves nothing, however good its form, and it is the commonest imitation of knowledge. True premises do not save a bad form either: if the form is invalid, the argument fails even when every premise and the conclusion are true. Each question therefore asks whether the form is valid and whether the premises are true, and the answer is “Valid & Sound,” “Valid, Unsound,” or “Invalid.” The form is judged first, by the general rules, and then each premise is tested.

@question 1.1
Question: “All fish are mammals; all whales are fish; so all whales are mammals.” How is it judged?
Choices:
- Valid, Unsound
- Valid & Sound
- Invalid
Answer: Valid, Unsound
Why: The form is Barbara, so it is valid; but “All fish are mammals” is false, so it proves nothing.
Why not the others: A true conclusion does not make the argument sound, and a false premise does not make the form invalid.

@question 1.2
Question: “All birds are animals; all robins are animals; so all robins are birds.” How is it judged?
Choices:
- Invalid
- Valid & Sound
- Valid, Unsound
Answer: Invalid
Why: “Animal” is the predicate of two affirmatives and is never distributed, so the form is invalid, although the premises and the conclusion are all true.
Why not the others: True premises cannot make an invalid form valid.

@question 1.3
Question: Does a valid argument with a false premise prove its conclusion?
Choices:
- No, since a valid form proves nothing from false matter
- Yes, since the form is valid
- Yes, if the conclusion happens to be true
- No, since a false premise always makes the form itself invalid
Answer: No, since a valid form proves nothing from false matter
Why: Validity concerns what would follow; proof needs true premises as well.
Why not the others: The form is still valid, but the argument is unsound, and a true conclusion reached this way is not proved by it.

@card 2

**Soundness and demonstration.** A sound argument is not yet a demonstration. A demonstration in the strict sense is a valid syllogism whose premises are true, better known than the conclusion, and express the necessary causes of the conclusion, so that it shows not only that the conclusion is true but why it must be. This exercise asks only whether the argument is valid and its premises true; it does not ask whether the premises are first and causal.

The arguments are drawn from four matters: the everyday world of living things and things made, classical mathematics after Euclid, the doctrine of virtue after Aristotle and St. Thomas, and the soul and living things after Aristotle’s *De Anima*. Each premise is judged by ordinary knowledge of these things and by their classical definitions, which are given above the argument as “Key terms.” Some true premises sound doubtful at first. For Euclid an isosceles triangle has two sides alone equal, so no equilateral triangle is isosceles; the unit is not a number; and a point is not a magnitude. For St. Thomas a passion is not a habit, the theological virtues observe no mean, and prudence is intellectual in what it is but is counted with the moral virtues because it concerns conduct. And according to the *De Anima* the oyster is an animal without sight. So a surprising premise is tested against the definitions rather than against first impressions.

@question 2.1
Question: Taken with Euclid’s definitions, is “No equilateral triangles are isosceles triangles” true?
Choices:
- True, since for Euclid an isosceles triangle has two sides alone equal
- False, since an equilateral triangle has two equal sides and so is isosceles too
- Neither true nor false
- True only of some triangles
Answer: True, since for Euclid an isosceles triangle has two sides alone equal
Why: An equilateral triangle has three equal sides, so it does not have two sides alone equal.
Why not the others: On the looser modern definition the statement would be false, but the exercise judges by the classical definition.

@question 2.2
Question: What does a demonstration in the strict sense have that a merely sound argument may lack?
Choices:
- Premises better known than the conclusion that express its necessary causes
- More than two premises
- Premises granted by everyone
- A surprising conclusion
Answer: Premises better known than the conclusion that express its necessary causes
Why: A demonstration shows why the conclusion must be so, through premises that give its cause.
Why not the others: Number of premises, general agreement, and surprise have nothing to do with demonstration.

@question 2.3
Question: A valid argument has true premises, but they do not give the cause of the conclusion. Is it a demonstration in the strict sense?
Choices:
- No, though it is sound
- Yes, since it is valid and its premises are true
- No, since it is unsound
- Yes, if its conclusion is necessary
Answer: No, though it is sound
Why: It is sound, and so it proves that its conclusion is true; but it does not show why it must be so.
Why not the others: Soundness is not yet demonstration, and the argument is not unsound.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · Dialectic
########################################################################

@deck leadin-20
Title: Before the Exercise · Dialectic
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963).

**Reasoning from the probable.** Dialectic is the use of reason to discover what is probably true in cases where we have not yet found demonstrative proof. It reasons from what is probable, that is, from what seems so to everyone, or to most people, or to the wise, and among the wise to all of them or to the most eminent. The probable in this sense is not a guess about odds but what a reasonable person may be asked to grant. So dialectic reaches a reasoned opinion, well supported and still open to challenge, while demonstration reaches science. The tradition places dialectic between rhetoric, which inclines us where the evidence is nearly even, and demonstration, which proves through the cause.

Some questions ask what standing a premise has before it is offered. It may be **demonstrable**, a matter of science that can be proved, and so not the business of dialectic. It may be **granted by everyone**, probable in the fullest sense, so that it may simply be assumed. It may be **granted by the learned**, still probable, though whoever uses it should expect to defend it. Or it may be **one person’s opinion**, perhaps true, but not yet something an opponent can be asked to grant. A principle known through itself as soon as its terms are understood, such as “Every whole is greater than any one of its parts,” is granted by everyone; it is certain, but it is not proved.

@question 1.1
Question: From what sort of premises does dialectic reason?
Choices:
- Probable ones, which seem so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise
- Ones that have been demonstrated
- Ones known to be false
- Ones invented for the sake of the argument and agreed on by the two parties
Answer: Probable ones, which seem so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise
Why: The probable is what a reasonable person may be asked to grant.
Why not the others: Demonstrated premises belong to science, and dialectic does not argue from what is known to be false or merely invented.

@question 1.2
Question: What does dialectic reach at its best?
Choices:
- A reasoned opinion, well supported but not proved
- Certain knowledge of the cause
- A persuasive speech
- A story that makes sense of the facts as they are known
Answer: A reasoned opinion, well supported but not proved
Why: Demonstration reaches science; dialectic reaches opinion held for good reasons.
Why not the others: Knowledge of the cause belongs to demonstration, persuasion to rhetoric, and stories to poetics.

@question 1.3
Question: What standing has “The angles of any triangle add to two right angles”?
Choices:
- Demonstrable
- Granted by everyone
- Granted by the learned
- One person’s opinion
Answer: Demonstrable
Why: It can be proved from the principles of geometry, so it is a matter of science, not of dialectic.
Why not the others: It need not be merely granted, by many or by few, since it can be proved.

@card 2

**Places and maxims.** Logic has two parts: the art of judging an argument already given, which the *Prior* and *Posterior Analytics* teach, and the art of finding an argument when one is needed, which Aristotle’s *Topics* teaches. The instrument of finding is the place (*locus*), also called the topic, a standing relation such as genus to species or whole to part, from which an argument may be drawn. Given something to be shown and something already granted, a place shows which relation will make the one a reason for the other. Each place carries its maxim (*maxima propositio*), the governing proposition on which an argument drawn from that place depends. When the maxim is true the argument holds; when it is false but looks true, the argument is a fallacy, so dialectic and the study of fallacies are one doctrine seen from opposite sides.

The exercise names twelve places. The place *from the definition* rests on the maxim that whatever fits the definition fits the thing defined, and whatever does not, does not. The place *from the wider kind* (genus) rests on the maxim that whatever belongs to the whole family belongs to each kind within it, and what the whole family lacks, each kind lacks. The place *from the narrower kind* (species) rests on the maxim that whatever the narrower kind is said of, the wider kind is said of too. The place *from the property* rests on the maxim that where the property is, the thing is, and where it is absent, the thing is absent. The place *from opposites* rests on the maxim that what holds of one opposite, the contrary holds of the other. The place *from the more and the less* rests on the maxim that if it holds where it is less to be expected, it holds all the more where it is more to be expected. The place *from likeness* rests on the maxim that what holds in one case holds in a case like it, so far as they are alike. The place *from the whole and its parts* rests on the maxim that what holds of the whole holds of the parts, when it holds of the whole by reason of the parts. The place *from the cause and the end* rests on the maxim that where the cause is, the effect follows, and a thing is judged by what it is for. The place *from words of the same root* (conjugates) rests on the maxim that what holds of the thing holds of what is named from it, so that if justice is good, acting justly is good. The place *from the name* rests on the maxim that what the name means tells something of what the thing is, though not always, and never on its own. The place *from authority* rests on the maxim that what those who know a subject agree on may be granted, until better reason appears. The tradition counts authority the weakest of the places, because it moves us by who says a thing rather than by what makes it so; but it is still a real reason.

@question 2.1
Question: “Every bird has feathers, and a robin is a bird, so a robin has feathers.” From which place is the argument drawn?
Choices:
- From the wider kind (genus)
- From the narrower kind (species)
- From the definition
- From likeness
Answer: From the wider kind (genus)
Why: What holds of the whole family, birds, is brought down to one kind within it.
Why not the others: An argument from the narrower kind would carry what holds of the robin up to the bird.

@question 2.2
Question: What is the maxim of a place?
Choices:
- The governing proposition an argument drawn from that place depends on
- The strongest argument the place can supply for the conclusion being sought
- The name of the place in Latin
- The conclusion the argument reaches
Answer: The governing proposition an argument drawn from that place depends on
Why: When the maxim is true the argument holds; when it is false but looks true, the argument is a fallacy.
Why not the others: The maxim is neither an argument, nor a name, nor a conclusion, but the rule the argument depends on.

@question 2.3
Question: Why is authority called the weakest of the places?
Choices:
- It moves us by who says a thing, not by what makes it so
- Authorities are usually wrong
- It is not a place at all
- Only the ignorant use it
Answer: It moves us by who says a thing, not by what makes it so
Why: It is a real place and a real reason, but it gives no reason drawn from the thing itself.
Why not the others: The tradition counts authority among the places, and those who know a subject are often right.

@card 3

**Answering an objection.** A disputation takes place between two parties, the one who puts the case and the one who answers (the opponent and the respondent, in Latin *opponens* and *respondens*). The schools answer an objection by distinguishing the term, by denying the premise, or by granting the premises and denying the consequence; and when the objection is simply sound, the respondent concedes it and states the thesis again more narrowly. So four answers are open. The respondent **distinguishes the term** (the *distinguo*) when a word in the objection is true in one sense and false in another, granting the one sense and denying the other. He **denies the premise** when the objection rests on something simply false. He **grants it all and denies that the conclusion follows** when every premise stands and the conclusion still does not follow, since the matter is sound but the form is not. And he **grants the whole objection** when it is right, and states the thesis again more narrowly.

A dialectical disputation also differs from a testing one. Dialectic argues toward the better view, while a testing disputation works from what seems true to the respondent, only in order to find out whether he really knows.

@question 3.1
Question: Claim: “A good knife cuts well.” Objection: “A good knife in a murderer’s hand does harm, so a good knife is not good.” How should it be answered?
Choices:
- Distinguish the term, since “good” is said in two ways
- Deny the premise, since it is simply not so
- Grant it all, and deny that the conclusion follows from the premises
- Grant the whole objection and narrow the thesis
Answer: Distinguish the term, since “good” is said in two ways
Why: “Good” as a knife and good in its use are two senses; the first is granted and the second denied.
Why not the others: The premise is true, and the trouble lies in the word “good,” not in the form or in the thesis.

@question 3.2
Question: Every premise of an objection is true, yet its conclusion does not follow. Which answer fits?
Choices:
- Grant it all, and deny that the conclusion follows
- Deny the premise
- Distinguish the term
- Grant the whole objection
Answer: Grant it all, and deny that the conclusion follows
Why: The matter is sound but the form is not, so the respondent concedes the premises and denies the consequence.
Why not the others: Nothing false is to be denied and no word is ambiguous; and since the conclusion does not follow, the thesis need not be given up.

@question 3.3
Question: How does a testing disputation differ from a dialectical one?
Choices:
- It only finds out whether someone really knows, while dialectic argues toward the better view
- They are the same thing
- Testing reaches certainty and dialectic does not
- Dialectic uses syllogisms and testing does not
Answer: It only finds out whether someone really knows, while dialectic argues toward the better view
Why: A testing disputation works from what seems true to the respondent, in order to see how much he knows.
Why not the others: They differ in aim, and neither one reaches the certainty of demonstration.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Enthymeme
########################################################################

@deck leadin-19
Title: Before the Exercise · The Enthymeme
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937).

**The unspoken premise.** An enthymeme is a syllogism with a premise (or, at times, the conclusion) left unspoken, which the hearer is trusted to supply: “Socrates is mortal, for he is a man” leaves out “All men are mortal.” The enthymeme is far more frequent than the full syllogism, in scientific speech as in common speech. It is called first-order when the major premise is left unsaid, and second-order when the minor premise is. A premise is left unsaid sometimes for brevity, sometimes because it is too obvious to state, and sometimes because it is weak and would not be granted if it were stated; so the suppressed premise is where an argument hides its weakness, and it should be supplied and examined. Restoring a valid form does not make the argument sound, since the premise supplied may be false.

Some questions give the stated premise and the conclusion and ask for the tacit premise, typed in standard form, or “none.” First find the two terms of the conclusion: its predicate is the major term and its subject the minor term. Then find the middle term, the term of the stated premise that is not in the conclusion. Then join the middle term to the term of the conclusion that the stated premise leaves out, in a quantity and quality that make a valid syllogism by the general rules; any premise that does so is accepted. So for “All A are C, since all B are C,” the tacit premise is “All A are B.” Sometimes no premise can complete the argument, because the stated premise is particular or negative and the conclusion claims more than any added premise could support, as in “All A are C, for some B are C”; then the answer is “none.”

@question 1.1
Question: “Socrates is mortal, for he is a man.” Which premise is left unspoken?
Choices:
- “All men are mortal”
- “All mortals are men”
- “Some men are mortal”
- “Socrates is a mortal man”
Answer: “All men are mortal”
Why: The middle term “man” must be joined to “mortal”; with this major premise the argument is valid.
Why not the others: “All mortals are men” leaves the middle term undistributed, “Some men are mortal” is too weak, and the last merely restates the conclusion.

@question 1.2
Question: “All A are C, since all B are C.” What tacit premise completes it?
Choices:
- “All A are B”
- “All B are A”
- “Some A are B”
- None completes it
Answer: “All A are B”
Why: The middle term B is joined to A, the term of the conclusion that the stated premise leaves out, and the syllogism is Barbara.
Why not the others: “All B are A” yields only “Some A are C,” and “Some A are B” yields only a particular conclusion.

@question 1.3
Question: “All A are C, for some B are C.” What tacit premise completes it?
Choices:
- None, since a particular premise cannot yield a universal conclusion
- “All A are B”
- “All B are A”
- “No A are B”
Answer: None, since a particular premise cannot yield a universal conclusion
Why: Whatever premise is added, the particular premise allows only a particular conclusion, and “All A are C” is universal.
Why not the others: With “All A are B” the middle term is undistributed, and with the others the conclusion still cannot be universal.

@card 2

**The direction of the premise, and signs.** The tacit premise must run in the right direction. For “Socrates is mortal, for he is a man,” the premise needed is “All men are mortal,” not its converse “All mortals are men,” which would leave the middle term undistributed; and “Some men are mortal” is too weak to conclude anything about Socrates. Other questions give such an argument in ordinary words and ask what is being taken for granted.

Aristotle also uses the word “enthymeme” in another sense, for a rhetorical argument from likelihoods and signs, and many of these examples are arguments from signs. A necessary sign concludes of necessity, as in Aristotle’s example “She has given birth, for she has milk,” whose tacit premise is “Whoever has milk has given birth.” A refutable sign does not: “It must have rained, for the streets are wet” is valid only with “Whenever the streets are wet, it has rained,” and that premise is false, since a street-sweeper also wets the streets. The familiar premise “Whenever it rains, the streets are wet” is true, but with it the argument only affirms the consequent.

@question 2.1
Question: “She has given birth, for she has milk.” What is being taken for granted?
Choices:
- “Whoever has milk has given birth”
- “Whoever has given birth has milk”
- “Some mothers have milk”
- “Milk is nourishing”
Answer: “Whoever has milk has given birth”
Why: This is Aristotle’s example of a necessary sign, and it needs the premise in this direction.
Why not the others: The converse, true or not, would leave the argument affirming the consequent; the others conclude nothing about her.

@question 2.2
Question: “It must have rained, for the streets are wet.” Which premise makes it valid?
Choices:
- “Whenever the streets are wet, it has rained”
- “Whenever it rains, the streets are wet”
- “The streets are usually dry”
- “Rain always wets something”
Answer: “Whenever the streets are wet, it has rained”
Why: Only this premise yields a valid argument, and it is false, since a street-sweeper also wets the streets; so the sign is refutable.
Why not the others: The familiar premise “Whenever it rains, the streets are wet” is true, but with it the argument affirms the consequent.

@question 2.3
Question: An enthymeme leaves its major premise unspoken. Of what order is it?
Choices:
- The first order
- The second order
- The third order
- It has no order
Answer: The first order
Why: An enthymeme is first-order when the major premise is hidden and second-order when the minor premise is.
Why not the others: The second order hides the minor premise.


########################################################################
# Before the Exercise · The Fallacies
########################################################################

@deck leadin-21
Title: Before the Exercise · The Fallacies
Small heading: BEFORE THE EXERCISE

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); St. Thomas Aquinas (attributed), *De fallaciis*; Aristotle, *Sophistical Refutations*.

**What a fallacy is.** A fallacy is an argument that looks sound and is not. The tradition studies the typical errors in reasoning so that we can avoid them; Aristotle treats them in the *Sophistical Refutations*, a book aimed especially at refuting those who use errors in reasoning to manipulate others, as the sophists of antiquity did. The exercise follows *De fallaciis*, a short treatise attributed to St. Thomas, which explains each fallacy by two causes. The **cause of the appearance** is what makes the argument look good, and the **cause of the failure** is what actually breaks it; we are deceived by the two together, since something appears to be so and is not. So “I know the man who is coming; Coriscus is the man who is coming; so I know Coriscus” looks sound because the man and the feature really do go together here, and it fails because a man and a feature he happens to have are not the same. The answer to a fallacy removes its cause of failure: the ambiguous word is stated twice in different words, the dropped qualification is put back, or the hidden question is answered first.

Every argument depends on a rule, usually unspoken. “Socrates is a man, therefore an animal” depends on a true rule, that whatever the narrower kind is said of, the wider kind is said of too. “Socrates is an animal, therefore a man” depends on the same rule reversed, which is false. So both premises may be true and the argument still fail, because the rule it depends on is false but looks true. When we reason badly by ourselves, *De fallaciis* notes (proem), it is always beside our intention, since nobody sets out to deceive himself; so this study is applied first to our own arguments.

@question 1.1
Question: According to *De fallaciis*, every fallacy has two causes. What are they?
Choices:
- A cause of the appearance and a cause of the failure
- Two false premises
- Two conclusions that contradict each other in one argument
- Two words with the same meaning
Answer: A cause of the appearance and a cause of the failure
Why: The cause of the appearance makes the argument look good; the cause of the failure breaks it.
Why not the others: A fallacy may have true premises and a single conclusion; what it always has is a look of soundness and a hidden fault.

@question 1.2
Question: “Socrates is an animal, therefore Socrates is a man.” On what rule does it depend?
Choices:
- “Whatever the wider kind is said of, the narrower kind is said of too”
- “Whatever the narrower kind is said of, the wider kind is said of too”
- “Whatever is true of a part is true of the whole”
- “Whatever is true at one time is true at every time”
Answer: “Whatever the wider kind is said of, the narrower kind is said of too”
Why: The rule is the true rule reversed, and it is false.
Why not the others: The true rule supports “Socrates is a man, therefore an animal,” not this argument.

@question 1.3
Question: “I know the man who is coming; Coriscus is the man who is coming; so I know Coriscus.” Why does it fail?
Choices:
- A man and a feature he happens to have are not the same
- One of its premises is false
- The word “know” has two meanings
- It asks two questions as one
Answer: A man and a feature he happens to have are not the same
Why: This is the fallacy of accident: what is true of the man as coming is concluded of the man himself.
Why not the others: The premises may be true, the word “know” is used in one sense, and nothing is asked.

@card 2

**The thirteen fallacies.** The appearance arises in two ways: from the words, when one expression seems to name one thing although it means several, and from outside the words, when the language is in order but two things that agree in some way are taken to be simply one. Six fallacies are in the words, and their number follows from the kinds of ambiguity.
