Why not the others: A species is not tall, and neither is the word; the subject stands for individual trees, in the proper sense.

@question 3.3
Question: On Ockham’s view, what does “man” stand for in “Man is a species”?
Choices:
- The concept in the mind
- The common nature in things
- Each individual man
- The spoken word “man”
Answer: The concept in the mind
Why: Ockham, a nominalist, denied common natures and held that the term stands for the concept.
Why not the others: The common nature is Burley’s realist answer, individuals would be personal supposition, and the word would be material.

@card 4

**Singular, determinate, distributive.** Personal supposition is **singular** (or discrete) when the term stands for one individual, as “Socrates” or “this man.” It is **common** when a common term stands for many. Common supposition is **determinate** when we may descend to the individuals by “or”: “Some man is running” means that this man is running, or that one, or that other one. It is **distributive** when we may descend by “and”: “Every man is mortal” means that this man is mortal, and that one, and that other one.

The descent shows what the proposition requires to be true. A determinate term needs only one of the individuals to make the proposition true; a distributive term needs every one of them. From the singulars we may also ascend: from “This man is running” to “Some man is running,” but not from “This man is mortal” to “Every man is mortal,” since one instance does not establish all of them.

@question 4.1
Question: In “Some river is frozen,” what supposition does “river” have?
Choices:
- Determinate
- Distributive
- Singular
- Collective
Answer: Determinate
Why: We may descend by “or”: this river, or that one, or that other one, is frozen.
Why not the others: We cannot descend by “and,” the term is common rather than singular, and the rivers are not taken together.

@question 4.2
Question: In “Every river flows downhill,” what supposition does “river” have?
Choices:
- Distributive
- Determinate
- Merely confused
- Simple
Answer: Distributive
Why: We may descend by “and”: this river flows downhill, and that one, and that other one.
Why not the others: The proposition is about each river, so the descent is by “and,” and the term stands for individuals.

@question 4.3
Question: From “This dog barks,” to which proposition may we ascend?
Choices:
- Some dog barks
- Every dog barks
- No dog barks
- Dog is a species
Answer: Some dog barks
Why: From a singular we may ascend to the determinate “Some dog barks,” but not to the distributive “Every dog barks.”
Why not the others: One instance does not establish every case, and the other options change the quality or the supposition.

@card 5

**Merely confused and collective.** Some common terms allow neither descent. In “Every man is an animal,” the predicate “animal” does not allow the descent “Every man is this animal, or every man is that animal,” since both are false; it allows only “Every man is this animal or that animal or that other one,” with the “or” inside the predicate. This is **merely confused** supposition, and it is the supposition of the predicate of a universal affirmative. The subject of “Workmen are needed to build a house” has the same supposition, since no particular workman is needed.

A term has **collective** supposition when it stands for many things taken together as one subject, as in “The apostles were twelve.” No apostle was twelve, so we cannot descend to “Peter was twelve, and Paul was twelve.” Collective supposition must not be confused with distributive: “All the soldiers are brave” is said of each soldier, while “All the soldiers surrounded the fort” is said of them together.

@question 5.1
Question: In “Every bird is an animal,” what supposition does “animal” have?
Choices:
- Merely confused
- Distributive
- Determinate
- Collective
Answer: Merely confused
Why: We cannot descend to “every bird is this animal, or every bird is that animal,” only to “every bird is this animal or that animal,” which is merely confused supposition.
Why not the others: The predicate of a universal affirmative is neither distributed nor determinate.

@question 5.2
Question: “The jurors were twelve.” What supposition does “jurors” have?
Choices:
- Collective
- Distributive
- Determinate
- Material
Answer: Collective
Why: No juror was twelve; the jurors are taken together as one subject, which is collective supposition.
Why not the others: We cannot descend to each juror by “and” or by “or,” and the term does not stand for the word.

@question 5.3
Question: “A key is needed to open this door.” Why can we not descend to “This key is needed, or that key is needed”?
Choices:
- No particular key is needed, so the supposition is merely confused
- Every key is needed, so the supposition is distributive
- The keys are all taken together, so the supposition is collective
- The word “key” is meant, so the supposition is material
Answer: No particular key is needed, so the supposition is merely confused
Why: As with Maritain’s workmen, the term stands for keys without picking out any one, which is merely confused supposition.
Why not the others: Not every key is needed, the keys are not taken together, and the word itself is not meant.

@card 6

**Supposition and distribution.** The modern word “distributed” names confused and distributive supposition, which is one mode, not three kinds. Common supposition is determinate or confused, and confused supposition is distributive or merely confused. In A, “Every S is P,” the subject is distributive and the predicate merely confused. In E, “No S is P,” both terms are distributive. In I, “Some S is P,” both terms are determinate. In O, “Some S is not P,” the subject is determinate and the predicate distributive. These are the rules of distribution that the exercises on the syllogism use.

The modes of supposition also explain conversion. E and I convert simply because their two terms have the same mode of supposition, so exchanging them changes nothing. A and O do not convert simply, because their terms have different modes, and a change in a term’s supposition is enough to spoil an inference. So the predicate of an affirmative is never taken universally, and the predicate of a negative is always taken universally.

@question 6.1
Question: In O, “Some S is not P,” which term is distributed?
Choices:
- The predicate only
- The subject only
- Both terms
- Neither term
Answer: The predicate only
Why: In O the subject is determinate and the predicate distributive.
Why not the others: Both terms are distributed in E and neither in I; in O only the predicate.

@question 6.2
Question: Why does E convert simply?
Choices:
- Both its terms have the same mode, distributive
- Its subject is determinate and its predicate distributive
- It is negative, and every negative converts
- Its terms are both singular
Answer: Both its terms have the same mode, distributive
Why: When both terms have the same mode of supposition, exchanging them changes nothing.
Why not the others: O is negative and does not convert simply; what matters is that E’s two terms share one mode.

@question 6.3
Question: What did the medieval logicians call what modern books call a distributed term?
Choices:
- Confused and distributive supposition
- Common, confused, and distributive, as three kinds
- Simple supposition
- Material supposition
Answer: Confused and distributive supposition
Why: “Distributed” names one mode, confused and distributive supposition. Common supposition is determinate or confused, and confused supposition is distributive or merely confused.
Why not the others: The phrase with three names is one mode, not three kinds; determinate supposition is undistributed, and simple and material are other kinds.

@card 7

**The copula and existence.** What a term stands for also depends on the time the copula signifies. An affirmative whose subject stands for nothing at that time is false: said now, “Napoleon will be emperor” is false, since there is no future Napoleon. A negative can be true of such a subject: “Napoleon is not in Paris” is true. This is why affirmatives carry existential import and negatives do not.

Maritain distinguishes **natural** supposition, where the predicate belongs to the subject by its essence and the copula abstracts from time, from **accidental** supposition, where the predicate belongs by accident and the copula signifies actual existence. So “Every triangle has three sides” is true even if no triangle now exists, because in necessary matter the subject is extended to what is possible, while “Peter is running” is false if Peter does not exist. Words such as “can,” “will,” and “was” likewise extend a term to possible, future, or past things, and qualifiers such as “the poor” or “of this town” narrow it. The technical names are **ampliation** and **restriction**.

@question 7.1
Question: There are no dragons. Is “Some dragon is green” true or false?
Choices:
- False, since its subject stands for nothing
- True, since nothing contradicts it
- Neither, since it is about nothing
- True in natural supposition
Answer: False, since its subject stands for nothing
Why: An affirmative proposition whose subject stands for nothing is false.
Why not the others: The rule leaves no third value: the affirmative is false, and its contradictory “No dragon is green” is true.

@question 7.2
Question: There are no dragons. Is “No dragon is green” true or false?
Choices:
- True, since a negative needs no existing subject
- False, since its subject stands for nothing that exists
- Neither, since it is about nothing
- False in natural supposition
Answer: True, since a negative needs no existing subject
Why: A negative proposition can be true when its subject stands for nothing, which is why negatives carry no existential import.
Why not the others: Only affirmatives are made false by an empty subject.

@question 7.3
Question: In “Every circle has a center,” what supposition does “circle” have?
Choices:
- Natural, since the predicate belongs by essence
- Accidental, since some circles are drawn badly
- Material, since “circle” is a word
- Collective, since the circles are taken together
Answer: Natural, since the predicate belongs by essence
Why: The predicate belongs to the subject by its essence, and the copula abstracts from time, which is natural supposition.
Why not the others: Accidental supposition is for predicates that belong by accident, and the term here stands for circles, each one.

@card 8

**A change of supposition breaks the argument.** Since a valid inference needs each term to stand for the same things throughout, an argument fails when a term’s kind of supposition changes from one premise to another. “The logical categories were set out by Aristotle; quantity is a logical category; so quantity was set out by Aristotle” fails, because in the minor premise “logical category” stands for the categories as known, and quantity itself, as a way of being, was not invented by anyone.

The same rule covers a change in the manner of existence. From “Every centaur is half man, half horse” and “A centaur is a creature of fable,” we may conclude that some creature of fable is half man, half horse, but not that such a creature really exists, since the premises speak of the centaur only as imagined. Many errors that look like failures of form are failures of supposition, and the remedy is the one the disputations used: to distinguish what each term stands for.

@question 8.1
Question: “Bread is a word; I ate bread; so I ate a word.” What has gone wrong?
Choices:
- “Bread” changes from material to personal supposition
- “Bread” changes from simple to collective supposition
- The argument has two negative premises
- Nothing, the argument is valid
Answer: “Bread” changes from material to personal supposition
Why: In the first premise the term stands for the word, in the second for the food, so the middle term stands for different things.
Why not the others: There is no negative premise and nothing collective; the term changes from the word to the thing.

@question 8.2
Question: “Rose is a species of flower; this flower is a rose; so this flower is a species of flower.” What has gone wrong?
Choices:
- The middle term shifts from the nature to an individual
- The conclusion is true, so nothing has gone wrong
- Both premises are affirmative, which is not allowed in this figure
- The argument has two particular premises
Answer: The middle term shifts from the nature to an individual
Why: In the first premise “rose” stands for the nature and in the second for individual roses, so the middle term stands for different things.
Why not the others: The conclusion is false and the premises are true, and two affirmatives may conclude; the fault is the change in what the middle term stands for.

@question 8.3
Question: What remedy do the disputations use against a change of supposition?
Choices:
- To distinguish what each term stands for
- To deny every premise of the argument
- To grant the argument and narrow the thesis
- To convert each premise simply
Answer: To distinguish what each term stands for
Why: The respondent distinguishes the senses in which the term is taken, granting one and denying the other.
Why not the others: Denying true premises or granting a bad argument does not answer it; the distinction shows where it fails.


########################################################################
# Opposition and Conversion in Depth
########################################################################

@deck adv-opp
Title: Opposition and Conversion in Depth
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Why contradiction is the strongest opposition; singular propositions; what necessary and contingent matter add to the square; future contingents; how conversion rests on supposition; Buridan’s limits on contraposition and obversion; why these operations are not inferences in the proper sense; the reciprocal and the inverse; and the modern reading of the universal.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); Timothy B. Noone and Gyula Klima, *A Little Practical Olde Logick* (manuscript); Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides); Aristotle, *De interpretatione* 7 and 9, with St. Thomas Aquinas’s commentary, bk. I, lect. 10 and 13–14.

**The strongest opposition.** It is natural to think that contraries, “Every man is just” and “No man is just,” are the most opposed of propositions. The tradition holds the opposite: **contradiction** is the strongest opposition, because the one proposition simply denies what the other affirms, and the two agree in nothing. Contraries still agree in something, since both take the subject universally, and so both can be false together.

Subalternation, between A and I or between E and O, is not opposition in the proper sense at all. The subaltern says the same thing as its universal, only partially: “Some man is mortal” repeats in part what “Every man is mortal” says. It is placed on the square of opposition for the sake of symmetry, so that all the relations between propositions with the same terms can be seen together, but Aristotle’s own account of opposition does not include it.

@question 1.1
Question: Why is contradiction a stronger opposition than contrariety?
Choices:
- Contradictories agree in nothing, while contraries can both be false
- Contradictories are both universal, while contraries are not
- Contraries can both be true, while contradictories cannot
- Contradictories differ only in quantity, and contraries in quality
Answer: Contradictories agree in nothing, while contraries can both be false
Why: The contradictory simply denies what the other affirms; contraries keep the universal subject and can share falsity.
Why not the others: Contraries cannot both be true, and contradictories differ in both quality and quantity.

@question 1.2
Question: Why is subalternation not opposition in the proper sense?
Choices:
- The subaltern repeats in part what the universal says
- The subaltern denies part of what the universal affirms
- The two can never both be true
- The two have different terms
Answer: The subaltern repeats in part what the universal says
Why: “Some S is P” says partially what “Every S is P” says, so the two are not opposed.
Why not the others: Subalterns share the same terms and quality and can both be true; nothing is denied.

@question 1.3
Question: “Every planet is inhabited” and “No planet is inhabited” are both false. What does this show?
Choices:
- Contraries can both be false
- Contradictories can both be false
- Subcontraries can both be false
- The square fails for planets
Answer: Contraries can both be false
Why: Contraries share something, the universal subject, and so they can both be false.
Why not the others: Contradictories cannot both be false, and these two are universals of opposite quality, which is contrariety.

@card 2

**Singulars and the universal taken as one.** The square assumes a universal and a particular subject. A singular proposition has only one kind of opposition: “Peter is wise” and “Peter is not wise” are contradictories, since the second simply denies the first, and there is no singular that could be their contrary or their subaltern. So two singulars of opposite quality cannot both be true or both be false.

The same holds when a universal nature is taken as one thing, as in “Man is the noblest of bodily creatures” and “Man is not the noblest of bodily creatures.” St. Thomas explains that here the predicate is said of the nature as it is one in the mind, though what is said belongs to the nature as it is in things (commentary on the *De interpretatione*, bk. I, lect. 10). Such propositions behave like singulars and admit only contradiction.

@question 2.1
Question: What opposition holds between “Plato is a poet” and “Plato is not a poet”?
Choices:
- Contradiction
- Contrariety
- Subcontrariety
- Subalternation
Answer: Contradiction
Why: Two singulars of opposite quality are contradictories; the second simply denies the first.
Why not the others: Contrariety and subcontrariety need a universal or a particular subject, which a singular does not have.

@question 2.2
Question: Can “Rome is the capital” and “Rome is not the capital” both be false?
Choices:
- No, since singulars of opposite quality are contradictories
- Yes, since they are contraries, which can both be false
- Yes, since singular propositions have no truth value
- No, since they are subcontraries, which cannot both be false
Answer: No, since singulars of opposite quality are contradictories
Why: Two singulars of opposite quality are contradictories, which divide truth and falsity between them.
Why not the others: Singulars admit only contradiction, not contrariety or subcontrariety.

@question 2.3
Question: “The horse is a noble animal,” said of the nature as one. What opposition does its denial have to it?
Choices:
- Only contradiction, as with singulars
- Contrariety, as with universals
- Subcontrariety, as with particulars
- No opposition, since it is not a proposition
Answer: Only contradiction, as with singulars
Why: A universal nature taken as one behaves like a singular subject, so its denial is its contradictory.
Why not the others: The subject is not taken universally or particularly here, so only contradiction applies.

@card 3

**What the matter adds.** The laws of opposition hold by form, whatever the terms: contraries cannot both be true, subcontraries cannot both be false, and contradictories divide truth and falsity between them. The matter can add more. In **necessary matter**, where the predicate belongs to the subject by its essence, contraries cannot both be false and subcontraries cannot both be true: “Every man is mortal” is true and “No man is mortal” is false, and “Some man is not mortal” is false.

In **contingent matter**, where the predicate may or may not belong, contraries are usually both false and subcontraries both true: “Every man is just” and “No man is just” are false, while “Some man is just” and “Some man is not just” are true. Only in necessary matter may we conclude from the truth of one subcontrary to the falsity of the other. An argument that does so in contingent matter mistakes a feature of the matter for a law of the form.

@question 3.1
Question: “Some triangle has three angles” is true, and the matter is necessary. What of “Some triangle does not have three angles”?
Choices:
- It is false
- It is true
- It is undetermined
- It is neither true nor false
Answer: It is false
Why: In necessary matter two subcontraries cannot both be true, so the second is false.
Why not the others: By form alone the subcontrary would be undetermined; the necessary matter decides it.

@question 3.2
Question: In contingent matter, what usually holds of “Some bird sings” and “Some bird does not sing”?
Choices:
- Both are true
- Both are false
- One is true and one false
- Neither has a truth value
Answer: Both are true
Why: Where the predicate may or may not belong, subcontraries are usually both true.
Why not the others: Subcontraries cannot both be false, and in contingent matter they are usually both true.

@question 3.3
Question: When may we conclude from the truth of one subcontrary to the falsity of the other?
Choices:
- Only in necessary matter
- In every matter
- Only in contingent matter
- Only when both are singular
Answer: Only in necessary matter
Why: Only where the predicate belongs by essence can two subcontraries not both be true.
Why not the others: By form alone subcontraries can both be true, so the conclusion depends on necessary matter.

@card 4

**Future contingents.** Aristotle asks whether “There will be a sea battle tomorrow” and “There will not be a sea battle tomorrow” must be one true and the other false now. If one were already true, the battle would be already fixed, and deliberation would be useless. His answer, which St. Thomas develops in his commentary (bk. I, lect. 13–14), is that such propositions about a contingent future are not yet determinately true or false, since their truth depends on things that do not yet exist even in their causes.

The law of contradiction is not abandoned. The disjunction “Either there will be a sea battle tomorrow or there will not” is necessarily true now; what is not yet determined is which part is true. So the two propositions exclude each other, but neither can yet be called the true one. This applies only to the contingent future: “The sun will rise tomorrow” concerns what follows from causes already at work, and “There was a battle yesterday” is already determinately true or false.

@question 4.1
Question: On Aristotle’s view, what is true now about “It will rain here a year from today”?
Choices:
- It is not yet determinately true or false
- It is already true or already false
- It is necessarily false, since it is future
- It is not a proposition at all
Answer: It is not yet determinately true or false
Why: A proposition about a contingent future is not yet determinately true or false.
Why not the others: It is a proposition, and its truth is not fixed yet; nothing makes it necessarily false.

@question 4.2
Question: Which proposition is necessarily true now?
Choices:
- Either the ship will sail tomorrow or it will not
- The ship will sail tomorrow
- The ship will not sail tomorrow
- The ship can neither sail nor stay
Answer: Either the ship will sail tomorrow or it will not
Why: The disjunction of the two contradictories is necessary; which part is true is not yet determined.
Why not the others: Neither part of the disjunction is yet determinately true, and the last option is impossible.

@question 4.3
Question: Why is “The sun will rise tomorrow” not a future contingent in this sense?
Choices:
- It follows from causes already at work
- It is about the past and not the future
- It is a negative proposition
- It is about a substance, not an accident
Answer: It follows from causes already at work
Why: What follows from causes already at work is determined now, unlike what depends on choice or chance.
Why not the others: The proposition is affirmative and about the future; what matters is that its causes are already acting.

@card 5

**Conversion rests on supposition.** To convert a proposition is to exchange its terms so as to say the same truth. The term that becomes the subject must keep the extension it had as the predicate, and so the rules of conversion follow from the supposition of the terms. In E both terms are distributive, and in I both are determinate, so E and I convert simply. In A the subject is distributive and the predicate merely confused, so A converts only by limitation: “Every man is mortal” gives “Some mortal is a man.”

O does not convert at all. In “Some man is not just,” the predicate “just” stands for just men, from whom some man is excluded; in “Some just thing is not a man,” the subject “just thing” could stand for something other than a man, such as God. So the supposed converse does not say the same truth. O converts only by contraposition: “Some man is not just” gives “Some non-just thing is not a non-man,” that is, some non-just thing is a man.

@question 5.1
Question: Why does “No fish is a mammal” convert simply?
Choices:
- Both its terms are distributive
- Both its terms are determinate
- Its subject is singular
- Its predicate is merely confused
Answer: Both its terms are distributive
Why: In E both terms stand for each of their individuals, so exchanging them keeps the same truth.
Why not the others: The terms of E are distributive, not determinate or merely confused, and the subject is common.

@question 5.2
Question: What is the converse of “Every rose is a flower”?
Choices:
- Some flower is a rose
- Every flower is a rose
- No flower is a rose
- Some flower is not a rose
Answer: Some flower is a rose
Why: A converts only by limitation, since its predicate is taken particularly.
Why not the others: “Every flower is a rose” would take the predicate universally, which the original never did.

@question 5.3
Question: Why does “Some animal is not a dog” have no simple converse?
Choices:
- In the converse the new subject could stand for other things
- It is affirmative, and only negatives convert
- It is universal, and universals convert only by limitation
- Its terms are both distributive
Answer: In the converse the new subject could stand for other things
Why: In the original the predicate stands for dogs, from whom some animal is excluded; as a subject it changes what it stands for, and “Some dog is not an animal” is false.
Why not the others: O is particular and negative; its subject is determinate and its predicate distributive, so the modes do not match.

@card 6

**Buridan’s limits on contraposition and obversion.** John Buridan, the fourteenth-century master at Paris, noticed that contraposition and obversion need a further condition when the affirmatives carry existential import. “Every man is a being” is true, but its contrapositive “Every non-being is a non-man” is false, because nothing is a non-being, and an affirmative whose subject stands for nothing is false. Contraposition of A and of O holds only when the terms stand for things that actually exist.

Obversion moves safely from the affirmative side of the square to the negative: “Every lawyer is learned” gives “No lawyer is non-learned.” But it moves from the negative to the affirmative only when the subject exists: “No chimera is a woman” is true, while “Every chimera is a non-woman” is false, since there are no chimeras. This is the same doctrine of existential import that the course follows throughout, applied to the operations on a single proposition.

@question 6.1
Question: On Buridan’s account, what further condition does the contraposition of an A proposition require?
Choices:
- That its terms stand for things that actually exist
- That its subject be a singular term
- That its predicate be distributed
- That it be in necessary matter
Answer: That its terms stand for things that actually exist
Why: An affirmative whose subject stands for nothing is false, so the contrapositive holds only when its terms stand for existing things.
Why not the others: The condition concerns existence, not the subject’s singularity, the predicate’s distribution, or the matter.

@question 6.2
Question: “No unicorn is a horse” is true. What of “Every unicorn is a non-horse”?
Choices:
- False, since there are no unicorns
- True, since it is the obverse
- True, since unicorns are not horses
- Undetermined, since unicorns are imaginary
Answer: False, since there are no unicorns
Why: Obversion from E to A needs an existing subject; an affirmative about nothing is false.
Why not the others: Obversion from the negative side to the affirmative holds only when the subject exists.

@question 6.3
Question: In which direction is obversion always safe?
Choices:
- From affirmative to negative
- From negative to affirmative
- From particular to universal
- In every direction alike
Answer: From affirmative to negative
Why: Moving from the affirmative side to the negative needs no further condition, since negatives carry no existential import.
Why not the others: Moving from negative to affirmative requires the subject to exist, and obversion never changes quantity.

@card 7

**Not an inference in the proper sense.** Conversion, obversion, subalternation, and the passage from a proposition to the falsity of its contradictory are often called immediate inferences. Maritain argues that none of them is an inference in the proper sense. In each, the mind says the same truth in a second form: “No man is an angel” and “No angel is a man” are two ways of stating one separation of the two terms, and the mind does not advance from a known truth to one not yet known.

An inference in the proper sense reaches a new truth, and for that the mind must relate one known truth to another; so every inference properly so called has at least two premises and three terms. The immediate operations are still useful, since they let us restate a premise so that it fits an argument, and the logician can verify them by a syllogism. But the verification comes after the mind has already seen that the two forms say the same thing.

@question 7.1
Question: Why does Maritain deny that conversion is an inference properly so called?
Choices:
- The mind states one truth in two forms and does not advance
- Conversion is invalid unless a premise is added
- Conversion uses three terms instead of two
- Conversion reaches a truth that is not yet known
Answer: The mind states one truth in two forms and does not advance
Why: The converse says the same truth as the original, so the mind does not move to a new truth.
Why not the others: Conversion is valid, uses two terms, and reaches no new truth; that is why it is not an inference properly so called.

@question 7.2
Question: How many premises does an inference in the proper sense require at least?
Choices:
- Two
- One
- Three
- None
Answer: Two
Why: To reach a new truth the mind must relate one known truth to another, so it needs at least two premises and three terms.
Why not the others: One proposition alone can only be restated, not developed into a new truth.

@question 7.3
Question: What use do the immediate operations still have?
Choices:
- They let us restate a premise so that it fits an argument
- They prove new conclusions from a single premise
- They replace the syllogism in demonstration
- They show which premises are true
Answer: They let us restate a premise so that it fits an argument
Why: Restating a premise in an equivalent form can make it fit the figure of an argument.
Why not the others: They do not prove new conclusions, replace the syllogism, or establish truth.

@card 8

**The reciprocal and the inverse.** The **reciprocal** of a universal affirmative exchanges its terms and keeps the universal quantity: the reciprocal of “Every square is a rectangle” is “Every rectangle is a square.” Since the predicate of an affirmative is taken particularly, the reciprocal says a different truth and must be proved on its own. Sometimes it is true, as with “Every square is a rhombus with right angles”; sometimes it is false, as here.

The **inverse** negates both the subject and the copula: the inverse of “Every man is an animal” is “Every non-man is not an animal,” which says in negative form the same thing as the reciprocal, and is false. To pass from a universal affirmative to its reciprocal is a common error: from “Every great artist astonishes ordinary people” it does not follow that whoever astonishes ordinary people is a great artist.

@question 8.1
Question: What is the reciprocal of “Every whale is a mammal”?
Choices:
- Every mammal is a whale
- Some mammal is a whale
- No whale is a non-mammal
- Every non-whale is not a mammal
Answer: Every mammal is a whale
Why: The reciprocal exchanges the terms and keeps the universal quantity.
Why not the others: “Some mammal is a whale” is the converse by limitation; the others are the obverse and the inverse.

@question 8.2
Question: Why must a reciprocal be proved on its own?
Choices:
- It says a different truth from the original
- It is always false
- It is the contradictory of the original proposition
- It changes the quality of the original
Answer: It says a different truth from the original
Why: Since the predicate of an affirmative is taken particularly, the reciprocal adds a claim the original did not make.
Why not the others: A reciprocal is sometimes true, it is not a contradictory, and it keeps the quality.

@question 8.3
Question: What does the inverse of “Every whale is a mammal” say in negative form?
Choices:
- The same as its reciprocal
- The same as the original
- The same as its converse by limitation
- The same as its obverse
Answer: The same as its reciprocal
Why: “Every non-whale is not a mammal” says the same as “Every mammal is a whale.”
Why not the others: The inverse is false here, while the original, its converse by limitation, and its obverse are true.

@card 9

**The modern reading of the universal.** Modern predicate logic, developed by Gottlob Frege, the German mathematician and logician, writes “All men are mortal” as “For anything whatever, if it is a man, it is mortal.” On this reading the universal is true when there are no men at all, since a conditional with a false antecedent is counted true; so the A proposition carries no existential import, the step from “All S are P” to “Some S are P” fails, and the square loses subalternation, contrariety, and subcontrariety as well, so that only contradiction remains.

The tradition answers in two ways. First, the formula represents the universal without saying what it means: “All men are mortal” attributes mortality to man as such, and does not merely report that nothing in the universe is a man without being mortal. Second, as Gyula Klima, the Hungarian-American logician, and Timothy B. Noone, the American medievalist, point out, the medieval logicians did see the problem of empty terms, and placed existence in the affirmatives rather than at the bottom of the square. Contemporary Aristotelians, such as Klima and Terence Parsons, the American logician, have since shown that the older square can be stated formally without contradiction.

@question 9.1
Question: On the modern reading, when is “All griffins have wings” true?
Choices:
- Even when there are no griffins
- Only when some griffin exists
- Only when every winged thing is a griffin
- Never, since griffins are imaginary
Answer: Even when there are no griffins
Why: It is read as “For anything, if it is a griffin, it has wings,” which is counted true when nothing is a griffin.
Why not the others: The modern reading gives the universal no existential import, so it does not require any griffin.

@question 9.2
Question: Which relations of the square does the modern reading give up?
Choices:
- Subalternation, contrariety, and subcontrariety
- Contradiction only
- Subcontrariety and contradiction
- Every relation on the square, contradiction included
Answer: Subalternation, contrariety, and subcontrariety
Why: Without existential import, the universals A and E can both be true when the subject stands for nothing, and the particulars I and O can both be false, so subalternation, contrariety, and subcontrariety fail, and only contradiction remains.
Why not the others: Contradiction remains. The other three relations fail when the subject can stand for nothing.

@question 9.3
Question: What is the tradition’s first answer to the modern reading?
Choices:
- The formula represents the universal without saying what it means
- The modern formula is simply invalid
- Universals should be read as particulars
- Existential import belongs to the negatives
Answer: The formula represents the universal without saying what it means
Why: “All men are mortal” attributes mortality to man as such; the formula only reports that nothing is a man without being mortal.
Why not the others: The tradition does not call the formula invalid, and it places existence in the affirmatives, not in the negatives.


########################################################################
# The Modal Proposition in Depth
########################################################################

@deck adv-modp
Title: The Modal Proposition in Depth
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Modes of the copula; the quality and quantity of a modal; the square with a quantified dictum; the vowels that mark mode and dictum; a modal read of what is said and of the thing, with St. Thomas’s use of the distinction; the two senses of “possible”; and why the mode belongs to the proposition and not to the act of judging.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.3, 13; St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Sentences* I, d. 38, q. 1, a. 5, ad 5, and *De veritate*, q. 2, a. 12, ad 4.

**Modes of the copula.** A mode is a determination added to something. Some modes affect only the subject, as in “The prudent man is wise,” and some affect only the predicate, as in “Peter runs fast.” Neither makes a proposition modal. A proposition is modal only when the mode affects the copula itself, that is, the way in which the predicate belongs to the subject: “Peter is necessarily a man,” “Peter can be ill.”

There are four such modes: necessity (it cannot not be), impossibility (it cannot be), possibility (it can be), and contingency (it can be and can also not be). In the *Prior Analytics* Aristotle treats the syllogism only with necessity and possibility, while *On Interpretation* 12–13 treats all four modes. A proposition with no mode of the copula, such as “Man is rational,” is called simply attributive (Latin *de inesse*, “of being in”).

@question 1.1
Question: Which proposition is modal in the proper sense?
Choices:
- The fire is necessarily hot
- The careful driver arrives safely
- The horse runs swiftly
- The old man walks slowly
Answer: The fire is necessarily hot
Why: “Necessarily” affects the copula, the way the predicate belongs to the subject.
Why not the others: “Careful,” “swiftly,” and “slowly” affect the subject or the predicate, not the copula.

@question 1.2
Question: In the *Prior Analytics*, with which modes does Aristotle treat the syllogism?
Choices:
- Necessity and possibility only
- All four modes
- Necessity alone
- Impossibility and contingency only
Answer: Necessity and possibility only
Why: In the *Prior Analytics* he treats the syllogism only with necessity and possibility; *On Interpretation* 12–13 treats all four modes.
Why not the others: All four modes are treated in *On Interpretation*, chapters 12 and 13; the account of the syllogism in the *Prior Analytics* treats only necessity and possibility.

@question 1.3
Question: What is a proposition with no mode of the copula called?
Choices:
- Simply attributive
- Contingent
- Composite
- Problematic
Answer: Simply attributive
Why: It simply says that the predicate is in the subject, and so it is called simply attributive.
Why not the others: “Contingent” names a mode, “composite” a sense of a modal, and “problematic” is Kant’s term for a judgment.

@card 2

**Quality and quantity of a modal.** A modal proposition has two qualities, that of the mode and that of the dictum. The proposition is affirmative when both are affirmative or both are negative, and negative when one is affirmative and the other negative. So “It is impossible that it not be,” with a negative mode and a negative dictum, is affirmative, and it comes to “It is necessary that it be.” Counting “impossible” as “not possible” makes this rule work.

The modes also have a kind of quantity. Necessity and impossibility are like universals, since what must be or cannot be holds in every case; possibility and contingency are like particulars, since what can be need hold only sometimes. This is why the four modes stand at the four corners of a square: “necessary” with the universal affirmative, “impossible” with the universal negative, “possible” with the particular affirmative, and “possible not” with the particular negative.

@question 2.1
Question: Is “It is impossible that he not breathe” affirmative or negative?
Choices:
- Affirmative, since mode and dictum are both negative
- Negative, since its mode is negative
- Negative, since its dictum is negative and that decides it
- Neither, since it has two negations
Answer: Affirmative, since mode and dictum are both negative
Why: When mode and dictum are both negative, the proposition is affirmative; it comes to “It is necessary that he breathe.”
Why not the others: One negation in mode or dictum makes a modal negative; two make it affirmative.

@question 2.2
Question: Which modes are like universals?
Choices:
- Necessity and impossibility
- Possibility and contingency
- Necessity and possibility
- Impossibility and contingency
Answer: Necessity and impossibility
Why: What must be or cannot be holds in every case, so these two modes are like universals.
Why not the others: Possibility and contingency are like particulars, since what can be need hold only sometimes.

@question 2.3
Question: Which corner of the modal square answers to the universal negative?
Choices:
- Impossible
- Necessary
- Possible
- Possible not
Answer: Impossible
Why: “Impossible” denies in every case, so it stands where the universal negative stands.
Why not the others: “Necessary” is the universal affirmative corner, and the possibles are the particular corners.

@card 3

**The square with a quantified dictum.** When the dictum itself has a universal or particular subject, the modal square grows to eight corners. “It is necessary that every man be mortal,” “It is necessary that some man be mortal,” “It is possible that every man be a geometer,” and so on, each standing in fixed relations to the others. The relations still follow the pattern of the square: what is necessary of every subject is necessary of some, and what is possible of some is not thereby possible of every.

The ordinary wording can mislead. “It is impossible that every man run” does not mean “It is impossible that any man run”; it means only “It is not possible that every man run,” which is the contradictory of “It is possible that every man run.” To say that no man can run, we must say “It is impossible that any man run,” or simply “No man can run.” Maritain gives a serious example: from the falsity of “Every error can be avoided without revelation,” it follows only that some error cannot be so avoided, not that none can.

@question 3.1
Question: What does “It is impossible that every student pass” mean?
Choices:
- It is not possible that every student pass
- It is impossible that any student pass
- It is necessary that no student pass
- It is possible that no student pass
Answer: It is not possible that every student pass
Why: “Impossible that every” denies only that all can pass together; it does not say that none can pass.
Why not the others: “Impossible that any” or “necessary that no” would say that none can pass, which is a stronger claim.

@question 3.2
Question: “Every illness can be cured” is false. What follows?
Choices:
- Some illness cannot be cured
- No illness can be cured
- Every illness cannot be cured
- Some illness can be cured
Answer: Some illness cannot be cured
Why: The contradictory of the false proposition is true: some illness cannot be cured.
Why not the others: The falsity of the universal does not make its contrary true, and it says nothing either way about some illness being curable.

@question 3.3
Question: What is necessary of every subject is necessary of some. Which relation of the square is this?
Choices:
- Subalternation
- Contradiction
- Contrariety
- Subcontrariety
Answer: Subalternation
Why: The universal implies the particular below it, which is subalternation.
Why not the others: Contradiction, contrariety, and subcontrariety are relations of opposed quality.

@card 4

**Marking the combinations.** To keep the combinations of mode and dictum in view, the scholastics marked each with a vowel (A, E, I, U): A when both mode and dictum are affirmative, E when the dictum is negative and the mode affirmative, I when the mode is negative and the dictum affirmative, and U when both are negative. Their verse runs: “E dictum negat, Ique modum, nihil A, sed U totum.”

Each of the four words Purpurea, Iliace, Amabimus, and Edentuli then lists, in order, the forms with the modes possible, contingent, impossible, and necessary that say the same thing. So the first word, Purpurea, gathers “not possible that not,” “not contingent that not,” “impossible that not,” and “necessary that it be,” which are equipollent. The words are an aid to memory; what they record is the rule that a negation before the mode moves to the contradictory corner, and a negation after the mode denies only the dictum.

@question 4.1
Question: Which vowel marks a modal with a negative mode and an affirmative dictum?
Choices:
- I
- A
- E
- U
Answer: I
Why: “Ique modum”: I denies the mode only.
Why not the others: A denies nothing, E denies the dictum, and U denies both.

@question 4.2
Question: What rule do the words Purpurea, Iliace, Amabimus, and Edentuli record?
Choices:
- A negation before the mode moves to the contradictory corner
- A negation after the mode leaves the proposition unchanged
- Every modal with two negations is negative
- The possible and the necessary always agree
Answer: A negation before the mode moves to the contradictory corner
Why: The words gather equipollent forms, and they rest on where the negation falls.
Why not the others: A negation after the mode denies the dictum, which changes the proposition, and two negations make the modal affirmative.

@question 4.3
Question: Which vowel marks a modal in which both the mode and the dictum are negative?
Choices:
- U
- A
- E
- I
Answer: U
Why: “Sed U totum”: U denies both the mode and the dictum.
Why not the others: A affirms both, E denies the dictum, and I denies the mode.

@card 5

**Of what is said, and of the thing.** A modal can be read in two ways. Read as said **of what is said** (*de dicto*), the mode governs the whole proposition: “It is necessary that whatever God knows is true” says that the proposition “What God knows is true” cannot be false. Read as said **of the thing** (*de re*), the mode is said of the thing the subject stands for: “Whatever God knows is necessarily true” would say that each thing God knows is necessary in itself.

St. Thomas uses the distinction to answer an objection that God’s knowledge makes all things necessary. The statement “Everything known by God is necessarily so” is true of what is said and false of the thing (commentary on the *Sentences* I, d. 38, q. 1, a. 5, ad 5). God knows contingent things as contingent; what is necessary is the truth of the whole statement, not each thing known. The distinction runs close to that of the composite and divided senses, and it decides many arguments that seem to make free acts necessary.

@question 5.1
Question: “Whatever is seen is necessarily seen.” How is this true?
Choices:
- Of what is said, as the statement “What is seen is seen”
- Of the thing, as each thing seen being necessary
- In neither way, since seeing is contingent
- Of the thing, but not of what is said
Answer: Of what is said, as the statement “What is seen is seen”
Why: The whole statement cannot be false, which is the reading of what is said; the things seen are not necessary in themselves.
Why not the others: Read of the thing, it would make each thing seen necessary, which is false; it is true of what is said.

@question 5.2
Question: How does St. Thomas answer the objection that God’s knowledge makes all things necessary?
Choices:
- The statement is true of what is said and false of the thing
- God does not know contingent things at all
- God’s knowledge is only probable
- Contingent things become necessary once they are known by God
Answer: The statement is true of what is said and false of the thing
Why: “Everything known by God is necessarily so” is true of what is said and false of the thing; God knows contingent things as contingent.
Why not the others: St. Thomas affirms that God knows contingent things, certainly, without making them necessary.

@question 5.3
Question: When a modal is read as said of the thing, what is the mode said of?
Choices:
- The thing the subject stands for
- The whole proposition
- The word that names the subject
- The mind of the speaker
Answer: The thing the subject stands for
Why: Read of the thing, the mode is said of the thing; read of what is said, of the whole statement.
Why not the others: The whole proposition is the other reading, and the word and the speaker are not in question.

@card 6

**Two senses of “possible.”** Aristotle distinguishes two senses of “possible” (*Prior Analytics* I.13). In one sense the possible is whatever is not impossible, and in this sense the necessary is also possible, since what must be certainly can be. In the other, stricter sense the possible is what is neither necessary nor impossible, what can be and can also not be; this is the **contingent**, and in this sense the necessary is not possible.

Much confusion comes from passing between the two senses. From “It is necessary that fire is hot” it follows that it is possible in the first sense, but not that it is contingent. And from “It is contingent that Peter walks” it follows both that he can walk and that he can not walk. The course takes “contingent” in the strict sense: what can be and can also not be.

@question 6.1
Question: In which sense is what is necessary also possible?
Choices:
- In the sense of “not impossible”
- In the sense of “contingent”
- In the sense of “can also not be”
- In no sense at all
Answer: In the sense of “not impossible”
Why: What must be certainly can be, so it is possible in the sense of not impossible.
Why not the others: In the strict sense the possible can also not be, which the necessary cannot.

@question 6.2
Question: From “It is contingent that the bell rings,” what follows?
Choices:
- It can ring, and it can also not ring
- It must ring, and it cannot fail to ring
- It cannot ring
- It rings now
Answer: It can ring, and it can also not ring
Why: The contingent in the strict sense can be and can also not be.
Why not the others: The contingent is neither necessary nor impossible, and it says nothing about what happens now.

@question 6.3
Question: From “It is necessary that a square has four sides,” what does not follow?
Choices:
- It is contingent that a square has four sides
- It is possible that a square has four sides
- It is not impossible that a square has four sides
- It is true that a square has four sides
Answer: It is contingent that a square has four sides
Why: The necessary is possible in the broad sense, but it is not contingent, since it cannot not be.
Why not the others: The necessary is true, possible, and not impossible; it is only not contingent.

@card 7

**The mode belongs to the proposition.** Aristotle’s modes concern the way the predicate belongs to the subject: whether it belongs necessarily, contingently, or simply. Immanuel Kant, the eighteenth-century German philosopher, divided judgments instead by their modality as problematic, assertoric, and apodictic, and he meant by this the possibility or necessity of the act of judging, not of the relation judged. Maritain points out that Kant also counts the simple assertion as a mode, though in it nothing modifies the copula.

The difference matters. For the tradition, “Man is necessarily an animal” states something about man and animal, not about the certainty of the one who judges; one can be certain of a contingent fact and uncertain of a necessary truth. A further sign that the mode belongs to the proposition is that conversion keeps it: “No man can think without images” converts to “No being that thinks without images can be a man,” with the mode unchanged.

@question 7.1
Question: What did Kant mean by the modality of a judgment?
Choices:
- The possibility or necessity of the act of judging
- The way in which the predicate belongs to the subject itself
- The number of terms in the proposition
- The quantity of the subject
Answer: The possibility or necessity of the act of judging
Why: Kant spoke of the act of judging, while Aristotle spoke of the relation judged.
Why not the others: The way the predicate belongs is Aristotle’s sense; quantity and the number of terms are other matters.

@question 7.2
Question: Why is a simple assertion not modal, on the tradition’s view?
Choices:
- Nothing in it modifies the copula
- It is always uncertain
- It is always about the future
- It has no predicate
Answer: Nothing in it modifies the copula
Why: A modal proposition requires a mode of the copula, and a simple assertion has none.
Why not the others: A simple assertion can be certain, can be about the past, and has a predicate.

@question 7.3
Question: “Every circle is necessarily round” converts to which proposition?
Choices:
- Something round is necessarily a circle
- Something round is possibly a circle
- Everything round is necessarily a circle
- Something round is a circle, with no mode
Answer: Something round is necessarily a circle
Why: Conversion keeps the mode and, for A, limits the quantity.
Why not the others: The mode is kept in conversion, and A converts only by limitation.


########################################################################
# Compound and Exponible Propositions
########################################################################

@deck adv-comp
Title: Compound and Exponible Propositions
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Propositions made of propositions: the copulative, disjunctive, conjunctive, and conditional; causal, rational, relative, and adversative propositions; how each is contradicted; and the hidden compounds, exclusive, exceptive, and reduplicative.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides).

**Simple and compound.** A simple, or categorical, proposition joins two terms by the copula “is”: “Man is mortal.” A **compound**, or hypothetical, proposition joins two propositions by a connecting word other than the verb, such as “and,” “or,” or “if”: “The brave gave their lives, and the cowards fled.” A compound proposition states a new truth of its own, which depends on the truths of its parts but is not the same as any of them.

A compound is **openly** compound when its structure shows two propositions joined, as in the examples with “and,” “or,” and “if.” It is **hiddenly** compound (exponible) when it looks simple but contains a word, such as “only,” “except,” or “as such,” that makes it equivalent to several propositions. “Only man laughs” looks like one categorical proposition, but it says two things: that man laughs, and that nothing else does.

@question 1.1
Question: “If it freezes, the pond will be safe to cross.” What kind of proposition is this?
Choices:
- Openly compound
- Hiddenly compound
- Simple
- Singular
Answer: Openly compound
Why: It plainly joins two propositions by “if,” so it is openly compound.
Why not the others: Its structure shows two propositions, so it is neither simple nor hiddenly compound.

@question 1.2
Question: “Only the owl hunts at night in this wood.” What kind of proposition is this?
Choices:
- Hiddenly compound
- Openly compound
- Simple and categorical
- Conditional
Answer: Hiddenly compound
Why: It looks simple, but “only” makes it equivalent to two propositions.
Why not the others: No connecting word joins two propositions, but “only” hides a second one.

@question 1.3
Question: How does a compound proposition differ from a simple one?
Choices:
- It joins propositions, not terms, by a word other than the verb
- It has more than one adjective in its predicate
- It always has a universal subject and also a particular predicate
- It is always longer than a simple proposition
Answer: It joins propositions, not terms, by a word other than the verb
Why: A simple proposition joins two terms by “is”; a compound joins propositions by “and,” “or,” “if,” and the like.
Why not the others: Adjectives, quantity, and length do not make a proposition compound.

@card 2

**The copulative.** A **copulative** proposition joins its parts by “and.” It is true only when every part is true, and false when any one part is false: “The moon moves and the earth stands still” is false because its second part is false. So from a true copulative we may assert either part by itself, but from a true part we may not assert the copulative, and from a false copulative we may not deny any particular part, since we do not know which one is false.

Some copulatives are hidden in a joined subject or predicate: “Peter and Paul died at Rome” means that Peter died at Rome and Paul died at Rome. But a joined subject is not always copulative: “Peter and Paul are friends” says something of the two together, and so it is a simple proposition with one subject. John of St. Thomas notes that the copulative follows its weaker part: if any part is false, or only probable, the whole is no stronger than that part.

@question 2.1
Question: “The sun is a star and the moon is a planet.” Is this true?
Choices:
- No, since one part is false
- Yes, since one part is true
- Yes, since both terms are bodies
- It cannot be decided
Answer: No, since one part is false
Why: A copulative is false when any part is false, and the moon is not a planet.
Why not the others: One true part is not enough for a copulative; every part must be true.

@question 2.2
Question: “Peter and John are cousins.” What kind of proposition is this?
Choices:
- Simple, with one joined subject
- Copulative, meaning Peter is a cousin and John is a cousin
- Disjunctive
- Conditional
Answer: Simple, with one joined subject
Why: Being cousins is said of the two together, so the proposition has one subject and is simple.
Why not the others: “Peter is a cousin and John is a cousin” would lose the relation between them.

@question 2.3
Question: It is false that “It is raining and cold.” What may we conclude?
Choices:
- At least one part is false, but not which
- It is not raining
- It is not cold
- It is neither raining nor cold
Answer: At least one part is false, but not which
Why: A false copulative tells us only that some part is false.
Why not the others: We may not deny any particular part, since we do not know which one is false.

@card 3

**The disjunctive and the conjunctive.** A **disjunctive** proposition joins its parts by “or.” In the strict sense it says that the parts cannot be true together and cannot be false together, as in “A number is either odd or even.” In the broad sense it says only that at least one part is true. Either way, the disjunctive is true when one part is true, and so it follows its stronger part. An improper disjunctive merely offers alternatives (“You may come by train or on foot”), and denying one of these posits nothing.

The **conjunctive** proposition denies that two propositions are true together, without denying that both may be false: “No one can be both a judge and a party in the same case.” Where there is no middle between the two, the conjunctive says the same thing as a disjunctive: “No one can serve both God and Mammon” amounts to “One serves either God or Mammon,” since on the Gospel’s account there is no middle between the two.

@question 3.1
Question: “Either the lamp is on or it is off.” What kind of disjunctive is this?
Choices:
- Strict, since the parts cannot be true or false together
- Broad, since both parts may be true
- Improper, since it only offers alternatives
- Conjunctive, since it denies both parts
Answer: Strict, since the parts cannot be true or false together
Why: A lamp cannot be both on and off, nor neither, so the disjunction is strict.
Why not the others: Both parts cannot be true together, so it is not broad, and it asserts a real alternative.

@question 3.2
Question: “No one can be both asleep and keeping watch.” What kind of proposition is this?
Choices:
- Conjunctive
- Copulative
- Disjunctive
- Conditional
Answer: Conjunctive
Why: It denies that two propositions are true together, without denying that both may be false.
Why not the others: It does not join by “and,” “or,” or “if” to assert; it denies that both are true together.

@question 3.3
Question: A disjunctive is said to follow its stronger part. Why?
Choices:
- It is true when any one part is true
- It is true only when every part is true
- Its parts are always necessary
- It asserts a sequence between its parts
Answer: It is true when any one part is true
Why: One true part is enough to make a disjunctive true.
Why not the others: It is the copulative that needs every part true, and the conditional that asserts a sequence.

@card 4

**The conditional.** A **conditional** proposition joins its parts by “if.” It does not assert either part; it asserts only that the second follows from the first. So it is true when the sequence holds, even if both parts are false: “If twenty were odd, it could not be divided by two” is true. And it is false when the sequence fails, even if both parts are true: “If England is an island, two and two make four” is false, since the second does not follow from the first.

This is why a conditional can be asserted without asserting its condition. St. Paul could write “But though we, or an angel from heaven, preach a gospel to you besides that which we have preached to you, let him be anathema” (Galatians 1:8) without thinking that an angel would do so. Maritain distinguishes conditionals in the strict sense, where “if” signifies a necessary sequence, from conditionals in a broad sense, where it signifies only a regular connection in fact: “If the speaker so much as opened his mouth, everyone yawned.” A broad conditional is true only if its parts are in fact verified.

@question 4.1
Question: “If a triangle had four sides, it would have four angles.” Is this conditional true?
Choices:
- Yes, since the second follows from the first
- No, since both parts are false
- No, since triangles have three sides and never four
- It cannot be judged
Answer: Yes, since the second follows from the first
Why: A conditional asserts only that the sequence holds, and it holds here.
Why not the others: The falsity of the parts does not make a conditional false; only a failed sequence does.

@question 4.2
Question: “If water is wet, Paris is in France.” Is this conditional true?
Choices:
- No, since the second does not follow from the first
- Yes, since both of its parts are plainly true of the world
- Yes, since the first part is true
- It cannot be judged
Answer: No, since the second does not follow from the first
Why: A conditional is false when the sequence fails, even if both parts are true.
Why not the others: The truth of the parts does not make the conditional true.

@question 4.3
Question: What does a strict conditional assert?
Choices:
- That the second part follows from the first
- That both parts are true
- That the first part is true, whatever the second
- That the second part is true
Answer: That the second part follows from the first
Why: The conditional asserts the sequence, not the truth of either part.
Why not the others: A conditional can be true when both parts are false.

@card 5

**Causal, rational, relative, adversative.** Other compounds can be resolved into categorical propositions. A **causal** proposition, “The soul is immortal because it is spiritual,” asserts three things: that the soul is immortal, that it is spiritual, and that its spirituality is the cause of its immortality. So, unlike a conditional, it requires the truth of both its parts. A **rational** proposition, “The soul is spiritual, therefore it is immortal,” is in fact a short argument, an enthymeme.

A **relative** proposition may have a causal sense (“The soul, which is spiritual, is immortal”) or may amount to a copulative (“The man whom I see is running” means that I see a man and he is running). An **adversative** proposition, joined by “but,” may deny a causal link (“The soul is spiritual but is not joined to the body by force”) or may simply join two facts (“He laughs, but I weep”).

@question 5.1
Question: “The bridge fell because the river flooded.” What must be true for this to be true?
Choices:
- Both parts, and that the flood caused the fall
- Only that a flood of that size would cause the fall
- Only that the bridge fell
- Only that the river flooded
Answer: Both parts, and that the flood caused the fall
Why: A causal proposition asserts both parts and the causal link between them.
Why not the others: Unlike a conditional, a causal proposition requires the truth of both its parts.

@question 5.2
Question: “He is honest, therefore he will be trusted.” What kind of proposition is this?
Choices:
- Rational, in fact a short argument
- Causal
- Conditional
- Copulative
Answer: Rational, in fact a short argument
Why: A rational proposition, joined by “therefore,” is an enthymeme.
Why not the others: It draws a conclusion; a causal proposition would say “because.”

@question 5.3
Question: “The man whom I met is a doctor.” Into what may this relative proposition be resolved?
Choices:
- I met a man, and he is a doctor
- If I met a man, he is a doctor
- I met a man because he is a doctor
- I met a man or he is a doctor
Answer: I met a man, and he is a doctor
Why: This relative has no causal sense, so it amounts to a copulative.
Why not the others: Nothing here is conditional, causal, or disjunctive; it joins two facts.

@card 6

**The contradictory of a compound.** To contradict a compound we must deny exactly what it asserts. The contradictory of a copulative is a disjunctive of the contradictories of its parts: the contradictory of “Every rich man is sad and honored” is “Some rich man is not sad or not honored.” The contradictory of a broad disjunctive is a copulative of the contradictories: the contradictory of “Either the bridge is closed or the road is flooded” is “The bridge is not closed and the road is not flooded.”

The contradictory of a conditional denies the sequence, not the parts: the contradictory of “If he is rich, he is happy” is “It is not the case that if he is rich he is happy,” that is, he could be rich and not happy. It is not “If he is rich, he is not happy,” which is a contrary claim. Modern logic states the first two rules as De Morgan’s laws, after Augustus De Morgan, the nineteenth-century English logician.

@question 6.1
Question: What is the contradictory of “The door is locked and the window is shut”?
Choices:
- The door is not locked or the window is not shut
- The door is not locked and the window is not shut
- If the door is locked, the window is not shut
- The door is locked or the window is shut
Answer: The door is not locked or the window is not shut
Why: The contradictory of a copulative is a disjunctive of the contradictories of its parts.
Why not the others: Denying both parts says more than the contradictory; the copulative is false if either part is false.

@question 6.2
Question: What is the contradictory of “Either he is ill or he is lazy,” taken broadly?
Choices:
- He is not ill and he is not lazy
- He is not ill or he is not lazy
- If he is ill, he is not lazy
- He is ill and he is lazy
Answer: He is not ill and he is not lazy
Why: A broad disjunctive is false only when both parts are false.
Why not the others: “Not ill or not lazy” is still compatible with the disjunctive being true.

@question 6.3
Question: What is the contradictory of “If the dam breaks, the town floods”?
Choices:
- The dam could break without the town flooding
- If the dam breaks, the town will certainly not flood
- The dam does not break and the town floods
- If the town floods, the dam broke
Answer: The dam could break without the town flooding
Why: The contradictory of a conditional denies the sequence, not its parts.
Why not the others: “If it breaks, the town does not flood” is a contrary claim, and the others change the subject.

@card 7

**Exclusive and exceptive propositions.** An **exclusive** proposition contains a word such as “only” or “alone,” and it resolves into two propositions. “Only the human species laughs” means that the human species laughs, and that no other species laughs; it is true only if both are true. An **exceptive** proposition contains “except” or “but,” and it resolves into three. “Every metal except mercury is solid at room temperature” means that every metal other than mercury is so solid, that mercury is a metal, and that mercury is not so solid.

The resolution shows how such propositions can be false. “Only citizens may vote” is false if a non-citizen may vote, even if every citizen may. And an exceptive is false if the excepted thing does not really belong to the subject, as “Every bird except the bat flies” is, since the bat is not a bird.

@question 7.1
Question: Into what does “Only the dog barked” resolve?
Choices:
- The dog barked, and nothing else barked
- The dog barked, and the dog is an animal
- If anything barked, the dog barked
- The dog barked or nothing barked
Answer: The dog barked, and nothing else barked
Why: An exclusive proposition resolves into two propositions, the positive and the exclusion.
Why not the others: The exclusion of everything else is what “only” adds.

@question 7.2
Question: Into how many propositions does an exceptive proposition resolve?
Choices:
- Three
- Two
- One
- Four
Answer: Three
Why: “Every A except B is C” says that every A other than B is C, that B is an A, and that B is not C.
Why not the others: An exclusive resolves into two; an exceptive adds that the excepted thing belongs to the subject.

@question 7.3
Question: Why is “Every fish except the whale lives in water” false?
Choices:
- The whale is not a fish
- The whale does not live in water
- Some fish live on land
- An exceptive cannot be universal
Answer: The whale is not a fish
Why: An exceptive requires that the excepted thing belong to the subject, and the whale is a mammal.
Why not the others: The fault is in the exception: the whale is not a fish at all.

@card 8

**Reduplicative propositions.** A **reduplicative** proposition contains “as,” “insofar as,” or “as such”: “The wicked, as wicked, are to be hated.” Sometimes the word names the very nature of the subject, so that the predicate is said to belong to the subject by its own nature: “The colored, as colored, is visible.” Sometimes it names a different feature of the subject, which is the reason or condition for the predicate: “Man, as rational, can laugh; as animal, he can be nourished.” This is the reduplicative in the strict sense.

Mistaking a reduplicative for a simple proposition causes many errors. “The wicked, as wicked, are to be hated” does not say that the wicked are to be hated simply, since as men they are to be loved. And “Man as man is not greedy” (greed is not part of what man is) differs from “Man as man is non-greedy” (freedom from greed belongs to his nature): man as man is neither greedy nor free of greed.

@question 8.1
Question: “The doctor, as doctor, aims at health.” Which feature does “as doctor” name?
Choices:
- The very nature by which the predicate belongs
- A feature with no bearing on the predicate
- The place where the doctor works
- A figure of speech for the doctor
Answer: The very nature by which the predicate belongs
Why: The doctor aims at health by the very art that makes him a doctor.
Why not the others: “As doctor” gives the reason for the predicate; it is neither irrelevant nor a figure.

@question 8.2
Question: “Man, as animal, can be nourished.” What does “as animal” supply?
Choices:
- The feature by reason of which the predicate belongs
- The whole definition of man
- A restriction of the subject to some men only, not all
- The cause of man’s rationality
Answer: The feature by reason of which the predicate belongs
Why: In the strict reduplicative, “as” names the feature that is the reason for the predicate.
Why not the others: Animal is not the whole definition, and it does not restrict the subject to some men.

@question 8.3
Question: Why must “The enemy, as enemy, is to be resisted” not be read as a simple proposition?
Choices:
- As men, enemies are still to be loved
- Enemies are not to be resisted at all
- It is a conditional in disguise
- It has no subject
Answer: As men, enemies are still to be loved
Why: The reduplication limits the predicate to the enemy as enemy; read simply, it would say too much.
Why not the others: The proposition is true as reduplicative; read simply, it would forget that the enemy is also a man.


########################################################################
# Figures and Moods
########################################################################

@deck adv-fig
Title: Figures and Moods
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Figure and mood; the special rules and principle of each figure and what each is good for; the nineteen moods, the indirect moods, and the later account’s fourth figure; singular premises and the expository syllogism; what a syllogism essentially is; and why the first figure is the figure of science.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.4–7 and *Posterior Analytics* I.14; Pierre Conway, *Aristotelian Formal and Material Logic*.

**Figure and mood.** A syllogism has a double matter, its terms and its propositions, and so a double form. The **figure** is the arrangement of the terms, fixed by the place of the middle term: subject of the major and predicate of the minor in the first figure, predicate of both in the second, subject of both in the third. The **mood** is the arrangement of the propositions by quantity and quality, named by the letters A, E, I, and O for the major, the minor, and the conclusion in order.

Each premise can be A, E, I, or O, so there are sixteen pairs of premises, and since the conclusion can be A, E, I, or O as well, there are sixty-four combinations. Most break one of the general rules. The scholastic account counts nineteen legitimate moods: four in the first figure, five indirect moods of the first figure, four in the second, and six in the third. The later textbook account counts the indirect moods as a fourth figure and adds the weakened moods, which conclude in particular what could be concluded universally.

@question 1.1
Question: What fixes the figure of a syllogism?
Choices:
- The place of the middle term
- The quantity of the premises
- The quality of the conclusion
- The number of terms used
Answer: The place of the middle term
Why: The figure is the arrangement of the terms, fixed by where the middle term stands.
Why not the others: Quantity and quality give the mood; every syllogism has three terms.

@question 1.2
Question: How many legitimate moods does the scholastic account count?
Choices:
- Nineteen
- Sixty-four
- Twenty-four
- Sixteen
Answer: Nineteen
Why: Four in the first figure, five indirect, four in the second, and six in the third.
Why not the others: Sixty-four are the combinations before the rules are applied; twenty-four is the later count with the weakened moods.

@question 1.3
Question: What does the later textbook account add to the scholastic count?
Choices:
- A fourth figure and the weakened moods
- A fifth figure for singular terms
- Moods with two negative premises
- Moods with four terms
Answer: A fourth figure and the weakened moods
Why: It counts the indirect moods as a fourth figure and adds moods that conclude particularly what could be concluded universally.
Why not the others: No account admits two negative premises or four terms, and there is no fifth figure.

@card 2

**The first figure.** In the first figure the major premise must be universal and the minor affirmative. If the major were particular, the middle term would be distributed in neither premise; if the minor were negative, the conclusion would be negative and would distribute the major term, which the affirmative major leaves undistributed. So four moods remain: AAA, EAE, AII, and EIO (Barbara, Celarent, Darii, Ferio).

These moods are called perfect because the principle of all syllogisms is seen in them directly. The principle is that what is said of every member of a class is said of whatever falls under it, and what is denied of every member is denied of whatever falls under it (the *dictum de omni et nullo*). In the first figure the middle term is the subject of the major and the predicate of the minor, so the minor term is plainly placed under the middle, and the major term is said or denied of it through the middle.

@question 2.1
Question: Why must the minor premise be affirmative in the first figure?
Choices:
- A negative minor would distribute the major term in the conclusion only
- A negative minor would leave the middle term undistributed in both premises
- A negative minor would make the conclusion particular
- A negative minor would require a singular subject
Answer: A negative minor would distribute the major term in the conclusion only
Why: The conclusion would be negative and distribute the major term, which the affirmative major leaves undistributed.
Why not the others: The fault is the major term distributed in the conclusion but not in its premise, not the middle term or the quantity.

@question 2.2
Question: “Every planet moves; some star is a planet; so some star moves.” In which mood is this?
Choices:
- AII in the first figure
- AAA in the first figure
- IAI in the third figure
- AII in the third figure
Answer: AII in the first figure
