Why: The middle term, “planet,” is subject of the major and predicate of the minor, and the propositions are A, I, I.
Why not the others: The minor and conclusion are particular, so not AAA; and the middle term’s place makes it the first figure.

@question 2.3
Question: What principle governs the first figure directly?
Choices:
- What is said of every member of a class is said of whatever falls under it
- Two things that share a single instance agree with each other at least in part
- What an attribute is denied of lies outside its class
- Whatever is true of a part is true of the whole
Answer: What is said of every member of a class is said of whatever falls under it
Why: This principle, what is said of every member is said of each one under it, is seen directly in the first figure.
Why not the others: The second and third options give the principles of the second and third figures; the last is not a rule of the syllogism.

@card 3

**The second figure.** In the second figure one premise must be negative and the major must be universal. If both premises were affirmative, the middle term, which is the predicate of both, would be distributed in neither; and a particular major would leave the major term undistributed while the negative conclusion distributes it. So four moods remain: EAE, AEE, EIO, and AOO (Cesare, Camestres, Festino, Baroco), and every conclusion of the second figure is negative.

The principle of this figure is that if an attribute is said or denied of every member of one class, whatever it cannot be said or denied of in the same way is not in that class (sometimes called the *dictum de diverso*). The second figure is therefore suited to refutation and to exclusion, as when we show that something is not a fish because it does not breathe by gills, while every fish does.

@question 3.1
Question: Why must one premise be negative in the second figure?
Choices:
- Otherwise the middle term, predicate of both, is never distributed
- Otherwise the major term would have to be a singular term in the conclusion
- Otherwise the conclusion would be universal
- Otherwise there would be four terms
Answer: Otherwise the middle term, predicate of both, is never distributed
Why: The middle term is the predicate of both premises, and the predicate of an affirmative is undistributed.
Why not the others: The rule concerns distribution of the middle term, not singular terms, quantity, or the number of terms.

@question 3.2
Question: What is true of every conclusion in the second figure?
Choices:
- It is negative
- It is universal
- It is particular
- It is affirmative
Answer: It is negative
Why: One premise is negative, so the conclusion is negative.
Why not the others: The second figure has universal and particular conclusions, but no affirmative ones.

@question 3.3
Question: For what is the second figure especially suited?
Choices:
- Showing that something does not belong to a class
- Showing that a property belongs to a whole species
- Finding a single counterexample to a universal
- Combining several definitions into one
Answer: Showing that something does not belong to a class
Why: Its negative conclusions exclude a thing from a class, which suits refutation.
Why not the others: Universal affirmatives belong to the first figure, and counterexamples to the third.

@card 4

**The third figure.** In the third figure the minor premise must be affirmative and the conclusion particular. A negative minor would make the conclusion negative and distribute the major term, which the affirmative major leaves undistributed; and a universal conclusion would distribute the minor term, which is the predicate of an affirmative minor and so undistributed. Six moods remain: AAI, EAO, IAI, AII, OAO, and EIO (Darapti, Felapton, Disamis, Datisi, Bocardo, Ferison).

The principle of this figure is that two terms that share a common instance agree at least in part, and if one has a part the other lacks, they differ at least in part (sometimes called the *dictum de exemplo*). So the third figure is suited to the counterexample, which shows that a universal claim does not hold: some swan is not white, since this bird is a swan and this bird is not white.

@question 4.1
Question: Why must the conclusion be particular in the third figure?
Choices:
- The minor term is undistributed in its affirmative premise
- The middle term is left undistributed in both of the premises of the figure
- The major premise must always be particular
- Both premises must always be negative
Answer: The minor term is undistributed in its affirmative premise
Why: The minor term is the predicate of the affirmative minor, so it cannot be distributed in the conclusion.
Why not the others: The middle term is the subject of both premises and can be distributed; the premises need not be particular or negative.

@question 4.2
Question: “Some doctor is not wealthy; every doctor is educated; so some educated person is not wealthy.” In which mood is this?
Choices:
- OAO in the third figure
- EAO in the third figure
- AOO in the second figure
- OAO in the first figure
Answer: OAO in the third figure
Why: The middle term, “doctor,” is the subject of both premises, and the propositions are O, A, O.
Why not the others: The major is O, not E; and the middle term’s place makes it the third figure.

@question 4.3
Question: For what is the third figure especially suited?
Choices:
- Disproving a universal by a counterexample
- Proving universal affirmative conclusions
- Showing why a property belongs to a species
- Excluding a thing from every class
Answer: Disproving a universal by a counterexample
Why: Its particular conclusions show that a universal does not hold.
Why not the others: It cannot conclude universally, so it cannot give the reason a property belongs to a whole species.

@card 5

**The indirect moods and the fourth figure.** When the middle term is the predicate of the major and the subject of the minor, the syllogism concludes indirectly: the term that is naturally the predicate becomes the grammatical subject of the conclusion, as in “Some mortal thing is Peter.” The scholastics, following Aristotle, counted these as five indirect moods of the first figure (Baralipton, Celantes, Dabitis, Fapesmo, Frisesomorum), since they are the first figure with the conclusion turned around.

The later textbook account counts the same arrangement as a separate fourth figure, with the moods AAI, AEE, IAI, EAO, and EIO (Bramantip, Camenes, Dimaris, Fesapo, Fresison). Historians of logic report that this fourth figure was attributed to Galen, the second-century physician. The Aristotelian and Thomist tradition, including St. Thomas and John of St. Thomas, does not count it as a distinct figure but treats it as the indirect first figure; in Maritain’s words, it is “in reality but the first figure concluding indirectly by the transposition of the premisses.” Maritain argues that a fourth figure attends to the words rather than to the thought, since in the mind the grammatical predicate of its conclusion is the real subject.

@question 5.1
Question: Why did the scholastics not count a fourth figure?
Choices:
- They treated its moods as the first figure concluding indirectly
- They did not know of its moods at all
- They thought its moods were all invalid, since they conclude indirectly
- They counted only moods with universal conclusions
Answer: They treated its moods as the first figure concluding indirectly
Why: The indirect moods are the first figure with the conclusion turned around, so they were counted with the first.
Why not the others: The scholastics knew and named these moods; they classified them differently.

@question 5.2
Question: Where is the middle term in the indirect moods?
Choices:
- Predicate of the major and subject of the minor
- Subject of the major and predicate of the minor
- Predicate of both premises
- Subject of both premises
Answer: Predicate of the major and subject of the minor
Why: That arrangement is the indirect one, which the later account calls the fourth figure.
Why not the others: The other arrangements are the first, second, and third figures.

@question 5.3
Question: On Maritain’s view, what does counting a fourth figure attend to?
Choices:
- The words rather than the thought
- The thought rather than the words
- The truth of the premises
- The number of the premises
Answer: The words rather than the thought
Why: In the mind the grammatical predicate of the indirect conclusion is the real subject.
Why not the others: Maritain’s objection concerns attending to grammar, not truth or the number of premises.

@card 6

**Singular premises.** A singular minor premise counts as universal, since it speaks of the whole of its subject: “Every man is mortal; Socrates is a man; so Socrates is mortal” is in Barbara. But a singular major does not count as a universal. “This man is a liar; Peter is a man; so Peter is a liar” does not conclude, since “man” in the minor is not the man spoken of in the major.

When the middle term itself is singular, the argument is an **expository syllogism**, which simply displays an individual in whom two terms meet. “Socrates is wise; Socrates is a man; so some man is wise” is in the third figure, which suits it best. Because its middle stands for one individual, it admits arrangements the ordinary syllogism forbids, such as two affirmatives in the second figure: “This apostle is Judas; this traitor is Judas; so this traitor is an apostle.”

@question 6.1
Question: “Every bird has feathers; this robin is a bird; so this robin has feathers.” In which mood is this?
Choices:
- AAA in the first figure
- AII in the first figure
- AAI in the third figure
- IAI in the third figure
Answer: AAA in the first figure
Why: A singular minor counts as universal, so the mood is AAA.
Why not the others: The singular minor is treated as universal, not particular.

@question 6.2
Question: “This dog bites; Rex is a dog; so Rex bites.” Does this conclude?
Choices:
- No, since a singular major does not count as universal
- Yes, since singular premises count as universal
- Yes, since it is in the first figure
- No, since it has two singular premises
Answer: No, since a singular major does not count as universal
Why: “Dog” in the minor need not be the dog spoken of in the major, so nothing follows.
Why not the others: Only a singular minor is treated as universal; a singular major is not.

@question 6.3
Question: What is an expository syllogism?
Choices:
- One whose middle term is singular
- One with a premise left unsaid
- One with a causal premise
- One with more than three terms
Answer: One whose middle term is singular
Why: It displays one individual in whom two terms meet.
Why not the others: A missing premise makes an enthymeme, a causal premise an epicheirema, and four terms no syllogism at all.

@card 7

**What a syllogism is.** It is often said that a syllogism moves from a more universal truth to a less universal one. Maritain grants that this is true of the logical relations, since in the first figure the minor term is placed under the middle; but he denies that it is the essence of the syllogism. The essence is the identification of two terms with one and the same third term, from which their identity with each other follows.

The proof is that the conclusion can be as universal as the major. “Every rational being is made to live in society; every man is a rational being; so every man is made to live in society” is a syllogism in Barbara, and its terms are convertible: its conclusion is as universal as its premises. Aristotle and the scholastics took such syllogisms, where the middle term is the definition of the subject, as the model of demonstration.

@question 7.1
Question: On Maritain’s account, what is the essence of the syllogism?
Choices:
- Two terms identified with one third term
- A descent from the more universal to the less
- A rule written in symbols
- A conclusion more universal than the premises
Answer: Two terms identified with one third term
Why: The two extremes are identified with the same middle, and so with each other.
Why not the others: The descent from more to less universal is a property of the logical relations, not the essence.

@question 7.2
Question: “Every equilateral triangle is equiangular; every triangle with three equal sides is equilateral; so every triangle with three equal sides is equiangular.” What does this show?
Choices:
- A conclusion can be as universal as its major
- A syllogism must descend to a smaller class
- Convertible terms make a syllogism invalid
- The conclusion is always less universal
Answer: A conclusion can be as universal as its major
Why: Its terms are convertible, and the conclusion is as universal as the major, as Maritain argues.
Why not the others: The syllogism is valid in Barbara, though it does not descend to a smaller class.

@question 7.3
Question: Which syllogisms did Aristotle and the scholastics take as the model of demonstration?
Choices:
- Those whose middle term is the definition of the subject
- Those with particular conclusions
- Those in the third figure
- Those with singular premises
Answer: Those whose middle term is the definition of the subject
Why: A middle term that defines the subject shows why the property belongs to it.
Why not the others: Particular conclusions, the third figure, and singular premises do not state a universal property.

@card 8

**The figure of science.** Aristotle holds that the first figure is the most suited to science (*Posterior Analytics* I.14). Science seeks the reason why a property belongs to a subject, and only the first figure concludes universally and affirmatively, in Barbara, which is the form of “Every triangle has angles equal to two right angles.” The second figure concludes only negatively, and the third only particularly, so neither can state that a property belongs to every member of a species.

The first figure is also the most direct, since the middle term stands between the extremes in extension. The other figures keep their uses: the second for showing what a thing is not, and the third for disproving a universal. Pierre Conway, the twentieth-century Dominican teacher of Aristotelian logic, adds that Barbara and Celarent are the moods proper to demonstration, since through them a definition or a cause is shown to bring its property with it.

@question 8.1
Question: Why is the first figure the most suited to science?
Choices:
- Only it concludes universally and affirmatively
- Only it uses three terms
- Only it has negative conclusions
- Only it admits singular premises
Answer: Only it concludes universally and affirmatively
Why: Science shows that a property belongs to every member of a species, which needs a universal affirmative conclusion.
Why not the others: Every figure uses three terms, and the second figure is the one whose conclusions are all negative.

@question 8.2
Question: Why can the third figure not demonstrate a property of a whole species?
Choices:
- Its conclusions are all particular
- Its conclusions are all negative
- It has no middle term
- It concludes indirectly
Answer: Its conclusions are all particular
Why: A property of a whole species needs a universal conclusion, and the third figure gives only particular ones.
Why not the others: Third-figure conclusions can be affirmative, and it has a middle term like every syllogism.

@question 8.3
Question: Which moods are proper to demonstration, on Conway’s account?
Choices:
- Barbara and Celarent
- Darapti and Felapton
- Baroco and Bocardo
- Disamis and Datisi
Answer: Barbara and Celarent
Why: In these first-figure moods a definition or cause is shown to bring its property with it.
Why not the others: The others are imperfect moods of the second and third figures.


########################################################################
# Reduction
########################################################################

@deck adv-red
Title: Reduction
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Perfect and imperfect moods; what the letters in the names of the moods mean; direct reduction by simple conversion, by limitation, and by transposition; reduction to the impossible; the indirect moods; and why the imperfect moods are valid in their own right.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.4–7; John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963).

**Perfect and imperfect moods.** Aristotle calls the moods of the first figure **perfect**, because their force is evident from the premises alone: what is said of every member of a class is said of whatever falls under it. He calls the moods of the other figures **imperfect**, because their force becomes evident through something not stated in the premises, such as the conversion of a premise or an argument from the impossible.

To **reduce** an imperfect mood is to show its validity by a perfect one. Every A conclusion is drawn most perfectly in AAA of the first figure (Barbara), every E conclusion in EAE (Celarent), every I in AII (Darii), and every O in EIO (Ferio). Reduction is either **direct**, drawing the same conclusion in a first-figure mood, or **to the impossible**, showing that whoever grants the premises and denies the conclusion contradicts himself.

@question 1.1
Question: Why does Aristotle call the moods of the second and third figures imperfect?
Choices:
- Their force becomes evident through something not stated
- They are invalid unless a further premise is added to them first
- They have more than three terms
- They conclude only particular propositions
Answer: Their force becomes evident through something not stated
Why: Their validity is made evident by a conversion or an argument from the impossible, which the premises do not state.
Why not the others: Imperfect moods are valid, have three terms, and some conclude universally.

@question 1.2
Question: To which perfect mood is every E conclusion best reduced?
Choices:
- EAE in the first figure (Celarent)
- AAA in the first figure (Barbara)
- AII in the first figure (Darii)
- EIO in the first figure (Ferio)
Answer: EAE in the first figure (Celarent)
Why: Every E conclusion is drawn most perfectly in EAE of the first figure.
Why not the others: Barbara concludes A, Darii I, and Ferio O.

@question 1.3
Question: What is reduction to the impossible?
Choices:
- Showing that denying the conclusion contradicts a granted premise
- Showing that the premises themselves are impossible and must be denied
- Converting both premises simply
- Replacing the middle term with a new one
Answer: Showing that denying the conclusion contradicts a granted premise
Why: Whoever grants the premises and denies the conclusion is shown to contradict himself.
Why not the others: It does not show the premises false or change the terms; it shows the denial of the conclusion impossible.

@card 2

**What the letters mean.** The Latin names of the moods record how each is reduced. The first letter tells which perfect mood it goes to: a name beginning with B goes to Barbara, with C to Celarent, with D to Darii, and with F to Ferio. Within the name, **s** after a vowel means that the proposition is converted simply; **p** means that it is converted by limitation (*per accidens*); **m** means that the premises are transposed; and **c** means that the mood is reduced to the impossible.

The scholastics summed this up in a verse: “S vult simpliciter verti, P vero per accid, M vult mutari, C per impossibile duci.” So Cesare (EAE in the second figure) goes to Celarent by converting its major simply, and Datisi (AII in the third) goes to Darii by converting its minor simply. The other letters in the names, such as the r and the t, mark nothing.

@question 2.1
Question: A mood’s name begins with D. To which perfect mood is it reduced?
Choices:
- Darii
- Barbara
- Celarent
- Ferio
Answer: Darii
Why: The initial letter names the perfect mood: D goes to Darii.
Why not the others: B goes to Barbara, C to Celarent, and F to Ferio.

@question 2.2
Question: What does the letter p in a mood’s name mean?
Choices:
- Convert that proposition by limitation
- Convert that proposition simply
- Transpose the premises
- Reduce the mood to the impossible
Answer: Convert that proposition by limitation
Why: P marks conversion by limitation, s simple conversion, m transposition, and c the impossible.
Why not the others: Each letter marks one operation; p is conversion by limitation.

@question 2.3
Question: What does the letter m in a mood’s name mean?
Choices:
- Transpose the premises
- Convert the major simply
- Convert the minor by limitation
- Deny the conclusion
Answer: Transpose the premises
Why: M (for *mutari*) means that the premises change places.
Why not the others: Conversion is marked by s and p, and the impossible by c.

@card 3

**Direct reduction by simple conversion and transposition.** Take a syllogism in AEE of the second figure (Camestres): “Every jealous man is bitter; no saint is bitter; so no saint is a jealous man.” Its name begins with C, so it goes to Celarent. The m tells us to transpose the premises, and each s tells us to convert simply the proposition it follows, here the minor and the conclusion.

Transposing gives “No saint is bitter” as the first premise; converting it gives “No bitter man is a saint.” Then “Every jealous man is bitter” stands as the minor, and the conclusion is “No jealous man is a saint,” which is Celarent. Converting that conclusion simply gives back “No saint is a jealous man,” the original conclusion. The same conclusion has been drawn in a perfect mood, and so the imperfect one is shown to be valid.

@question 3.1
Question: EAE in the second figure (Cesare): “No fish is a mammal; every whale is a mammal; so no whale is a fish.” How is it reduced?
Choices:
- Convert the major simply, giving Celarent
- Transpose the premises, giving Barbara
- Convert the minor by limitation, giving Ferio
- Reduce it to the impossible, giving Darii
Answer: Convert the major simply, giving Celarent
Why: The s after the first vowel calls for simple conversion of the major: “No mammal is a fish,” and the syllogism is in Celarent.
Why not the others: Cesare begins with C, so it goes to Celarent, and its only operation is the simple conversion of the major.

@question 3.2
Question: In AEE of the second figure (Camestres), which propositions are converted simply?
Choices:
- The minor and the conclusion
- The major and the minor
- The major only
- The conclusion only
Answer: The minor and the conclusion
Why: The s after the second and third vowels marks the minor and the conclusion.
Why not the others: In “Camestres” the s’s follow the E of the minor and the E of the conclusion.

@question 3.3
Question: After a direct reduction of Camestres, why is the conclusion converted again?
Choices:
- To recover the original conclusion
- To make the syllogism valid, which it was not before
- To change its quality
- To add a fourth term
Answer: To recover the original conclusion
Why: The reduced syllogism concludes the converse; converting it gives back the original conclusion.
Why not the others: The reduced syllogism is already valid, and simple conversion of E changes neither quality nor terms.

@card 4

**Reduction by conversion by limitation.** Some reductions need conversion by limitation. Take EAO in the third figure (Felapton): “No animal is incorruptible; every animal is a living being; so some living being is not incorruptible.” Its name begins with F, so it goes to Ferio, and the p after the second vowel tells us to convert the minor by limitation.

“Every animal is a living being” becomes “Some living being is an animal.” With the major unchanged, we have “No animal is incorruptible; some living being is an animal; so some living being is not incorruptible,” which is EIO in the first figure (Ferio). In the same way AAI of the third figure (Darapti) goes to Darii by converting its minor by limitation.

@question 4.1
Question: AAI in the third figure (Darapti): “Every square is a rectangle; every square is a rhombus; so some rhombus is a rectangle.” How is it reduced?
Choices:
- Convert the minor by limitation, giving Darii
- Convert the major simply, giving Celarent
- Transpose the premises, giving Barbara
- Reduce it to the impossible, giving Ferio
Answer: Convert the minor by limitation, giving Darii
Why: “Every square is a rhombus” becomes “Some rhombus is a square,” and the syllogism is in Darii.
Why not the others: Darapti begins with D, so it goes to Darii, and the p marks conversion of the minor by limitation.

@question 4.2
Question: Why must Darapti’s minor be converted by limitation and not simply?
Choices:
- An A proposition does not convert simply
- An I proposition does not convert at all
- The minor is negative
- The minor is singular
Answer: An A proposition does not convert simply
Why: A universal affirmative converts only by limitation, since its predicate is not distributed.
Why not the others: Darapti’s minor is an A, affirmative and universal; I converts simply.

@question 4.3
Question: Felapton goes to which perfect mood?
Choices:
- Ferio
- Celarent
- Darii
- Barbara
Answer: Ferio
Why: Its name begins with F, so it goes to Ferio.
Why not the others: The initial letter decides: F goes to Ferio.

@card 5

**Reduction to the impossible.** Two moods, AOO in the second figure (Baroco) and OAO in the third (Bocardo), cannot be reduced directly, since an O cannot be converted simply. They are reduced to the impossible. We suppose an opponent who grants the premises but denies the conclusion. We take the contradictory of the conclusion, put it in place of the premise marked by the c, and draw a conclusion in Barbara that contradicts the other premise, which the opponent had granted.

Take Baroco: “Every fool is tiresome; some talker is not tiresome; so some talker is not a fool.” If the conclusion is denied, then every talker is a fool. Joined to “Every fool is tiresome,” this gives in Barbara “Every talker is tiresome,” which contradicts the granted minor “Some talker is not tiresome.” So the opponent contradicts himself, and the conclusion must be granted. Any imperfect mood can be reduced this way, but these two can be reduced in no other.

@question 5.1
Question: Why can Baroco and Bocardo not be reduced directly?
Choices:
- An O proposition cannot be converted simply
- They have two negative premises and so conclude nothing
- They are invalid moods
- They have singular premises
Answer: An O proposition cannot be converted simply
Why: Their O premise cannot be converted to fit the first figure, so only reduction to the impossible remains.
Why not the others: They are valid, with one negative premise each; the obstacle is the O.

@question 5.2
Question: In reducing Bocardo (OAO in the third figure) to the impossible, which premise is replaced?
Choices:
- The major, marked by the c
- The minor, marked by the c
- Both premises
- Neither premise
Answer: The major, marked by the c
Why: In “Bocardo” the c follows the O of the major, so the contradictory of the conclusion replaces the major.
Why not the others: In Baroco the c follows the minor; in Bocardo it follows the major.

@question 5.3
Question: In a reduction to the impossible, what does the new conclusion contradict?
Choices:
- The premise that was kept and granted
- The contradictory just assumed
- The original conclusion
- The middle term
Answer: The premise that was kept and granted
Why: The conclusion drawn in Barbara contradicts the premise that the opponent granted and that was not replaced.
Why not the others: The assumed contradictory is a premise of the new syllogism; what it leads to contradicts the other granted premise.

@card 6

**The indirect moods reduced.** The indirect moods of the first figure are reduced by restoring to the first premise its proper role as the major. A syllogism in Celantes, “No philosopher is an angel; Descartes is a philosopher; so no angel is Descartes,” becomes Celarent by reading the conclusion directly: “Descartes is not an angel.” A syllogism in Baralipton, “Every artist is imaginative; every poet is an artist; so something imaginative is a poet,” becomes Barbara, “so every poet is imaginative.”

Maritain notes that this works because the indirect conclusion already was the direct one, stated with its terms turned around. In the later account, where these moods form a fourth figure, they are reduced by transposition and conversion: AAI of the fourth figure (Bramantip) goes to Barbara by transposing the premises and converting the conclusion by limitation.

@question 6.1
Question: How is a syllogism in an indirect mood reduced?
Choices:
- By giving the first premise its proper role as the major
- By converting both premises by limitation and keeping the conclusion
- By reducing it to the impossible
- By adding a third premise
Answer: By giving the first premise its proper role as the major
Why: Restoring the first premise as the major and reading the conclusion directly gives a first-figure mood.
Why not the others: The indirect moods need no third premise and are not reduced to the impossible.

@question 6.2
Question: Why does reading the indirect conclusion directly work, on Maritain’s account?
Choices:
- It already was the direct conclusion with its terms turned around
- The indirect conclusion is always false and so must be replaced by another
- The direct conclusion is weaker
- The terms have changed their meaning
Answer: It already was the direct conclusion with its terms turned around
Why: The indirect conclusion states the direct one with the grammatical predicate as subject.
Why not the others: The two conclusions say the same thing; nothing is false, weaker, or changed in meaning.

@question 6.3
Question: In the later account, AAI of the fourth figure (Bramantip) goes to which perfect mood?
Choices:
- Barbara
- Celarent
- Darii
- Ferio
Answer: Barbara
Why: Its name begins with B, so it goes to Barbara, by transposing the premises and converting the conclusion by limitation.
Why not the others: The initial B decides the perfect mood.

@card 7

**Valid in their own right.** Reduction does not mean that the imperfect moods borrow their validity from the first figure. Maritain reads Aristotle as saying that their evidence depends on a principle more particular than the principle of the first figure, not that they prove nothing by themselves. A syllogism in the second figure concludes by its own form, and the reduction verifies it by showing the same truth in a perfect form.

The figures also keep their own uses, which reduction would hide: the second figure is the natural form of a refutation, and the third the natural form of a counterexample. So reduction is a test and an explanation of validity, not a rule that every argument should be rewritten in the first figure.

@question 7.1
Question: Does an imperfect mood borrow its validity from the first figure?
Choices:
- No, reduction verifies a validity it already has
- Yes, it is valid only once it has been reduced to the first figure
- Yes, since it is invalid by itself
- No, since it is not valid at all
Answer: No, reduction verifies a validity it already has
Why: An imperfect mood concludes by its own form; reduction shows the same truth in a perfect form.
Why not the others: The imperfect moods are valid by themselves; reduction is a test and an explanation.

@question 7.2
Question: Why should not every argument be rewritten in the first figure?
Choices:
- The other figures have their own natural uses
- The first figure is the least clear
- Reduction always changes the conclusion
- The first figure cannot conclude negatively
Answer: The other figures have their own natural uses
Why: The second figure suits refutation and the third the counterexample, which reduction would hide.
Why not the others: The first figure is the clearest and can conclude negatively; the point is that other figures have their uses.

@question 7.3
Question: On Maritain’s reading, what does the evidence of an imperfect mood depend on?
Choices:
- A principle more particular than that of the first figure
- A further premise that has been left unstated by the arguer
- The truth of its conclusion
- The authority of Aristotle
Answer: A principle more particular than that of the first figure
Why: Each imperfect figure applies the general principle through a more particular one.
Why not the others: Nothing is left unstated, and validity does not depend on the conclusion’s truth or on authority.


########################################################################
# The Modal Syllogism in Depth
########################################################################

@deck adv-mods
Title: The Modal Syllogism in Depth
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

Why Aristotle’s first-figure rule holds and why the reverse mixture fails; the necessity of the consequence and of the consequent; St. Thomas’s distinction applied to a syllogism; converting modal premises; one-sided possibility; and why the theory was called the logicians’ cross.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.3, 9, 13–17; St. Albert the Great, commentary on the *Prior Analytics*, bk. I, tract. 3, ch. 3; Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, “Medieval Theories of Modality” (on Kilwardby); St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, bk. I, lect. 14, and commentary on the *Sentences* I, d. 38, q. 1, a. 5, ad 5; Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides).

**Why the first-figure rule holds.** Aristotle holds that in the first figure a necessary major premise with a plain minor premise gives a necessary conclusion (*Prior Analytics* I.9). The reason is the same principle that governs the first figure. If a predicate belongs necessarily to everything under the middle term, it belongs necessarily to whatever falls under the middle term; and the plain minor premise places the minor term under the middle.

So “Every metal necessarily conducts electricity; this wire is metal; so this wire necessarily conducts electricity” concludes necessarily, though the minor premise is plain, since the wire might have been made of something else. The necessity in the conclusion is the necessity with which the property follows from being metal; the minor premise contributes only that the wire falls under “metal,” which is enough.

@question 1.1
Question: “Every circle is necessarily round; this plate is a circle; so this plate is ____.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Necessarily round
- Round, but only as a plain assertion
- Contingently round
- Nothing follows
Answer: Necessarily round
Why: In the first figure a necessary major with a plain minor gives a necessary conclusion.
Why not the others: By Aristotle’s rule the plain minor does not weaken the conclusion here; the necessity comes from the major.

@question 1.2
Question: In such a syllogism, what does the plain minor premise contribute?
Choices:
- That the minor term falls under the middle term
- The necessity of the conclusion
- The quality of the conclusion
- A second middle term
Answer: That the minor term falls under the middle term
Why: The minor places the subject under the middle; the major supplies how the predicate belongs.
Why not the others: The necessity comes from the major, and there is only one middle term.

@question 1.3
Question: On which principle does Aristotle’s first-figure rule rest?
Choices:
- What is necessary of all under the middle is necessary of what falls under it
- The conclusion always follows the weaker premise in every respect
- A necessary premise of any kind makes every conclusion necessary, in every figure alike
- A plain premise can never appear in a necessary syllogism
Answer: What is necessary of all under the middle is necessary of what falls under it
Why: The principle of the first figure, applied to necessity, gives the rule.
Why not the others: The weaker-premise rule is Theophrastus’s, and Aristotle’s rule holds in the first figure with the necessary major.

@card 2

**Why the reverse mixture fails.** The reverse does not hold: a plain major premise with a necessary minor premise gives only a plain conclusion. Aristotle’s example sets motion, animal, and man as the terms (*Prior Analytics* I.9). Every man is necessarily an animal, and suppose that every animal is in fact moving; it follows that every man is moving, but not that every man is necessarily moving, since neither animal nor man moves necessarily.

The principle explains the difference. The major premise states how the predicate belongs to everything under the middle term, and what is said of the middle is said of what falls under it in the same way. A necessary minor only makes the minor term fall under the middle necessarily; it cannot make the predicate belong to the middle any more firmly than the major says it does.

@question 2.1
Question: “Every swimmer is in fact wet; every fish is necessarily a swimmer; so every fish is ____.” In what mode does the conclusion follow?
Choices:
- Wet, as a plain assertion
- Necessarily wet
- Contingently wet
- Nothing follows
Answer: Wet, as a plain assertion
Why: A plain major with a necessary minor gives only a plain conclusion.
Why not the others: The major says only that the predicate belongs, not that it must; a necessary minor cannot add that.

@question 2.2
Question: Why can a necessary minor not make the conclusion necessary?
Choices:
- It cannot make the predicate belong to the middle more firmly
- A necessary minor is always false
- The minor never contains the middle term
- Necessity cannot appear in a minor premise of the first figure at all
Answer: It cannot make the predicate belong to the middle more firmly
Why: How the predicate belongs to what falls under the middle depends on how it belongs to the middle, which the major states.
Why not the others: The minor does contain the middle term and can be necessary; it simply cannot strengthen the major.

@question 2.3
Question: In Aristotle’s example, which proposition is necessary?
Choices:
- Every man is an animal
- Every animal is moving
- Every man is moving
- No animal is moving
Answer: Every man is an animal
Why: Man is necessarily an animal, but neither animal nor man moves necessarily.
Why not the others: Moving belongs to animals and men in fact, not of necessity.

@card 3

**The necessity of the consequence.** Every valid syllogism has a kind of necessity: if the premises are true, the conclusion cannot be false. St. Thomas calls this the **necessity of the consequence**, and he distinguishes it from the **necessity of the consequent**, the necessity of the conclusion taken by itself. From contingent premises a conclusion follows necessarily, in the first sense; but the conclusion need not be necessary in the second sense (commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, bk. I, lect. 14).

The two must not be confused. “If Socrates is sitting, he is necessarily sitting” is true of the consequence (it cannot be that he sits and does not sit) and false of the consequent (his sitting is not necessary). A modal syllogism concerns the second kind: whether the conclusion is itself necessary or contingent, not whether it follows.

@question 3.1
Question: “If the door is open, it is necessarily open.” In which sense is this true?
Choices:
- Of the consequence
- Of the consequent
- In both senses
- In neither sense
Answer: Of the consequence
Why: It cannot be that the door is open and not open, but its being open is not necessary in itself.
Why not the others: The open door could have been shut, so the consequent taken alone is not necessary.

@question 3.2
Question: From contingent premises, does a conclusion follow necessarily?
Choices:
- Yes, with the necessity of the consequence
- No, nothing follows from contingent premises
- Yes, and the conclusion is necessary in itself
- Only in the second figure
Answer: Yes, with the necessity of the consequence
Why: The conclusion follows necessarily, though it need not be necessary by itself.
Why not the others: Necessity of the consequence does not make the conclusion necessary in itself.

@question 3.3
Question: What does a modal syllogism ask about its conclusion?
Choices:
- Whether the conclusion is itself necessary or contingent
- Whether the conclusion follows at all
- Whether the conclusion is singular
- Whether the premises are probable
Answer: Whether the conclusion is itself necessary or contingent
Why: A modal syllogism concerns the necessity of the consequent, not of the consequence.
Why not the others: Whether it follows is the question of validity; the modal question is about the conclusion’s own mode.

@card 4

**What is said, and the thing, in a syllogism.** The distinction between a modal read as said of what is said and as said of the thing decides some modal syllogisms. An old objection runs: “Whatever God knows is necessarily so; God knows that this man will sin; so this man will necessarily sin.” The objection appeals to Aristotle’s first-figure rule, since its major premise is necessary and its minor plain.

St. Thomas answers by distinguishing the major (commentary on the *Sentences* I, d. 38, q. 1, a. 5, ad 5). Said of what is said, the major is true: the statement “What God knows is so” cannot be false. Said of the thing, it is false: the things God knows are not each necessary in themselves. Read the first way, the major does not say that the predicate belongs necessarily to everything under the middle term, and so the rule does not apply. He answers by distinguishing the premise, not by denying the rule.

@question 4.1
Question: How does St. Thomas answer the argument that God’s knowledge makes sins necessary?
Choices:
- He distinguishes the major premise
- He denies Aristotle’s first-figure rule
- He denies that God knows future events
- He grants the conclusion
Answer: He distinguishes the major premise
Why: The major is true of what is said and false of the thing, so the rule does not apply.
Why not the others: St. Thomas keeps the rule and God’s knowledge of the future; he distinguishes the premise.

@question 4.2
Question: Read as said of the thing, “Whatever God knows is necessarily so” would mean what?
Choices:
- Each thing God knows is necessary in itself
- The statement “What God knows is so” cannot be false
- God knows only necessary things
- Nothing God knows is contingent in its cause
Answer: Each thing God knows is necessary in itself
Why: Said of the thing, the mode attaches to each thing known.
Why not the others: “Cannot be false” is the reading of what is said, which is true.

@question 4.3
Question: Why does the first-figure rule not apply when the major is read as said of what is said?
Choices:
- The major is then not necessary of each thing under the middle
- The minor premise is then necessary
- The syllogism is then in the second figure, where the rule does not hold
- The conclusion is then negative
Answer: The major is then not necessary of each thing under the middle
Why: The rule needs a major that is necessary of the things under the middle term.
Why not the others: The figure and the quality are unchanged; the major no longer has the needed sense.

@card 5

**Converting modal premises.** The reduction of modal syllogisms depends on converting modal premises, and Aristotle sets out how they convert (*Prior Analytics* I.3). A necessary universal negative converts simply: if it is necessary that no stone is an animal, it is necessary that no animal is a stone. A necessary universal affirmative converts by limitation: if every man is necessarily an animal, some animal is necessarily a man.

The contingent universal negative behaves differently (I.17). “It is contingent that no man is white,” in the strict sense, means that any man may be white or not white, and it does not convert into “It is contingent that no white thing is a man.” Aristotle shows this just before he finds that two contingent premises give no conclusion in the second figure, where such a conversion would be needed to reduce the syllogism.

@question 5.1
Question: “It is necessary that no circle is a square.” What follows by conversion?
Choices:
- It is necessary that no square is a circle
- It is possible that some square is a circle
- It is necessary that some square is a circle
- Nothing follows by conversion
Answer: It is necessary that no square is a circle
Why: A necessary universal negative converts simply.
Why not the others: The converse keeps the mode and the quality; nothing is said of squares being circles.

@question 5.2
Question: “Every horse is necessarily an animal.” What follows by conversion?
Choices:
- Some animal is necessarily a horse
- Every animal is necessarily a horse
- No animal is necessarily a horse
- Some animal is possibly not a horse
Answer: Some animal is necessarily a horse
Why: A necessary universal affirmative converts by limitation, keeping its mode.
Why not the others: An affirmative universal converts only by limitation, and the mode is kept.

@question 5.3
Question: Which modal proposition does not convert simply, according to Aristotle?
Choices:
- The contingent universal negative
- The necessary universal negative
- The necessary particular affirmative
- The plain universal negative
Answer: The contingent universal negative
Why: In the strict sense, “It is contingent that no S is P” does not convert (Prior Analytics I.17).
Why not the others: Necessary and plain universal negatives convert simply, and so does the particular affirmative.

@card 6

**One-sided possibility in a conclusion.** Since “possible” has two senses, the conclusion of a modal syllogism can be possible in one sense and not in the other. In the first figure, when the major premise is plain and the minor contingent, Aristotle says that the syllogisms are imperfect and that the negative ones conclude not to contingency in the strict sense, but only that the predicate does not belong necessarily (*Prior Analytics* I.15).

The difference is between “can be and can also not be” (contingent, or two-sided) and “need not be” (one-sided). A conclusion of the second kind says less: it denies necessity without asserting that the opposite can also hold. Aristotle’s mixtures with contingent premises differ in this way from mood to mood, and each has to be examined on its own.

@question 6.1
Question: What does a one-sided possible conclusion assert?
Choices:
- That the predicate need not belong
- That the predicate can and can also not belong
- That the predicate must belong
- That the predicate cannot belong
Answer: That the predicate need not belong
Why: The one-sided possible denies necessity without asserting the opposite possibility.
Why not the others: “Can and can also not” is the two-sided, contingent sense.

@question 6.2
Question: In which case does Aristotle find such one-sided conclusions?
Choices:
- First figure, plain major, contingent minor, negative
- First figure, two necessary premises
- Third figure, two plain premises
- Second figure, necessary major, plain minor
Answer: First figure, plain major, contingent minor, negative
Why: With a contingent minor in the first figure the negative syllogisms conclude only that the predicate does not belong necessarily (I.15).
Why not the others: Two necessary premises give a necessary conclusion, and plain premises a plain one.

@question 6.3
Question: Which says less, “It need not rain” or “It can rain and can also not rain”?
Choices:
- “It need not rain”
- “It can rain and can also not rain”
- They say the same
- Neither says anything
Answer: “It need not rain”
Why: “Need not” denies necessity only; the contingent also asserts that it can rain.
Why not the others: The two-sided statement adds the possibility of raining.

@card 7

**The logicians’ cross.** When both premises are necessary, the conclusion is necessary, and the general rules apply without difficulty. The other mixtures led to so many distinctions that the theory of the modal syllogism has been called “the logicians’ cross” (*crux logicorum*), as Maritain reports. The *Summa totius logicae*, once attributed to St. Thomas, follows Aristotle and lists the cases figure by figure. St. Albert the Great, St. Thomas’s teacher, also follows Aristotle, but he adds that the plain minor premise must be true “simply” (*simpliciter*), by the nature of its terms, and not only “as of now” (*ut nunc*); by “simply” he means that the predicate belongs to the subject substantially (commentary on the *Prior Analytics*, bk. I, tract. 3, ch. 3). Historians of logic report that Robert Kilwardby, the thirteenth-century English Dominican, gave the same qualification.

**A short note.** Theophrastus, Aristotle’s successor at the Lyceum, held a simpler rule, that the conclusion always follows the weaker premise in mode. The medieval commentators did not adopt it for the first figure, and this course follows Aristotle.

@question 7.1
Question: What has the theory of the modal syllogism been called?
Choices:
- The logicians’ cross
- The bridge of asses
- The tree of Porphyry
- The square of opposition
Answer: The logicians’ cross
Why: Its many mixtures earned it the name *crux logicorum*.
Why not the others: The bridge of asses is a name for finding the middle term, and the tree and the square belong elsewhere.

@question 7.2
Question: What did St. Albert add to Aristotle’s first-figure rule?
Choices:
- That the plain minor must be true by the nature of its terms
- That the major must be contingent
- That the conclusion must always follow the weaker premise in mode
- That the rule holds in every figure
Answer: That the plain minor must be true by the nature of its terms
Why: He required that the plain minor be true “simply,” by the nature of its terms.
Why not the others: He followed Aristotle, not the weaker-premise rule, and kept the rule to the first figure.

@question 7.3
Question: Whose rule is it that the conclusion follows the weaker premise in mode?
Choices:
- Theophrastus’s
- Aristotle’s
- St. Albert’s
- Kilwardby’s
Answer: Theophrastus’s
Why: Theophrastus, Aristotle’s successor, held the simpler rule.
Why not the others: Aristotle and Albert grant a necessary conclusion in the first-figure case, and historians of logic report that Kilwardby did as well.


########################################################################
# Compound Arguments
########################################################################

@deck adv-carg
Title: Compound Arguments
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

The epicheirema; the polysyllogism and the sorites, Aristotelian and Goclenian, with their rules; the dilemma, its three rules, and the three ways of answering it; the dilemma and the disjunctive syllogism; and oblique syllogisms.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); John A. Oesterle, *Logic: The Art of Defining and Reasoning*, 2nd ed. (Prentice-Hall, 1963); Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* I.25 and *Topics* VIII.11.

**The epicheirema.** A syllogism is compound when it contains several syllogisms, stated or implied. The simplest is the **epicheirema**, a syllogism in which one premise, or both, is joined to its proof: “Every martyr is a saint, because every martyr has heroic charity; Peter is a martyr; so Peter is a saint.” The causal premise is itself a syllogism in brief: whoever has heroic charity is a saint, and every martyr has it, so every martyr is a saint.

Aristotle used the word “epicheirema” in another sense, for a dialectical argument from probable premises. The later sense may come from the fact that such premises invite further argument, and a premise that carries its own proof answers the invitation in advance. In judging an epicheirema, we judge each syllogism it contains, the stated one and the one hidden in the causal premise.

@question 1.1
Question: “Every judge must be impartial, since a judge decides between parties; Ruth is a judge; so Ruth must be impartial.” What is this?
Choices:
- An epicheirema
- A sorites
- A dilemma
- An enthymeme
Answer: An epicheirema
Why: Its major premise carries its own proof, which makes it an epicheirema.
Why not the others: Nothing is left unsaid, no chain of predicates runs on, and there is no disjunction.

@question 1.2
Question: What must be judged in an epicheirema?
Choices:
- Each syllogism it contains, stated and hidden
- Only the stated syllogism
- Only the causal premise, since it carries the proof
- Only the conclusion
Answer: Each syllogism it contains, stated and hidden
Why: The causal premise is itself a syllogism in brief, and it must hold as well.
Why not the others: The whole argument depends on both the stated syllogism and the one in the causal premise.

@question 1.3
Question: In what sense did Aristotle use the word “epicheirema”?
Choices:
- A dialectical argument from probable premises
- A syllogism with a causal premise
- A chain of conditionals leading to one conclusion
- A syllogism with a singular middle term
Answer: A dialectical argument from probable premises
Why: Aristotle’s epicheirema is a dialectical argument; the later sense is the syllogism with a causal premise.
Why not the others: The causal premise is the later sense; the other options are other kinds of argument.

@card 2

**Polysyllogism and sorites.** A **polysyllogism** links several syllogisms so that the conclusion of one is a premise of the next, each stated in full. A **sorites** links several propositions so that the predicate of each is the subject of the next, until a conclusion joins the subject of the first with the predicate of the last: “Peter is a man; every man is an animal; every animal has instincts; whatever has instincts reacts on its own; so Peter reacts on his own.” It contains as many syllogisms as it has premises, less one, but states none of their conclusions until the last.

Cicero called the sorites the most deceptive of arguments, because a defect in any link is easily hidden among the others. Its validity depends on the validity of every syllogism it contains. Aristotle does not use the name, but he describes the reasoning in the *Prior Analytics*.

@question 2.1
Question: A sorites has five premises. How many syllogisms does it contain?
Choices:
- Four
- Five
- Six
- Two
Answer: Four
Why: A sorites contains as many syllogisms as premises, less one.
Why not the others: Each syllogism after the first uses the previous conclusion and one new premise.

@question 2.2
Question: How does a sorites differ from a polysyllogism?
Choices:
- It leaves the intermediate conclusions unstated
- It uses only negative premises
- It has no middle terms
- It always has a particular conclusion and a negative premise
Answer: It leaves the intermediate conclusions unstated
Why: A polysyllogism states each conclusion; a sorites states only the last.
Why not the others: A sorites has several middle terms and may conclude universally or affirmatively.

@question 2.3
Question: Why did Cicero call the sorites deceptive?
Choices:
- A defect in one link is easily hidden among the others
- It always has a false conclusion
- It uses equivocal words by design, so as to mislead the hearer
- It has no premises at all
Answer: A defect in one link is easily hidden among the others
Why: Its validity depends on every hidden syllogism, and one bad link is easy to miss.
Why not the others: A sorites can be valid and true; the danger is a hidden defect.

@card 3

**The Aristotelian and the Goclenian sorites.** In the form just described, called the Aristotelian sorites, the series runs from the minor premise toward the major, and each middle term is wider than the one before. Rudolph Goclenius, a sixteenth-century German logician, gave his name to the reverse form, which begins with the premise that contains the predicate of the conclusion and ends with the one that contains its subject: “Whatever has instincts reacts on its own; every animal has instincts; every man is an animal; Peter is a man; so Peter reacts on his own.”

Each form has its rules, which follow from the rules of the syllogism. In the Aristotelian sorites only the first premise may be particular and only the last may be negative. In the Goclenian sorites the rules are reversed: only the last premise may be particular and only the first may be negative. A particular premise elsewhere leaves a middle term undistributed, and a negative premise elsewhere lets the major term be distributed in the conclusion when it is not distributed in its premise.

@question 3.1
Question: In an Aristotelian sorites, which premise may be particular?
Choices:
- Only the first
- Only the last
- Any premise
- None of them
Answer: Only the first
Why: A particular premise elsewhere would leave a middle term undistributed.
Why not the others: In the Goclenian form it is the last premise that may be particular.

@question 3.2
Question: In a Goclenian sorites, which premise may be negative?
Choices:
- Only the first
- Only the last
- Any premise
- None of them
Answer: Only the first
Why: The first Goclenian premise contains the predicate of the conclusion, so only it may be negative.
Why not the others: In the Aristotelian form it is the last premise that may be negative.

@question 3.3
Question: “Every mammal breathes; every dog is a mammal; every terrier is a dog; so every terrier breathes.” What form is this?
Choices:
- A Goclenian sorites
- An Aristotelian sorites
- A dilemma
- A polysyllogism
Answer: A Goclenian sorites
Why: It begins with the premise that contains the predicate of the conclusion, which is the Goclenian order.
Why not the others: An Aristotelian sorites would begin with “Every terrier is a dog.” No conclusions are stated in between, so it is no polysyllogism.

@card 4

**The dilemma.** A **dilemma** sets out a disjunction such that, whichever member is taken, the same conclusion follows. It is usually built on conditionals: “Either this man will do the act his conscience commands or he will not. If he does it, he acts against the eternal law; if he does not, he acts against his conscience. Either way he does wrong.” This is Maritain’s example of a conscience formed badly through the man’s own fault.

A dilemma can also be categorical: “Every just man needs grace to persevere; every sinner needs grace to be converted; every man is just or a sinner; so every man needs grace.” The dilemma is chiefly an argument of attack, and it is used more often about what to do than about what is so. The dilemma of Tertullian, the Christian writer of Carthage around the year 200, against the emperor Trajan’s decree on the Christians is a famous example: if they are guilty, why forbid searching for them; if they are innocent, why punish them?

@question 4.1
Question: What makes an argument a dilemma?
Choices:
- The same conclusion follows from either member of a disjunction
- One member of a disjunction is removed and the other posited
- A conclusion is drawn from two conditionals in a chain
- A premise is supported by its own proof
Answer: The same conclusion follows from either member of a disjunction
Why: In a dilemma the conclusion holds whichever member is taken.
Why not the others: Removing one member is the disjunctive syllogism, and a premise with its proof is an epicheirema.

@question 4.2
Question: For what is the dilemma chiefly used?
Choices:
- To attack, often about what to do
- To define a species
- To demonstrate a property of a species
- To divide a genus
Answer: To attack, often about what to do
Why: The dilemma is chiefly an argument of attack, usually about action.
Why not the others: Definition, demonstration, and division use other forms.

@question 4.3
Question: “Every honest witness is believed; every dishonest witness is exposed; every witness is honest or dishonest; so every witness is believed or exposed.” What is this?
Choices:
- A categorical dilemma
- A conditional dilemma
- A sorites
- An epicheirema
Answer: A categorical dilemma
Why: It sets out a disjunction and draws a conclusion from each member without conditionals.
Why not the others: No “if” is used, so it is categorical; there is no chain of predicates or causal premise.

@card 5

**The rules of a dilemma.** A dilemma holds only if three conditions are met. First, the disjunction must be **complete**, leaving no third possibility. “Every philosopher holds either that ideas are innate or that all knowledge is sensation” fails, since the Aristotelian tradition holds neither. Second, each consequence must **follow** from its member. Third, the common conclusion must be the **only** one that can be drawn, or the dilemma can be turned against its author.

The rules also show how a dilemma is answered. We may show that the disjunction is not complete, which is called escaping between the horns. We may deny that a consequence follows from its member, which is called taking the dilemma by the horns. Or we may draw an opposite conclusion from the same members, which is called retorting it. The dilemma of the caliph Omar, “The books in the library either agree with the Koran or not; if they agree, they are superfluous; if not, they are harmful; so burn them,” fails the second rule, since agreement does not make a book superfluous.

@question 5.1
Question: “Either we stay home or we go to the city; if we stay, we will be bored; if we go, we will be tired.” Someone points out that we could go to the country. Which answer is this?
Choices:
- Escaping between the horns
- Taking the dilemma by the horns
- Retorting the dilemma
- Granting the dilemma
Answer: Escaping between the horns
Why: Showing a third possibility denies that the disjunction is complete.
Why not the others: Taking by the horns denies a consequence; retorting draws an opposite conclusion.

@question 5.2
Question: Which rule of the dilemma is broken when a consequence does not follow from its member?
Choices:
- The second rule
- The first rule
- The third rule
- No rule is broken
Answer: The second rule
Why: The second rule requires each consequence to follow from its member.
Why not the others: The first concerns the completeness of the disjunction, and the third the uniqueness of the conclusion.

@question 5.3
Question: Why does the dilemma of the caliph Omar fail?
Choices:
- Agreement with the Koran does not make a book superfluous
- The disjunction leaves out a third possibility for the books
- It can be retorted against the caliph
- It has no conclusion
Answer: Agreement with the Koran does not make a book superfluous
Why: The consequence “if they agree, they are superfluous” does not follow, so the second rule is broken.
Why not the others: Every book either agrees or does not, so the disjunction is complete.

@card 6

**Retorting a dilemma.** To retort an argument is to draw the opposite conclusion from its own premises. Maritain’s example is the dilemma urged on someone considering public office: “Either you will govern honestly or dishonestly; if honestly, you will please God; if dishonestly, you will please men; either way it is to your advantage.” It is retorted: “If you govern honestly, you will displease men; if dishonestly, you will displease God; either way it is not to your advantage.”

A dilemma that can be retorted fails the third rule, since its members support two opposite conclusions equally. A sound dilemma cannot be retorted, because its conclusion is the only one its members allow. So before urging a dilemma, its author should try to retort it himself.

@question 6.1
Question: What is it to retort an argument?
Choices:
- To draw the opposite conclusion from its own premises
- To deny that its disjunction is complete and leaves nothing out
- To repeat it more forcefully
- To grant its conclusion
Answer: To draw the opposite conclusion from its own premises
Why: Retorting turns the members of the argument toward the opposite conclusion.
Why not the others: Denying completeness is escaping between the horns.

@question 6.2
Question: “If you study, you will tire yourself; if you do not, you will fail; so you will suffer.” What retort is possible?
Choices:
- If you study, you will pass; if not, you will rest; so you gain
- You could neither study nor fail to study, so the disjunction fails
- Studying does not tire anyone
- You will both study and not study
Answer: If you study, you will pass; if not, you will rest; so you gain
Why: The retort draws the opposite conclusion from the same members.
Why not the others: The second option denies an exhaustive disjunction, the third takes a horn, and the last is impossible.

@question 6.3
Question: Which rule does a dilemma that can be retorted fail?
Choices:
- The third, since its conclusion is not the only one
- The first, since its disjunction is incomplete
- The second, since a consequence does not follow
- None, since retorting is only rhetoric
Answer: The third, since its conclusion is not the only one
Why: If the members support an opposite conclusion equally, the stated conclusion is not the only one.
Why not the others: Retorting does not show an incomplete disjunction or a failed consequence.

@card 7

**Dilemma and disjunctive syllogism.** A dilemma is not the same as a disjunctive syllogism, though speakers often confuse them. In a dilemma the same conclusion follows from each member of the disjunction, so that we need not know which member is true. In a disjunctive syllogism one member is removed, and the other is concluded: “Either the minister was deceived or he knew; he was not deceived; so he knew.”

An orator who says he has caught his opponent “in a dilemma” usually means a disjunctive syllogism, with one member closed off by a further premise. The difference matters for answering: a disjunctive syllogism is answered by defending the member that was removed, and a dilemma by one of the three answers that suit it.

@question 7.1
Question: “Either the lamp is broken or the power is out; the lamp is not broken; so the power is out.” What is this?
Choices:
- A disjunctive syllogism
- A dilemma
- A sorites
- An epicheirema
Answer: A disjunctive syllogism
Why: One member is removed and the other concluded, which is a disjunctive syllogism.
Why not the others: In a dilemma the same conclusion would follow from both members.

@question 7.2
Question: How is a disjunctive syllogism answered?
Choices:
- By defending the member that was removed
- By retorting it with an opposite conclusion
- By denying that it has a disjunction
- By granting it and narrowing the thesis
Answer: By defending the member that was removed
Why: The argument stands or falls with the premise that removes one member.
Why not the others: Retorting is an answer to a dilemma, not to a disjunctive syllogism.

@question 7.3
Question: What do orators often mean by having an opponent “in a dilemma”?
Choices:
- A disjunctive syllogism with one member closed off
- A dilemma in the strict sense
- An argument with no conclusion
- A sorites with hidden steps and an unstated conclusion
Answer: A disjunctive syllogism with one member closed off
Why: They usually remove one member by a further premise, which is a disjunctive syllogism.
Why not the others: In a strict dilemma neither member is closed off; both lead to the conclusion.

@card 8

**Oblique syllogisms.** In an **oblique** syllogism one term stands in an oblique case, that is, it is not itself the subject or predicate but qualifies one by some relation, marked in English by a preposition or a possessive. “Christ is God; Mary is the mother of Christ; so Mary is the mother of God” is an oblique syllogism, in which the middle term “Christ” is direct in the major and oblique in the minor.

Maritain shows that some arguments usually taken to need a new “logic of relations” are oblique syllogisms. “A is greater than B; B is greater than C; so A is greater than C” is not a syllogism as it stands, since its middle term is “B” in one premise and “greater than B” in the other. It becomes valid when its hidden premise is stated: whatever is greater than something greater than C is itself greater than C. An oblique syllogism can always be restated as a direct one, but it has its own way of proceeding.

@question 8.1
Question: “Every friend of Peter is welcome; John is a friend of Peter; so John is welcome.” Where is the oblique term?
Choices:
- “Peter,” which qualifies the term by a relation
- “John,” the subject of the conclusion
- “welcome,” the predicate
- There is no oblique term
Answer: “Peter,” which qualifies the term by a relation
Why: “Of Peter” is in an oblique case, qualifying “friend” by a relation.
Why not the others: The direct terms are “friend of Peter,” “John,” and “welcome”; “Peter” itself stands obliquely.

@question 8.2
Question: Why is “A is older than B; B is older than C; so A is older than C” not a syllogism as it stands?
Choices:
- Its middle term is not the same in both premises
- It has two negative premises and a particular conclusion
- It concludes particularly
- It has a singular term
Answer: Its middle term is not the same in both premises
Why: The middle is “B” in one premise and “older than B” in the other.
Why not the others: The premises are affirmative, and singular terms are allowed; the problem is the middle term.

@question 8.3
Question: What makes the argument about “older than” valid?
Choices:
- Adding the premise that what is older than one older than C is older than C
- Converting both premises simply, so that the terms change their places in each
- Adding a second conclusion
- Reading the terms as material
Answer: Adding the premise that what is older than one older than C is older than C
Why: With that premise stated, the middle term is the same in both premises.
Why not the others: Conversion would not fix the middle term, and nothing material is meant.


########################################################################
# Induction and Example
########################################################################

@deck adv-ind
Title: Induction and Example
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

The two ways of making truth known; why induction has no middle term; why it concludes about a nature and not a collection; complete and incomplete induction; its two rules; why it is not a syllogism in Darapti; reasoning by resemblance; and analogy in reasoning and in knowledge.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); Aristotle, *Prior Analytics* II.23–24 and *Posterior Analytics* I.1, I.18, II.19; St. Albert the Great, commentary on the *Prior Analytics*; Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides).

**Two ways to make truth known.** Aristotle and St. Thomas teach that we come to know in two ways: by the syllogism, which proceeds from universals, and by induction, which proceeds from singulars. The reason lies in our knowledge itself. All of it depends formally on first principles known through themselves, and all of it takes its origin materially from the singular things the senses perceive.

Maritain puts the difference this way. A syllogism stays on the plane of the intelligible and moves from one universal truth to another, resolving a conclusion into the principles it depends on. Induction rises from the plane of the senses to the intelligible, resolving a conclusion into the experience from which the mind drew it. So induction is not a weaker syllogism; it is the other way of making truth known, and it supplies the principles that syllogisms use.

@question 1.1
Question: From what does induction proceed?
Choices:
- From singulars known through the senses
- From first principles known through themselves
- From probable opinions held by the wise
- From definitions stated by authority
Answer: From singulars known through the senses
Why: Induction rises from singulars to the universal; the syllogism proceeds from universals.
Why not the others: First principles are the starting point of the syllogism; opinion and authority belong to dialectic.

@question 1.2
Question: On Maritain’s account, how does induction differ from the syllogism?
Choices:
- It rises from the senses to the intelligible
- It stays on the plane of the intelligible
- It is a weaker form of the syllogism
- It uses four terms instead of three
Answer: It rises from the senses to the intelligible
Why: The syllogism moves within the intelligible; induction rises to it from experience.
Why not the others: Induction is not a weaker syllogism, and it has no middle term at all.

@question 1.3
Question: What does induction supply to syllogisms?
Choices:
- The principles they use
- The middle terms they need
- Their rules of validity
- Their conclusions
Answer: The principles they use
Why: Universal principles are first known by induction from experience.
Why not the others: Induction supplies principles, not rules, middle terms, or conclusions.

@card 2

**No middle term.** In a syllogism two terms are joined because each is joined to a third, the middle term. Induction has no middle term. Its mean is an **enumeration** of individuals or of kinds: “This water boils at one hundred degrees, and this, and this; and the nature found in these is water; so water boils at one hundred degrees.” The enumeration does the work that the middle term does in a syllogism.

The two premises of an induction therefore differ from those of a syllogism. Its first premise joins a predicate to a series of individuals taken one by one; its second joins those individuals to the universal they share; and the conclusion joins the predicate to the universal. Aristotle says the same in the *Prior Analytics* (II.23): in induction one extreme is shown of the middle through the other extreme, the enumerated cases.

@question 2.1
Question: What takes the place of the middle term in an induction?
Choices:
- An enumeration of individuals or kinds
- A definition of the subject that is being studied
- A conditional premise
- A universal principle
Answer: An enumeration of individuals or kinds
Why: The enumerated cases are the mean that joins the predicate to the universal.
Why not the others: Induction has no middle term; a definition or principle would make it a syllogism.

@question 2.2
Question: “This crow is black, and this, and this; and these are crows; so the crow is black.” What does the second premise join?
Choices:
- The individuals to the universal they share
- The predicate to the universal under which the cases fall
- The predicate to the individuals
- Two universals to each other
Answer: The individuals to the universal they share
Why: The second premise joins the enumerated cases to the nature they share.
Why not the others: The first premise joins the predicate to the cases, and the conclusion joins it to the universal.

@question 2.3
Question: What does the first premise of an induction join?
Choices:
- A predicate to a series of individuals
- Two universals to each other by means of a middle term
- An individual to its species
- A cause to its effect
Answer: A predicate to a series of individuals
Why: It says that the predicate belongs to this case, and this, and this.
Why not the others: No middle term is used, and the joining of cases to their species is the second premise.

@card 3

**“Every,” not “all.”** Maritain insists that induction concludes about a nature, not about a collection. From “this metal conducts electricity, and this, and this,” it concludes that metal, every metal, conducts electricity, and so it claims a connection between the property and the nature, even if that connection is not yet seen. It does not merely sum up the cases observed.

Read as a statement about a collection, induction would be either worthless or empty. If it passed from some members to the whole collection taken together (“this soldier has a soul, and that one, so the regiment has a soul”), it would be a fallacy. If it simply listed every member (“Peter, James, and John were present, so all three were present”), it would say nothing new. Induction properly so called passes from some to *every*, from the cases to the nature found in each.

@question 3.1
Question: “This rose has thorns, and this, and this; so every rose has thorns.” What does the conclusion claim?
Choices:
- A connection between the nature and the property
- A summary of the roses observed
- That all the roses, taken together as one group, have thorns
- That some roses have thorns
Answer: A connection between the nature and the property
Why: Induction concludes about the nature found in each case, not about a collection.
Why not the others: A mere summary would say nothing new, and a claim about all roses together would be a fallacy.

@question 3.2
Question: “This brick weighs a pound, and this one, and this one; so the wall weighs a pound.” What is wrong?
Choices:
- It passes from members to the collection taken together
- The enumeration is complete
- It concludes about the nature rather than about the bricks
- It has a middle term
Answer: It passes from members to the collection taken together
Why: What holds of each brick need not hold of the wall as a whole; this reads induction as about a collection.
Why not the others: Induction properly passes to the nature in each case, not to the group taken together.

@question 3.3
Question: Why should an induction be stated with “every” rather than “all”?
Choices:
- “Every” signifies the nature in each case, not a collection
- “All” is not correct English
- “Every” makes the induction complete, whatever has been enumerated
- “All” makes the conclusion particular
Answer: “Every” signifies the nature in each case, not a collection
Why: Maritain prefers “every,” since “all” first suggests a collection of individuals.
Why not the others: Both are good English; the point is what each word signifies first.

@card 4

**Complete and incomplete induction.** An induction is **complete** when every part of the universal has been enumerated: “Sight, hearing, touch, taste, and smell each have a bodily organ; these are all the senses; so every sense has a bodily organ.” It is **incomplete** when only some of the parts have been enumerated, as in nearly all inductions from experience, since we cannot review every case.

“Incomplete” does not mean “badly done.” An incomplete enumeration can still be **sufficient**, and when it is sufficient, the induction is valid; a sufficient enumeration is in effect a complete one. The hard question, which belongs to the study of each science, is when an enumeration is sufficient. Complete induction is not empty either: to know that each of the five senses has an organ is one thing, and to know that sense, as such, has an organ is another.

@question 4.1
Question: “Copper, iron, and gold conduct heat; so every metal conducts heat.” What kind of induction is this?
Choices:
- Incomplete, though it may be sufficient
- Complete, since all metals are named
- Not an induction, since it has a middle term
- A syllogism in Darapti
Answer: Incomplete, though it may be sufficient
