Why: Only some metals are enumerated, so the induction is incomplete; it is valid if the enumeration is sufficient.
Why not the others: Not every metal is named, and there is no middle term.

@question 4.2
Question: What does “incomplete” mean when said of an induction?
Choices:
- That not every part has been enumerated
- That the induction is badly done
- That the conclusion is false
- That the induction has no conclusion
Answer: That not every part has been enumerated
Why: It is short for “by incomplete enumeration,” and such an induction can still be sufficient.
Why not the others: An incomplete induction can be valid and its conclusion true.

@question 4.3
Question: Why is a complete induction not empty?
Choices:
- Knowing the nature as such differs from knowing each part
- It adds a new case that was not counted among the others before
- It changes the predicate
- It is not really an induction
Answer: Knowing the nature as such differs from knowing each part
Why: To know that sense as such has an organ is more than to know that each sense does.
Why not the others: A complete induction adds no case and keeps the predicate; it reaches the universal.

@card 5

**The two rules.** Maritain gives two rules for the form of induction. First, the enumeration must be **sufficient**; otherwise the inference is not formal. “Peter is French, and Paul, and James, so every man is French” is a defective induction, since three Frenchmen do not represent mankind.

Second, we may not make a particular term universal by an inductive ascent, even with a sufficient enumeration. “Some saint lacked this kind of heroism, and some saint lacked that kind, and so on through every kind, so some saint lacked every kind of heroism” does not follow, because a different saint may lack each kind. Each premise says only that one or another saint lacked one kind, and that does not add up to one saint lacking all of them.

@question 5.1
Question: “This swan is white, and that one, and that one; so every swan is white.” What would make this induction defective?
Choices:
- An enumeration too small to represent swans
- An enumeration that is complete
- Stating its conclusion with the word “every”
- Having a predicate in it
Answer: An enumeration too small to represent swans
Why: The enumeration must be sufficient; otherwise the inference is not formal.
Why not the others: A complete enumeration would be sufficient, and “every” is the right word.

@question 5.2
Question: “Some student failed the first test, some failed the second, and so on; so some student failed every test.” Why does this not follow?
Choices:
- A different student may have failed each test
- The enumeration of the tests is not sufficient
- The conclusion is universal
- It has a middle term
Answer: A different student may have failed each test
Why: We may not make a particular term universal by ascent: each premise is about one student or another.
Why not the others: Even a sufficient enumeration of tests would not let us pass to one student failing all.

@question 5.3
Question: Which of Maritain’s rules forbids ascending from a particular term?
Choices:
- The second rule
- The first rule
- Neither rule
- Both rules
Answer: The second rule
Why: The second rule forbids making a determinate particular universal by an inductive ascent.
Why not the others: The first rule requires a sufficient enumeration.

@card 6

**Not a syllogism in Darapti.** Some modern logicians treat induction as a syllogism in the third figure: “This magnet attracts iron; this body is a magnet; so some magnet attracts iron,” with “every magnet” then assumed. Maritain answers that this destroys induction rather than explains it. As a syllogism the argument concludes only “some magnet,” and the step to “every magnet” would be invalid in form.

St. Albert the Great, St. Thomas’s teacher, had already said that the matter of induction can be put into a syllogism but its form cannot, and that induction is opposed to the syllogism properly so called. The syllogism proves that a subject and predicate agree because both agree with a third term; induction proves that a property belongs to a nature by producing the cases in which they are found together.

@question 6.1
Question: Why does Maritain reject reading induction as a syllogism in Darapti?
Choices:
- As a syllogism it concludes only “some,” not “every”
- Darapti is not a valid mood
- Induction has more premises than Darapti, so it cannot fit the mood
- Darapti has no middle term
Answer: As a syllogism it concludes only “some,” not “every”
Why: The third figure concludes particularly, so the universal conclusion would not follow in form.
Why not the others: Darapti is valid and has a middle term; it simply cannot give a universal conclusion.

@question 6.2
Question: What did St. Albert say about induction and the syllogism?
Choices:
- The matter of induction can be put in a syllogism, but not its form
- Induction is only a weaker kind of syllogism, in the third figure as a rule
- The syllogism is a kind of induction
- Induction has no matter
Answer: The matter of induction can be put in a syllogism, but not its form
Why: Albert held that induction is opposed to the syllogism properly so called.
Why not the others: Albert denied that induction is a kind of syllogism or the reverse.

@question 6.3
Question: How does a syllogism prove that a subject and predicate agree?
Choices:
- Both agree with a third term
- By listing the cases in which they are found together
- By a resemblance between two cases
- By the authority of the wise
Answer: Both agree with a third term
Why: The syllogism joins the extremes through the middle term.
Why not the others: Listing the cases is the way of induction, and resemblance that of the example.

@card 7

**Reasoning by resemblance.** Reasoning by resemblance, which Aristotle calls the example, passes from one case to another like it: “This remedy cured Paul’s headaches, so it will cure John’s.” It is an induction that stops halfway. Instead of rising to the universal of which Paul’s case is a part, it rises only to a resemblance between the two cases, and from there it descends to the second case.

Because the resemblance may be accidental, the example gives probable knowledge, not certainty, though it plays a great part in discovery. It is not an inference from particular to particular without any universal, as some modern logicians claim: the universal in it is the likeness itself. The scientist who guesses that dew forms as moisture forms on a cold glass is reasoning by resemblance, and so is a rhetorician who argues from a past war to a coming one.

@question 7.1
Question: “This plan worked in our city, so it will work in yours.” What kind of reasoning is this?
Choices:
- Reasoning by resemblance, the example
- Complete induction
- A syllogism in Barbara
- An expository syllogism
Answer: Reasoning by resemblance, the example
Why: It passes from one case to another like it, without rising to a universal.
Why not the others: It neither enumerates all cases nor uses a middle term.

@question 7.2
Question: What kind of knowledge does reasoning by resemblance give?
Choices:
- Probable knowledge, useful in discovery
- Certain knowledge, as in demonstration
- No knowledge at all
- Knowledge of first principles
Answer: Probable knowledge, useful in discovery
Why: The resemblance may be accidental, so the conclusion is only probable.
Why not the others: It is useful, but it does not give the certainty of demonstration or of principles.

@question 7.3
Question: Is reasoning by resemblance an inference from particular to particular with no universal?
Choices:
- No, the likeness itself is its universal
- Yes, as the modern logicians claim
- Yes, since it names no species
- No, since it is a complete induction
Answer: No, the likeness itself is its universal
Why: Maritain argues that every reasoning has some universal, here the resemblance between the cases.
Why not the others: It is not complete induction; but it does rest on a universal, the likeness.

@card 8

**Analogy in reasoning and in knowledge.** Reasoning by resemblance is often called reasoning by analogy, but it must not be confused with analogical knowledge. In reasoning by analogy the likeness is the means of an inference, often a likeness that is more or less accidental, and the conclusion is only probable.

In analogical knowledge the analogy belongs to a concept and to the things it is said of, as “being” is said of creatures and of God, each in its own way. When one of the things is known to us and the other is not, we can know the second through the first, inadequately but with certainty. So an argument “from analogy” in the first sense may be weak, while knowledge by analogy in the second sense can be certain.

@question 8.1
Question: “The moon’s light comes from the sun, so the light of the planets does too.” Which kind of analogy is this?
Choices:
- Reasoning by analogy, which is probable
- Analogical knowledge, which can be certain
- Metaphor, which proves nothing
- Univocal predication
Answer: Reasoning by analogy, which is probable
Why: The likeness is the means of an inference, so the conclusion is only probable.
Why not the others: Analogical knowledge concerns a concept said of things in different ways, not an inference from a likeness.

@question 8.2
Question: In analogical knowledge, to what does the analogy belong?
Choices:
- To a concept and the things it is said of
- To the likeness between two cases
- To the probability of a conclusion
- To the words of a figure of speech
Answer: To a concept and the things it is said of
Why: In analogical knowledge the analogy is in the concept, as “being” is said of creatures and of God.
Why not the others: A likeness between cases is reasoning by analogy, which is a different thing.

@question 8.3
Question: Can knowledge by analogy be certain?
Choices:
- Yes, though it is inadequate
- No, since every analogy is weak
- Only when the things are equal
- Only in mathematics
Answer: Yes, though it is inadequate
Why: We can know one thing through another truly, though not fully.
Why not the others: Arguments by resemblance are weak; knowledge by analogy can be certain.


########################################################################
# Demonstration
########################################################################

@deck adv-dem
Title: Demonstration
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

The conditions of demonstrative premises; the four ways of being said per se; demonstration of the fact through an effect and through a remote cause, and of the reasoned fact through the proper cause; proper and common principles; the first figure as the figure of science; and fact and reason in different sciences.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); St. Thomas Aquinas, commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, bk. I, lect. 4, 10, 23–25; Aristotle, *Posterior Analytics* I.2, 4, 9, 13–14; Pierre Conway, *Aristotelian Formal and Material Logic*; Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides).

**The conditions of the premises.** A demonstration is a syllogism that produces science, that is, knowledge of a thing through its cause and as something that cannot be otherwise. So its premises must be of a certain kind. Aristotle requires that they be **true**, **first** and **immediate** (not themselves proved through a middle), **better known** than the conclusion, **prior** to it, and its **causes** (*Posterior Analytics* I.2).

St. Thomas explains each condition (commentary, bk. I, lect. 4). The premises must be true, since what is not cannot be known. They must be first and immediate, or else resolve into premises that are, since proof cannot go back without end. And they must be better known, prior, and causes, since the conclusion is known through them. A valid argument from true premises proves its conclusion, but it is a demonstration only when its premises meet all these conditions.

@question 1.1
Question: Which of these is not one of Aristotle’s conditions for the premises of a demonstration?
Choices:
- That they be widely believed
- That they be prior to the conclusion
- That they be causes of the conclusion
- That they be better known than the conclusion
Answer: That they be widely believed
Why: Being widely believed is the mark of a dialectical premise, not a demonstrative one.
Why not the others: Priority, causality, and being better known are among Aristotle’s conditions.

@question 1.2
Question: Why must the premises of a demonstration be first and immediate?
Choices:
- Proof cannot go back without end
- Every premise must be self-evident to everyone
- A premise with a middle term is false
- The conclusion must be immediate too
Answer: Proof cannot go back without end
Why: Every chain of proof must end in premises not proved through a middle.
Why not the others: Mediate premises are not false; but they must resolve into immediate ones.

@question 1.3
Question: A valid argument from true premises fails to give the cause. What is it?
Choices:
- Sound, but not a demonstration of the reasoned fact
- Invalid, since it has no cause in its middle term at all
- A fallacy of the consequent
- A demonstration in the full sense
Answer: Sound, but not a demonstration of the reasoned fact
Why: Soundness is not enough for demonstration; the premises must also be causes.
Why not the others: The argument is valid and sound; what it lacks is the cause.

@card 2

**The first and second ways of being said per se.** The premises of a demonstration are necessary because their predicates belong to their subjects *per se*, that is, through the subject itself. St. Thomas distinguishes four ways (commentary, bk. I, lect. 10). In the **first**, the predicate is the definition of the subject or part of it: “A triangle is a figure,” “A line is bounded by points.” In the **second**, the subject enters the definition of the predicate, as its proper subject: “A number is odd or even,” “A nose is snub,” since odd and snub cannot be defined without number and nose.

Whatever is predicated neither way is predicated *per accidens*, as “musical” and “white” are said of an animal. Demonstration uses the first way in its principles, since the definition is the middle term, and it concludes in the second way, since the property it proves is defined through the subject in which it belongs.

@question 2.1
Question: “A circle is a figure.” In which way is this said per se?
Choices:
- The first, since the predicate is part of the definition
- The second, since the subject enters the definition of the predicate
- The third, since a circle exists by itself
- It is said per accidens
Answer: The first, since the predicate is part of the definition
Why: “Figure” is the genus in the definition of the circle.
Why not the others: The predicate is part of the subject’s definition, which is the first way.

@question 2.2
Question: “A number is prime or composite.” In which way is this said per se?
Choices:
- The second, since number enters the definition of prime
- The first, since prime is part of the definition of number
- The fourth, since number causes primeness
- It is said per accidens
Answer: The second, since number enters the definition of prime
Why: “Prime” cannot be defined without “number,” its proper subject.
Why not the others: Prime is not part of what a number is; the subject enters the predicate’s definition.

@question 2.3
Question: “The animal is musical.” How is the predicate said?
Choices:
- Per accidens
- Per se in the first way
- Per se in the second way
- Per se in the fourth way
Answer: Per accidens
Why: Neither enters the definition of the other, so it is said per accidens.
Why not the others: Musical is not part of animal, nor is animal its proper subject by definition.

@card 3

**The third and fourth ways.** In the **third** way, “per se” means existing by itself, as an individual substance does: Socrates exists by himself, while “the walking” or “the white” exists only in something else. This sense concerns things, not predication. In the **fourth** way, a predicate belongs to a subject per se when the subject is the cause of it: “What was killed, died,” since the killing caused the death. What merely happens along with something belongs per accidens: “While he was walking, there was lightning.”

The fourth way joins effect to efficient cause, and so it too can appear in demonstration, when the cause named is the proper and necessary cause. The four ways together show what kind of necessity science looks for: a predicate that belongs to a subject through what the subject is or through what it causes, not through what happens to accompany it.

@question 3.1
Question: “The beheaded man died.” In which way is this said per se?
Choices:
- The fourth, since the beheading caused the death
- The first, since death is part of man’s definition
- The third, since he existed by himself
- It is said per accidens
Answer: The fourth, since the beheading caused the death
Why: In the fourth way the subject is the cause of what is predicated.
Why not the others: Death is not in man’s definition; the beheading is the cause.

@question 3.2
Question: “While she was reading, the bell rang.” How is this said?
Choices:
- Per accidens, since the reading did not cause the ringing
- Per se in the fourth way
- Per se in the first way
- Per se in the third way
Answer: Per accidens, since the reading did not cause the ringing
Why: What merely happens along with something is per accidens.
Why not the others: The reading is not the cause of the ringing.

@question 3.3
Question: What does the third way of being per se concern?
Choices:
- A thing existing by itself, as an individual substance
- A predicate that is placed in the definition of the subject
- A cause and its effect
- A subject in the definition of the predicate
Answer: A thing existing by itself, as an individual substance
Why: It concerns things, not predication: a substance exists by itself.
Why not the others: The other ways concern predication.

@card 4

**Through the effect: the planets.** Aristotle distinguishes knowing *that* a thing is so from knowing *why* (*Posterior Analytics* I.13). His example: “Whatever does not twinkle is near; the planets do not twinkle; so the planets are near.” This is valid and true, but the middle term, not twinkling, is not the cause of nearness; the reverse is true, since the planets do not twinkle because they are near. So this demonstrates only **that** the planets are near (*quia*).

Exchange the terms and the demonstration gives the reason: “Whatever is near does not twinkle; the planets are near; so the planets do not twinkle.” Now the middle term is the cause, and we know **why** (*propter quid*). St. Thomas adds that the demonstration of the fact can proceed through an effect that is better known to us, since effects are often more evident to the senses than their causes (commentary, bk. I, lect. 23).

@question 4.1
Question: “Whatever has smoke has fire; this house has smoke; so this house has fire.” What kind of demonstration is this?
Choices:
- Of the fact, through an effect
- Of the reasoned fact, through the cause
- Of the fact, through a remote cause
- No demonstration at all
Answer: Of the fact, through an effect
Why: Smoke is the effect of fire, so the argument shows that, not why.
Why not the others: The middle term is an effect, not a cause, near or remote.

@question 4.2
Question: In “Whatever is near does not twinkle; the planets are near; so they do not twinkle,” what is the middle term?
Choices:
- Being near, which is the cause
- Not twinkling, which is the effect
- The planets
- Twinkling
Answer: Being near, which is the cause
Why: The middle term, nearness, is the cause, so this shows why.
Why not the others: Not twinkling is here the conclusion’s predicate.

@question 4.3
Question: Why can a demonstration of the fact proceed through an effect?
Choices:
- The effect is often better known to us than the cause
- The effect is the cause of the cause
- The effect is always prior by nature
- The effect is more certain in itself
Answer: The effect is often better known to us than the cause
Why: Effects are often more evident to the senses, though causes are prior by nature.
Why not the others: The cause is prior and better known by nature; the effect is better known to us.

@card 5

**Through a remote cause: the wall.** A demonstration can also show only the fact when its middle term is a cause, but not the proper and proximate one. Aristotle’s example: “Whatever is not an animal does not breathe; a wall is not an animal; so a wall does not breathe.” The conclusion is true, but not being an animal is not the reason a wall does not breathe. If it were, being an animal would be the reason for breathing, and many animals do not breathe in the way meant (St. Thomas, commentary, bk. I, lect. 24).

The middle term here is too wide: it lies outside the proper genus of the conclusion and does not convert with the major term. Pierre Conway gives a parallel: no diamond is a tiger, but not because the diamond is not an animal; more basically, because it is not living. The demonstration of the reasoned fact needs the proximate cause, the one that converts with the effect.

@question 5.1
Question: “Whatever is not a living thing does not grow; a stone is not a living thing; so a stone does not grow.” Is the middle term the proximate cause?
Choices:
- Yes, since not living explains not growing
- No, it is a remote cause
- No, it is an effect
- No, it is an accident
Answer: Yes, since not living explains not growing
Why: Growth belongs to living things as such, so not living is the proximate reason.
Why not the others: Here the middle term converts with the predicate; it is the proper cause.

@question 5.2
Question: Why does “a wall is not an animal” not explain why it does not breathe?
Choices:
- Then being an animal would explain breathing, which it does not
- Walls are animals in a figurative sense, and so do breathe in a way
- The premise is false
- Breathing has no cause
Answer: Then being an animal would explain breathing, which it does not
Why: If a negation is the cause of a negation, the affirmation must be the cause of the affirmation.
Why not the others: The premise is true; the middle term is too wide to be the proper cause.

@question 5.3
Question: What must the middle term of a demonstration of the reasoned fact do?
Choices:
- Be the proximate cause, converting with the effect
- Be wider than the major term
- Be an effect better known to us
- Be a probable opinion
Answer: Be the proximate cause, converting with the effect
Why: The proper cause converts with the effect and explains it.
Why not the others: A wider middle is a remote cause, and an effect gives only the fact.

@card 6

**Proper principles, not common ones.** A demonstration must proceed from the principles proper to its own science, not from principles common to many. Aristotle cites the attempt of Bryson, a Greek mathematician of Plato’s time, to square the circle: “In whatever genus there is something greater and something less than a given thing, there is something equal to it; there are squares greater and less than a given circle; so there is a square equal to it.” The principle is true of quantity in general, but it says nothing about the forms of the circle and the square.

Such an argument may be persuasive, but it does not give the reason proper to geometry, and so it is not a demonstration. For the same reason a proof in geometry does not proceed from arithmetic, nor a proof in medicine from mere statistics: each science demonstrates from the principles that belong to its own subject. Squaring the circle is impossible, as Ferdinand von Lindemann, the German mathematician, proved in 1882.

@question 6.1
Question: Why is Bryson’s argument not a demonstration?
Choices:
- It proceeds from a principle common to all quantity
- Its premises are false
- It is invalid
- It uses the second figure
Answer: It proceeds from a principle common to all quantity
Why: The principle is true of quantity in general and says nothing of circles and squares as such.
Why not the others: The argument may be valid with true premises; it fails to give the proper reason.

@question 6.2
Question: A proof in geometry uses only principles of arithmetic. What has gone wrong?
Choices:
- It does not proceed from the principles proper to its science
- It has four terms
- It reasons from effects instead of from causes, which is not allowed
- It concludes negatively
Answer: It does not proceed from the principles proper to its science
Why: Each science demonstrates from principles proper to its own subject.
Why not the others: The fault is not the number of terms or the quality of the conclusion.

@question 6.3
Question: What may an argument from a common principle still be?
Choices:
- Persuasive, but not a demonstration
- A demonstration of the reasoned fact
- A first principle
- A definition
Answer: Persuasive, but not a demonstration
Why: It may seem convincing, yet it does not give the proper reason.
Why not the others: Only proper principles give the reasoned fact.

@card 7

**The figure of science.** Aristotle holds that the first figure is the most scientific (*Posterior Analytics* I.14). Mathematics and the sciences that give the reasoned fact demonstrate in it; only in it can a universal affirmative be concluded, and science seeks the universal affirmative “every S is P” through its cause. A demonstration in the second or third figure can be completed only by being reduced to the first.

A demonstration of the reasoned fact usually has the definition of the subject, or of the property, as its middle term. “Every rational animal is capable of laughter; every man is a rational animal; so every man is capable of laughter.” The definition explains why the property belongs, and the conclusion states a property in the second way of being said per se.

@question 7.1
Question: Why is the first figure the most scientific?
Choices:
- Only it concludes universally and affirmatively
- Only it has three terms
- Only it uses necessary premises
- Only it avoids definitions
Answer: Only it concludes universally and affirmatively
Why: Science seeks “every S is P” through its cause, which only the first figure concludes.
Why not the others: All figures have three terms and may use necessary premises.

@question 7.2
Question: What is usually the middle term of a demonstration of the reasoned fact?
Choices:
- A definition
- An accident
- A probable opinion
- A singular term
Answer: A definition
Why: The definition explains why the property belongs.
Why not the others: Accidents, opinions, and singulars do not give the cause.

@question 7.3
Question: In “Every rational animal is capable of laughter; every man is a rational animal; so every man is capable of laughter,” in which way is the conclusion said per se?
Choices:
- The second
- The first
- The third
- Per accidens
Answer: The second
Why: A property is defined through its subject, so it is said per se in the second way.
Why not the others: Laughter is not part of man’s definition, so not the first way.

@card 8

**Fact and reason in different sciences.** Sometimes one science knows the fact and another the reason. Aristotle’s example: the physician knows *that* round wounds heal more slowly, while the geometer knows *why*. So also the practical astronomer or the musician knows facts whose reason belongs to mathematics. In such cases the lower science is **subalternated** to the higher, taking its principles from it.

This shows that “science” in the classical sense has degrees. A science that knows the fact without the reason is still knowledge, but it rests on another science for its explanation. St. Thomas counts theology a science in this way, subalternated to the knowledge God and the blessed have, from which it receives its principles by faith (*Summa theologiae* I, q. 1, a. 2).

@question 8.1
Question: The engineer knows that a bridge of a certain shape holds, and the physicist knows why. What is the relation?
Choices:
- The engineer’s knowledge is subalternated to the physicist’s
- The physicist’s is subalternated to the engineer’s
- Neither is science
- They are the same science
Answer: The engineer’s knowledge is subalternated to the physicist’s
Why: The lower science knows the fact and takes the reason from the higher.
Why not the others: The one that supplies the reason is the higher science.

@question 8.2
Question: Which science does Aristotle say knows why round wounds heal more slowly?
Choices:
- Geometry
- Medicine
- Astronomy
- Music
Answer: Geometry
Why: The physician knows the fact; the geometer knows the reason.
Why not the others: Medicine knows only that it is so.

@question 8.3
Question: How does St. Thomas count theology a science?
Choices:
- As subalternated to the knowledge of God and the blessed
- As a science that proves all of its own principles by reason
- As a branch of geometry
- As a dialectical art only
Answer: As subalternated to the knowledge of God and the blessed
Why: It receives its principles by faith from a higher knowledge (Summa theologiae I, q. 1, a. 2).
Why not the others: Theology does not prove its own first principles.

# The Fallacies in Depth
########################################################################

@deck adv-fal
Title: The Fallacies in Depth
Small heading: LOGIC AS A SCIENCE

After De fallaciis, attributed to St. Thomas: the three kinds of multiplicity behind the six fallacies in the words and their modes; why there are seven outside the words; accident and consequent; the five ways to beg the question; non-cause and many questions; and why every fallacy is an ignorance of the refutation.

@card 1
Sources: Timothy Kearns, “Logic (General and Historical),” *Encyclopedia of Catholic Theology* (ect.org, 2025); Jacques Maritain, *Formal Logic*, trans. Imelda Choquette (Sheed & Ward, 1937); St. Thomas Aquinas (attributed), *De fallaciis*; John of St. Thomas, *Outlines of Formal Logic*, trans. Francis C. Wade (Marquette University Press, 1955); Aristotle, *Sophistical Refutations*; Matthew Minerd, “BCS Logic 2023” (course slides).

**Three kinds of multiplicity.** *De fallaciis*, the treatise attributed to St. Thomas, explains why there are exactly six fallacies in the words. A fallacy in the words deceives because one expression seems to signify one thing while it signifies several, and this can happen in three ways (ch. 5). The multiplicity is **actual** when an expression, unchanged, signifies several things: in one word this is equivocation, and in a phrase amphiboly.

The multiplicity is **potential** when the expression signifies several things only as it is spoken in one way or another: in one word this is accent, and in a phrase composition and division. It is **apparent** when a word signifies one thing but seems to signify something else by its likeness to other words, which is the figure of speech. So the six fallacies in the words are equivocation, amphiboly, accent, composition, division, and figure of speech.

@question 1.1
Question: A word unchanged signifies several things. What kind of multiplicity is this?
Choices:
- Actual
- Potential
- Apparent
- Collective
Answer: Actual
Why: An expression that signifies several things without any change is actually multiple.
Why not the others: Potential multiplicity depends on how the words are spoken or joined; apparent multiplicity only seems to be there.

@question 1.2
Question: Which fallacy arises from apparent multiplicity?
Choices:
- Figure of speech
- Equivocation
- Accent
- Amphiboly
Answer: Figure of speech
Why: In the figure of speech a word signifies one thing but seems to signify another.
Why not the others: Equivocation and amphiboly are actual, and accent potential.

@question 1.3
Question: Which pair of fallacies arises from potential multiplicity in a phrase?
Choices:
- Composition and division
- Equivocation and amphiboly
- Accident and consequent
- Accent and figure of speech
Answer: Composition and division
Why: A phrase that signifies differently as its words are joined or separated gives composition and division.
Why not the others: Accent is potential multiplicity in one word; accident and consequent are outside the words.

@card 2

**The modes of equivocation and amphiboly.** *De fallaciis* counts three modes of equivocation (ch. 6). In the first, a word signifies several things equally, as “dog” signifies the animal, the star, and the sea creature. In the second, a word signifies one thing properly and another by a figure, as a meadow is said to “laugh” when it flowers, and analogous words such as “healthy,” said of the body and of food, are counted here. In the third, a word changes what it signifies with its tense, number, or person, as in “Whoever was rising is standing; the seated man was rising; so the seated man is standing.”

Amphiboly, the equivocation of a phrase, also has three modes (ch. 7). A phrase may be built in one way and still bear two relations, as “the book of Aristotle” can mean the book he wrote or the book he owns. It may be built in two ways, as when a word can be taken as subject or as object. Or the whole phrase may be taken properly in one premise and figuratively in another, as “to plow the seashore” means both cutting the sand and labor wasted. In each case the remedy is to distinguish the senses and grant the premise in one and deny it in the other.

@question 2.1
Question: “Whatever runs has legs; the river runs; so the river has legs.” Which mode of equivocation is this?
Choices:
- The second, where one sense is proper and one figurative
- The first, where the word signifies several things equally
- The third, where the word changes with tense or number
- None, since it is amphiboly
Answer: The second, where one sense is proper and one figurative
Why: “Runs” is said properly of animals and figuratively of the river, which the treatise counts in the second mode.
Why not the others: The senses are not equal and unrelated, and nothing turns on tense or number.

@question 2.2
Question: “The portrait of the king” can mean the one he owns or the one that shows him. What is this?
Choices:
- Amphiboly
- Equivocation
- Accent
- Figure of speech
Answer: Amphiboly
Why: One phrase, built the same way, bears two relations, which is amphiboly.
Why not the others: No single word changes its sense; the phrase does.

@question 2.3
Question: “Light” said of a feather and of a lamp. Which fallacy can it produce?
Choices:
- Equivocation
- Amphiboly
- Composition
- Accident
Answer: Equivocation
Why: One word with several meanings produces equivocation.
Why not the others: The ambiguity is in one word, not in a phrase or in the things.

@card 3

**Composition, division, and accent.** In composition and division the same words can be joined or separated in different ways, so that one phrase is potentially several (ch. 8). “Whatever lives always exists” is false if “always” goes with “exists” and true if it goes with “lives.” The fallacy is one of **composition** when the phrase is false taken together, and of **division** when it is false taken apart. “Two and three are five, so two and three are odd and even” divides what is true only together.

Accent is the same fault in one word: a word pronounced in different ways signifies different things (ch. 9). In Latin the difference lay in the pitch of a syllable; in English it lies chiefly in stress and in the emphasis of the speaker, as when “I never said he stole the money” is made to mean different things by stressing different words.

@question 3.1
Question: “A man who sits can walk; so a man can sit and walk at once.” Which fallacy is this?
Choices:
- Composition
- Division
- Accent
- Accident
Answer: Composition
Why: The premise is true taken apart and false taken together, and the argument takes it together.
Why not the others: The fault comes from joining what is true only separately.

@question 3.2
Question: “Five is two and three; so five is even and odd.” Which fallacy is this?
Choices:
- Division
- Composition
- Equivocation
- Figure of speech
Answer: Division
Why: What is true of two and three together is divided into what is true of each.
Why not the others: The phrase is false taken apart, which makes it division.

@question 3.3
Question: In English, where does the fallacy of accent mainly lie?
Choices:
- In stress and emphasis
- In spelling
- In the gender of words
- In word order alone
Answer: In stress and emphasis
Why: Latin accent was pitch; in English the difference lies mainly in stress and emphasis.
Why not the others: Gender belongs to the figure of speech, and word order to amphiboly.

@card 4

**The modes of figure of speech.** In the figure of speech a word has one way of signifying but seems to have another, by its likeness to other words (ch. 10). The treatise counts three modes. A word of one gender may seem to be of another, which matters chiefly in Latin. A word in one category may seem to be in another, as when “what you saw yesterday you see today” passes from a quality (“white”) to a substance. And a word that signifies a kind may seem to signify an individual, as “man” seems to name this something.

The third mode explains a famous sophism: “Man is a species; Socrates is a man; so Socrates is a species.” The treatise places it under the figure of speech, passing from a term that stands for the nature to one that stands for an individual. The figure of speech is the fallacy closest to grammar, since its cause is the likeness between the forms of words.

@question 4.1
Question: “You bought raw meat yesterday; you eat what you bought; so you eat raw meat.” Which mode of figure of speech is this?
Choices:
- A word in one category seems to be in another
- A word of one gender seems to be of another
- A word for a kind seems to name an individual
- It is not a figure of speech
Answer: A word in one category seems to be in another
Why: “What you bought” names a quantity, and “raw” is a quality; the argument passes from one category to the other.
Why not the others: Nothing turns on gender or on kind and individual; a quantity is taken as a quality.

@question 4.2
Question: “Animal is a genus; a horse is an animal; so a horse is a genus.” Where does De fallaciis place this?
Choices:
- Under the figure of speech
- Under equivocation
- Under accident
- Under begging the question
Answer: Under the figure of speech
Why: It passes from a term for the nature to a term for an individual, which the treatise counts as figure of speech.
Why not the others: The word has one meaning; it seems to signify an individual when it signifies a kind.

@question 4.3
Question: Why is the figure of speech the fallacy closest to grammar?
Choices:
- Its cause is the likeness between the forms of words
- It concerns pronunciation
- It concerns the meaning of a whole phrase taken together
- It concerns the order of premises
Answer: Its cause is the likeness between the forms of words
Why: It arises because a word looks like others with a different way of signifying.
Why not the others: Pronunciation is accent, and a phrase’s meaning is amphiboly.

@card 5

**Why seven outside the words.** The fallacies outside the words deceive because things that agree in some way are taken as the same simply, or things that differ in some way are taken as different simply (ch. 11). *De fallaciis* derives seven from the general conditions of being. From what is *per se* and what is *per accidens* comes the fallacy of **accident**; from the perfect and the imperfect, **in a certain respect taken flatly**; from the opposed and the not opposed, **missing the point**.

From the same and the different comes **begging the question**; from the prior and the posterior, the **consequent**; from cause and effect, **treating what is not the cause as the cause**; and from the one and the many, **many questions as one**. So the list of seven is not a collection of observed mistakes but an order taken from the ways in which things can be one or many.

@question 5.1
Question: From which condition of being does the fallacy of accident come?
Choices:
- What is per se and what is per accidens
- Cause and effect
- The one and the many
- The prior and the posterior in a consequence
Answer: What is per se and what is per accidens
Why: Accident arises from taking what belongs per accidens as if per se.
Why not the others: Cause and effect give non-cause, the one and the many give many questions, and prior and posterior the consequent.

@question 5.2
Question: From which condition of being does many questions as one come?
Choices:
- The one and the many
- The same and the different
- The perfect and the imperfect
- The opposed and the not opposed
Answer: The one and the many
Why: Several questions are taken as one.
Why not the others: The same and the different give begging the question, the perfect and imperfect in a certain respect, and opposition missing the point.

@question 5.3
Question: What does the derivation of the seven show?
Choices:
- The list is an order taken from the conditions of being
- The list is a collection of observed mistakes
- There could be any number of such fallacies
- The seven are the same as the six fallacies found in the words
Answer: The list is an order taken from the conditions of being
Why: Each fallacy outside the words answers to one general condition of being.
Why not the others: The treatise derives the seven; it does not merely collect them.

@card 6

**Accident and consequent.** The two fallacies resemble each other, and the treatise explains the difference (ch. 16). Accident arises when a predicate is taken to be wholly the same as its subject: “Socrates is an animal, so Socrates is a man” assumes that whatever is an animal is a man, as if animal and man were the same. The consequent arises when a consequent is taken to be wholly the same as its antecedent, so that the consequence is thought to run both ways.

The consequent therefore always involves two consequences, one true and one false: “If someone runs, he moves” is true, but “If he moves, he runs” is false, and “Socrates moves, so Socrates runs” depends on the false one. So, the treatise says, when an argument proceeds from a categorical proposition, the fallacy is accident, and when it proceeds from a conditional, it is the consequent. Aristotle puts it briefly: accident is in one, the consequent in two.

@question 6.1
Question: “If it is a square, it has four sides; this has four sides; so it is a square.” Which fallacy is this?
Choices:
- The consequent
- Accident
- Begging the question
- Non-cause
Answer: The consequent
Why: It proceeds from a conditional as if the consequence ran both ways.
Why not the others: Accident proceeds from a categorical; this uses a conditional.

@question 6.2
Question: “Fido is a mammal, so Fido is a whale.” Which fallacy is this, according to De fallaciis?
Choices:
- Accident
- The consequent
- Composition
- Many questions
Answer: Accident
Why: It proceeds from a categorical, taking the predicate as wholly the same as the subject.
Why not the others: With no conditional, the treatise calls it accident, not the consequent.

@question 6.3
Question: What does Aristotle mean by “accident is in one, the consequent in two”?
Choices:
- The consequent involves two consequences, one true and one false
- Accident needs two premises and the consequent only one premise
- Accident is found in one figure only
- The consequent has two middle terms
Answer: The consequent involves two consequences, one true and one false
Why: The consequent depends on a true consequence and its false reverse.
Why not the others: The phrase concerns consequences, not premises, figures, or middle terms.

@card 7

**Five ways to beg the question.** To beg the question is to ask that the very thing to be proved be granted, under another name (ch. 15). If it were asked under the same name, the asker would only be laughed at; the fallacy deceives because the words are different. The treatise counts five modes. The thing defined is asked to prove the definition, or the reverse. A universal is asked to prove its particular, as when “all opposites are known by one science” is asked to prove that contraries are.

Or all the particulars are asked to prove the universal they make up; or the parts of a proposition are asked one by one to prove the whole, as when “medicine treats the healthy” and “medicine treats the sick” are asked to prove that it treats both; or one of two correlatives is asked to prove the other, as when “Plato is the son of Socrates” is asked in order to prove that Socrates is the father of Plato. In every mode the premise is no better known than the conclusion.

@question 7.1
Question: “Every human being is a person, since every man is a person.” Which fallacy is this, if “man” and “human being” mean the same?
Choices:
- Begging the question
- Accident
- Missing the point
- The consequent
Answer: Begging the question
Why: The conclusion is asked under another name, which is begging the question.
Why not the others: The premise is no better known than the conclusion.

@question 7.2
Question: Proving that all metals melt by first asking that iron, copper, and every other metal be granted to melt. Which mode of begging is this?
Choices:
- Asking all the particulars to prove the universal
- Asking the universal to prove a particular
- Asking the definition to prove the defined
- Asking one of two correlatives to prove the other
Answer: Asking all the particulars to prove the universal
Why: Asking each particular that makes up the universal begs the universal.
Why not the others: Here the particulars are asked in order to prove the universal.

@question 7.3
Question: What is common to all five modes?
Choices:
- The premise is no better known than the conclusion
- The premise is false
- The argument is invalid, since the conclusion does not follow
- The terms are equivocal
Answer: The premise is no better known than the conclusion
Why: Begging the question is valid but proves nothing, since the premise is no clearer than what it proves.
Why not the others: The argument may be valid with true premises.

@card 8

**Non-cause and many questions.** In *De fallaciis*, treating what is not the cause as the cause is a fault of argument, not of causal explanation (ch. 17). It occurs when among the premises one is placed that does nothing toward the conclusion, but seems to because it shares terms with the others. It deceives chiefly in an argument to the impossible, where an absurd conclusion is blamed on a premise that did not produce it, and so a true premise is rejected.

The modern “false cause,” which takes what merely goes with a thing for its cause, is a different fault. Many questions as one arises when two questions are put in one form, so that one answer is given to both (ch. 18): “Are Socrates and Plato running?” asked when only one of them runs. A proposition is one when one thing is said of one, or when the several things said make one by nature, as “rational mortal animal”; otherwise it is several, and so is the question.

@question 8.1
Question: In De fallaciis, where does the fallacy of non-cause chiefly deceive?
Choices:
- In an argument to the impossible
- In a direct syllogism in Barbara
- In an induction
- In a definition
Answer: In an argument to the impossible
Why: An absurd conclusion is blamed on a premise that did not produce it.
Why not the others: In a direct argument an idle premise leads to no absurdity.

@question 8.2
Question: “Have you and your brother paid the fine?” when only one has paid. Which fallacy is this?
Choices:
- Many questions as one
- Amphiboly
- Begging the question
- Accident
Answer: Many questions as one
Why: Two questions are put in one form, so that one answer is given to both.
Why not the others: The phrase is clear; the problem is that it asks two things at once.

@question 8.3
Question: When is a proposition one rather than several?
Choices:
- When one is said of one, or the several things said make one by nature
- When it has a single verb
- When it is short
- When it is affirmative
Answer: When one is said of one, or the several things said make one by nature
Why: “Rational mortal animal” makes one by nature; “white man” does not.
Why not the others: Grammar and length do not decide unity; the things said do.

@card 9

**Every fallacy is an ignorance of the refutation.** A refutation is a syllogism that concludes the contradictory of what the opponent holds. Since every fallacy fails either as a syllogism or as a contradiction, *De fallaciis* says that every fallacy is reduced to ignorance of the refutation as to its general principle (ch. 14). The other twelve each omit something that a true syllogism or a true contradiction requires.

Missing the point is also a fallacy of its own, when what is omitted concerns the contradiction itself: the conclusion seems to contradict the thesis, but not of the same thing, in the same respect, in relation to the same, or at the same time. “Two is double of one and not double of three, so two is double and not double” is the treatise’s kind of case. So the study of fallacies is the study of what a real refutation needs, approached from the side of its failures.

@question 9.1
Question: Why does De fallaciis reduce every fallacy to ignorance of the refutation?
Choices:
- Every fallacy fails as a syllogism or as a contradiction
- Every fallacy is about some ignorance on the part of the arguer
- Every fallacy has a false premise
- Every fallacy is in the words
Answer: Every fallacy fails as a syllogism or as a contradiction
Why: A refutation is a syllogism of contradiction, and each fallacy omits something it needs.
Why not the others: Some fallacies are outside the words, and some have true premises.

@question 9.2
Question: “This liquid is sweet to the healthy and bitter to the sick; so it is sweet and not sweet.” What has gone wrong?
Choices:
- The contradiction is not in the same respect
- The terms are equivocal
- The premise is begged
- The consequence is reversed
Answer: The contradiction is not in the same respect
Why: A real contradiction must deny the same thing, in the same respect, in relation to the same.
Why not the others: The fault is the missing condition of the contradiction, which is missing the point.

@question 9.3
Question: What does the study of fallacies study, on this account?
Choices:
- What a real refutation needs, from the side of its failures
- The tricks of debate for winning
- The history of mistaken opinions
- The psychology of persuasion
Answer: What a real refutation needs, from the side of its failures
Why: Each fallacy shows a condition of a true syllogism or contradiction by failing it.
Why not the others: The study is logical, not a collection of tricks, opinions, or motives.

@bank PRED_PRINCIPLES
Title: Pred Principles (samples)

@example 1

Question: What turns a genus into one of the species under it?

Choices:
- A difference (differentia) added to the genus
- By an accident that belongs to it alone
- By a property that follows from what it is
- By listing the individuals under it

Answer: A difference (differentia) added to the genus

Why: A species is the genus narrowed by the difference that constitutes it, as rational added to animal gives man.

@example 2

Question: What sets a PROPERTY (proprium) apart from an accident?

Choices:
- It follows from what the thing is, and belongs to the whole species, to it alone, and always
- The subject may have it or lack it and stay the same thing
- It answers the bare question “what is it?”
- It is the widest kind said of the subject

Answer: It follows from what the thing is, and belongs to the whole species, to it alone, and always

Why: A property (the power to laugh) always goes together with the species, even though it is not part of what the thing is; an accident (white) may come and go.

@example 3

Question: Is “animal” a genus or a species?

Choices:
- Both, since it is a genus in relation to man and a species in relation to living body; the terms are relative
- A genus only, never a species
- A species only, never a genus
- Neither: it is an individual

Answer: Both, since it is a genus in relation to man and a species in relation to living body; the terms are relative

Why: In the tree of Porphyry a middle term is a genus to whatever lies below it and a species to whatever lies above it; only the topmost is always a genus, only the lowest always a species.


@bank CAT_PRINCIPLES
Title: Cat Principles (samples)

@example 1

Question: On which category do all the others depend, since they exist only in it or by affecting it?

Choices:
- Substance
- Quality
- Quantity
- Relation

Answer: Substance

Why: Substance alone exists in its own right; the nine other categories are accidents, which exist only in a substance (Categories 5; Metaphysics VII).

@example 2

Question: How many are the categories, and who set them out?

Choices:
- Ten, set out by Aristotle in the Categories
- Five, set out by Porphyry in the Isagoge
- Four, as the causes are four
- Three, as the acts of the mind are three

Answer: Ten, set out by Aristotle in the Categories

Why: Aristotle’s Categories names ten: substance, quantity, quality, relation, action, passion, when, where, posture, and having.

@example 3

Question: Which of these does NOT signify a substance?

Choices:
- white
- man
- horse
- stone

Answer: white

Why: “White” signifies a quality present in a subject; man, horse, and stone signify substances existing in themselves.

@bank FAL_DOCTRINE
Title: Fal Doctrine (samples)

@example 1

Question: According to De fallaciis, the treatise attributed to St. Thomas, what does every fallacy have two of?

Choices:
- Two causes, one that makes it look sound and one that makes it fail
- Two premises, both of them false
- Two conclusions that contradict each other
- Two words that mean the same thing

Answer: Two causes, one that makes it look sound and one that makes it fail

Why: The cause of the appearance makes the argument look good; the cause of the failure breaks it. A man is deceived by the two together, since something appears to be so and is not.

@example 2

Question: “Sophistical” comes from a word meaning what?

Choices:
- Apparent wisdom, that is, wisdom that only looks like wisdom
- Careful reasoning
- A short argument
- Speech before a crowd

Answer: Apparent wisdom, that is, wisdom that only looks like wisdom

Why: De fallaciis, the treatise attributed to St. Thomas, says that “sophistical” means apparent wisdom (sophistica quasi apparens sapientia). The sophist argues for glory, wanting to seem wise.

@example 3

Question: According to De fallaciis, the treatise attributed to St. Thomas, a disputation is an act of one person toward another. For what purpose?

Choices:
- To show something proposed
- To display a form of argument as an example
- To pass the time agreeably
- To discover a new science

Answer: To show something proposed

Why: That last part marks a real disputation off from an argument given merely to illustrate a pattern, since a disputation aims at showing something.


@bank DIAL_DOCTRINE
Title: Dial Doctrine (samples)

@example 1

Question: Dialectic reasons from premises of what sort?

Choices:
- Probable ones, that is, what seems so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise
- Ones that have been demonstrated and cannot be otherwise
- Ones that are known to be false
- Ones invented for the sake of the argument

Answer: Probable ones, that is, what seems so to everyone, or to most, or to the wise

Why: This is the definition Aristotle gives (*Topics* I.1), and St. Thomas repeats it (commentary on the *Posterior Analytics*, bk. I, lect. 31). The probable is not a guess about odds; it is what a reasonable person may be asked to grant.

@example 2

Question: What does dialectic reach, at its best?

Choices:
- A reasoned opinion, well supported but not proved
- Certain knowledge of the cause
- A persuasive speech
- A story that makes sense of the facts

Answer: A reasoned opinion, well supported but not proved

Why: Demonstration reaches science; dialectic reaches opinion held for good reasons. Knowing which of the two we are doing keeps us honest about how much we have shown.

@example 3

Question: Logic divides into two arts. What are they?

Choices:
- The art of finding an argument, and the art of judging one
- The art of speaking, and the art of writing
- The art of defining, and the art of dividing
- The art of the true, and the art of the probable

Answer: The art of finding an argument, and the art of judging one

Why: The schools called them the science of finding (inventio) and the science of judging (judicium). The Prior and Posterior Analytics teach judging; the Topics teaches finding. This exercise is mostly about finding.


@bank SUPP_ITEMS
Title: Supp Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: **Horse** has five letters

Answer: Material, since it stands for the word itself

Why: No horse has letters; the term stands for the word “horse.”

@example 2

Question: **Pious** has two syllables

Answer: Material, since it stands for the word itself

Why: The term stands for the spoken word, which has two syllables.

@example 3

Question: I can find no rhyme for **orange**

Answer: Material, since it stands for the word itself

Why: What lacks a rhyme is the word, not the fruit, so the term stands for itself.


@bank COMP_ITEMS
Title: Comp Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: The sun had set and the lamps were lit

Answer: Copulative

@example 2

Question: Rome is in Italy and Madrid is in Spain

Answer: Copulative

@example 3

Question: The garden is weeded and the hedge is trimmed

Answer: Copulative


@bank CARG_ITEMS
Title: Carg Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: Every tyrant is unjust, since he rules for his own good and not for the common good; Nero was a tyrant; so Nero was unjust.

Answer: An epicheirema

@example 2

Question: Every virtue is praiseworthy, for it makes its possessor good; courage is a virtue; so courage is praiseworthy.

Answer: An epicheirema

@example 3

Question: Every metal conducts heat; copper is a metal, as its luster and hardness show; so copper conducts heat.

Answer: An epicheirema


@bank DIL_ITEMS
Title: Dil Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: Either you agree with me or you are a fool. If you agree, you must vote my way; if you are a fool, your vote should not count. So your vote must go my way or not count.

Answer: It fails: the disjunction is incomplete (escape between the horns)

Why: One may disagree without being a fool, so the disjunction is incomplete.

@example 2

Question: Every student either loves the subject or is forced to study it. If he loves it, rules are unnecessary; if he is forced, rules are useless. So rules are useless or unnecessary.

Answer: It fails: the disjunction is incomplete (escape between the horns)

Why: Some students are indifferent and may be moved by rules, so the disjunction leaves out a third case.

@example 3

Question: Every philosopher holds either that ideas are innate or that all knowledge is sensation. Either way he falls into error. So every philosopher errs.

Answer: It fails: the disjunction is incomplete (escape between the horns)

Why: The Aristotelian tradition holds neither view, so the disjunction is incomplete.


@bank DEMO_ITEMS
Title: Demo Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: Whatever is near does not twinkle; the planets are near; so the planets do not twinkle.

Answer: Of the reasoned fact, through the proper cause

Why: Nearness is the cause of not twinkling, so this shows why.

@example 2

Question: Whatever does not twinkle is near; the planets do not twinkle; so the planets are near.

Answer: Of the fact, through an effect

Why: Not twinkling is the effect of nearness, so this shows only that the planets are near.

@example 3

Question: Wherever there is smoke there is fire; there is smoke in the valley; so there is fire in the valley.

Answer: Of the fact, through an effect

Why: Smoke is an effect of fire, so the argument shows the fact, not the reason.


@bank PERSE_ITEMS
Title: Perse Items (samples)

@example 1

Question: A triangle is a figure

Answer: Per se in the first way: part of the definition

@example 2

Question: Man is an animal

Answer: Per se in the first way: part of the definition

@example 3

Question: A line is bounded by points

Answer: Per se in the first way: part of the definition


@bank DEMO_FAULTS
Title: Demo Faults (samples)

@example 1

Question: Every metal is liquid; iron is a metal; so iron is liquid.

Answer: A premise is false

@example 2

Question: Every bird can fly; the penguin is a bird; so the penguin can fly.

Answer: A premise is false

@example 3

Question: Most people who study logic reason well; Paul studies logic; so Paul reasons well.

Answer: A premise is only probable, not necessary
